with the new elements corresponding to the reflections mixing
\Delta
and
J

\begin{split}h\rightarrow 1-h\iff\Delta\rightarrow 1-J,\,\,\,J\rightarrow 1-\Delta,\\
\bar{h}\rightarrow 1-\bar{h}\iff\Delta\rightarrow 1+J,\,\,\,J\rightarrow\Delta-1,\end{split}

(8.2)

which are the light and dual light transforms. The shadow transform in a Lorentzian two-dimensional CFT is simply the product of these commuting elements, giving the interpretation of the light transforms as the ‘half-shadow transforms’.

With the extended little group as a guiding principle, we will now define explicit formulae for the light transforms. Before we begin, note that the discrete weights
s_{h}
and
s_{\bar{h}}
do not appear in the Casimir eigenvalues, and so representations with differing values of
s_{h}
and
s_{\bar{h}}
could in principle be equivalent. The only possible Weyl reflection would be to relate the even and odd
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
representations so that
s_{h}\rightarrow s_{h}+1
, however, it is a trivial fact that the even and odd representations of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
are not equivalent. Simply put, the lack of an intertwining operator for even/odd representations corresponds to the fact that we cannot anti-symmetrise a function which is already even. A consequence of this is that the light transforms do not change the weights
s_{h}
and
s_{\bar{h}}
.

The light transform in homogeneous coordinates is given by a projective integral over the spinor
\lam
. We define the light transformed celestial state as

\boxed{\begin{split}\mu,\ket{\mu, \lamt;1-h, \hb, s_h,s_{\hb}};1-h,\bar{h},s_{h},s_{\bar{h}}&=i^{-s_{h}}\frac{\Gamma(2-2h)}{\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-h+\frac{s_{h}}{2})\Gamma(h-\frac{s_{h}}{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\int_{\mathbb{RP}^{1}}\langle\lam d\lam\rangle\,\langle\absolutevalue{\agl{ \lam}{\mu}}\mu\rangle^{2h-2}\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\mu\rangle)^{s_{h}}\ket{\lam, \lamt;h, \hb, s_h,s_{\hb}},\lamt;h,\bar{h},s_{h},s_{\bar{h}}.\end{split}}

(8.3)

We will denote this light transform operation symbolically as
L
.
It can be easily checked that the new state is an
\slR\times\slR
conformal primary with shifted weights
1-h,\bar{h}
, while the discrete weights
s_{h},s_{\bar{h}}
are unchanged as expected. This formula for the light transform is adapted to the novel (2,2) chiral Mellin transform which builds states homogeneous under
\RR_{*}
scalings. Note, however, that since (
8.3
) leaves the discrete weights
s_{h},s_{\bar{h}}
invariant, its group theoretic behaviour is equivalent to the light transforms presented elsewhere in the literature for example
[
60
]
16
16

16

In
[
60
]
, a definition of the light transform was given which acts on states living in two patches of a celestial torus labelled by an incoming and outgoing parameter. This is not the same as the above definition but contains completely equivalent information, just not separated into even and odd parts.
.

The definition (
8.3
) features a factor of
i^{-s_{h}}
despite the fact that
s_{h}
is only defined mod 2. Nevertheless, the entire formula is invariant when we shift
s_{h}\rightarrow s_{h}+2
thanks to compensation from the gamma function factors
[\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-h+\frac{s_{h}}{2})\Gamma(h-\frac{s_{h}}{2}-\frac{1}{2})]^{-1}
. In fact, the normalisation in (
8.3
) is chosen such that
L^{2}=1
, as shown in Appendix
D
.

We can recast the light transform in a compact form by combining it with a half-Mellin transform which, as explained above equation (
7.22
), acts on a momentum eigenstate with (2,2) helicity
l
and
s_{l}

\begin{split}\mu,\ket{\mu, \lamt;1-h, s_h, l, s_l};1-h,s_{h},l,s_{l}&=i^{-s_{h}}\frac{\Gamma(2-2h)}{\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-h+\frac{s_{h}}{2})\Gamma(h-\frac{s_{h}}{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\int_{\mathbb{RP}^{1}}\langle\lam d\lam\rangle\,\langle\absolutevalue{\agl{ \lam}{\mu}}\mu\rangle^{2h-2}\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\mu\rangle)^{s_{h}}\\
&\qquad\times\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{*}}\!\frac{du}{\absolutevalue{u}}\,\absolutevalue{u}^{2h}\,\text{sgn}(u)^{s_{h}}|u\lam,\lamt;l,s_{l}\rangle\\
=&\frac{i^{-s_{h}}}{2}\frac{\Gamma(2-2h)}{\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-h+\frac{s_{h}}{2})\Gamma(h-\frac{s_{h}}{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}d^{2}\lam\,\langle\absolutevalue{\agl{ \lam}{\mu}}\mu\rangle^{2h-2}\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\mu\rangle)^{s_{h}}|\lam,\lamt;l,s_{l}\rangle,\end{split}

(8.4)

where we have used the fact that the measure
d^{2}\!\lam
breaks up into
\absolutevalue{u}\,du\,\langle\lam d\lam\rangle
. This measure is in fact the only thing responsible for the weight shift behaviour
h\rightarrow 1-h
of the light transform, and its independence of
\text{sgn}(u)
is the reason why
s_{h}
is unchanged.

The light transformed state is labelled with a pair spinors
\mu,\lamt
much like a twistor. Indeed under rescalings of both spinors
\mu,\lamt
we have the weight
-2(1-h+\bar{h})=-2(1-l)
which is the usual scaling of a twistor. Of course, unlike a twistor the celestial state (
8.3
) is homogeneous under rescalings of each spinor independently. As one might expect, the bulk conformal symmetry generators in the light transformed basis in Appendix
C
also have many similarities with those in twistor variables, for example all generators are first order differential operators.

The connection between light and twistor transforms was made concrete in
[
42
]
and we will spend some time refining this relation in the following Section
9
.

For completeness we record the formula for the dual light transformed state

\boxed{\begin{split}\ket{\lam, \mut;h, 1-\hb, s_h,s_{\hb}},\tilde{\mu};h,1-\bar{h},s_{h},s_{\bar{h}}&=i^{-s_{\bar{h}}}\frac{\Gamma(2-2\bar{h})}{\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-\bar{h}+\frac{s_{\bar{h}}}{2})\Gamma(\bar{h}-\frac{s_{\bar{h}}}{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\int_{\mathbb{RP}^{1}}[\lamt d\lamt]\,[\tilde{\mu}\absolutevalue{\sqr{\mut}{\lamt}}]^{2\bar{h}-2}\text{sgn}([\tilde{\mu}\lamt])^{s_{\bar{h}}}\ket{\lam, \lamt;h, \hb, s_h,s_{\hb}},\lamt;h,\bar{h},s_{h},s_{\bar{h}}.\end{split}}

(8.5)

We will also refer to this dual light transform operation as
\bar{L}
.

9
From Twistor to Light

The half-Fourier or twistor transform was introduced in the seminal paper
[
22
]
as a means to directly map amplitudes in (2,2) signature (analytically continued from (1,3) signature) directly to either twistor or dual twistor space in four dimensions. The transform to twistor space takes the form

\mu,\ket{\mu, \lamt}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\RR^{2}}d^{2}\lam\,e^{i\langle\lam\mu\rangle}\ket{\lam, \lamt},\lamt\,,

(9.1)

while the dual twistor transform takes the form

\ket{\lam, \mut},\tilde{\mu}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\RR^{2}}d^{2}\lamt\,e^{i[\tilde{\mu}\lamt]}\ket{\lam, \lamt},\lamt\,.

(9.2)

We will again define the symbolic shorthand
T
to denote the half-Fourier transform (
9.1
) and
\bar{T}
to denote its dual (
9.2
). The above half-Fourier transforms are both self inverse since, like the Fourier transform (
5.1
), they are themselves Fourier transforms with a skew-symmetric kernel.

The relationship between the twistor and the Fourier transform defined in (
5.1
) is that in the former we only transform half of the phase space. This is reminiscent of the relationship between light and shadow transforms. Furthermore we can see this relationship already at the level of the dilatation and helicity operators written in twistor variables

\begin{split}\cJ\sim\frac{1}{2}\mu^{\alpha}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{\alpha}}+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}}+1=:d_{T},\\
d\sim-\frac{1}{2}\mu^{\alpha}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{\alpha}}+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}}=:\cJ_{T},\end{split}

(9.3)

which, upon Mellin transforming with weights
k,\bar{k}
, give the light transform relations
k=1-h,\bar{k}=\bar{h}
. We have also defined the distinct operators
\cJ_{T},d_{T}
generating helicity and dilatation operations of the variables
\mu_{\alpha},\lamt_{\dot{\alpha}}
based purely on their transformation properties in either the left or right hand copy of
\slR\times\slR
. That is
\cJ_{T},d_{T}
are not just the operators
\cJ,d
in the twistor basis but are distinct and are related to
\cJ,d
by the naive replacement
\lam\rightarrow\mu
.

Similar considerations hold for the dual twistor basis

\begin{split}\cJ\sim-\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{\alpha}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda^{\alpha}}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}}}-1=:-d_{\bar{T}},\\
d\sim\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{\alpha}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda^{\alpha}}+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}}}=:-\cJ_{\bar{T}},\end{split}

(9.4)

which upon Mellin transforming give the dual light transform relations
k=h,\bar{k}=1-\bar{h}
. In Appendix
C
we give all the conformal symmetry generators
\cJ,d,p,k,m
in the twistor and dual twistor basis.
Just as for the original generators and the ‘Fourier’ ones
d_{F},\cJ_{F},\ldots
etc. we find that the relation between
T
and
\bar{T}
conformal generators is described by the same automorphism of the conformal algebra, but now generated by the full Fourier transform which maps from twistor space to dual twistor space.

Momentum Basis
Twistor Basis
Mellin Basis
Light Basis

\scriptstyle T

\scriptstyle C_{h,\bar{h}}

\scriptstyle C_{1-h,\bar{h}}

\scriptstyle L

Figure 3
:
We denote the chiral Mellin transform with weights
h,\bar{h},s_{h},s_{\bar{h}}
by
C_{h,\bar{h}}
, half-Fourier by
T
and light transform by
L
.

Momentum Basis
Dual Twistor Basis
Mellin Basis
Dual Light Basis

\scriptstyle\bar{T}

\scriptstyle C_{h,\bar{h}}

\scriptstyle C_{h,1-\bar{h}}

\scriptstyle\bar{L}

Figure 4
:
We denote the chiral Mellin transform with weights
h,\bar{h},s_{h},s_{\bar{h}}
by
C_{h,\bar{h}}
, dual half-Fourier by
\bar{T}
and dual light transform by
\bar{L}
.

The precise relationship between twistor and light transforms is via the commuting diagrams in Figures
3
and
4
which state that the (dual) light transformed conformal primary is the same as the chiral Mellin transformed (dual) twistor eigenstate. These diagrams were first established in
[
42
]
, but there the diagrams commuted with the proviso that twistor transformed states corresponded to a linear combination of light transforms with different ‘incoming/outgoing parameters’. One of the goals of this paper has been to make it clear that we do not need to introduce any incoming or outgoing parameters in split signature since there is no sense of the past or future. In Section
8
we made some crucial modifications to the light transforms to ensure they act properly on states which are homogeneous under
\RR_{*}
scalings. These modifications lead to an exact relation between twistor and light transforms according to Figure
3
, which we now prove. The proof for the dual light and dual twistor case is completely analogous.

Since the (2,2) chiral Mellin transform is given by two independent half-Mellin transforms, the transform on the unchanged spinor
\lamt
passes through as usual and so we just focus on half-Mellin transforming the
\mu
spinor

\begin{split}\mu,\ket{\mu, \lamt; k, s_k};k,s_{k}&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\RR_{*}}\frac{dt}{\absolutevalue{t}}\absolutevalue{t}^{2k}\text{sgn}(t)^{-s_{k}}t\mu,\ket{t\mu,\lamt}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\RR_{*}}\frac{dt}{\absolutevalue{t}}\absolutevalue{t}^{2k}\text{sgn}(t)^{-s_{k}}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\RR^{2}}d^{2}\lam\,e^{it\langle\lam\mu\rangle}\ket{\lam, \lamt},\lamt\\
&=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\RR^{2}}d^{2}\lam\,\mathcal{I}\ket{\lam, \lamt},\lamt.\end{split}

(9.5)

We now perform the half-Mellin integral

\mathcal{I}:=\int_{\RR_{*}}\frac{dt}{\absolutevalue{t}}\absolutevalue{t}^{2k}\text{sgn}(t)^{-s_{k}}e^{it\langle\lam\mu\rangle}=\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,t^{2k-1}\left(e^{it\langle\lam\mu\rangle}+(-1)^{-s_{k}}e^{-it\langle\lam\mu\rangle}\right),

(9.6)

and changing variables
t\rightarrow t\langle\absolutevalue{\agl{\lam}{\mu}}\mu\rangle^{-1}
we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}=&\langle\absolutevalue{\agl{\lam}{\mu}}\mu\rangle^{-2k}\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\mu\rangle)^{-s_{k}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,t^{2k-1}\left(e^{it}+(-1)^{-s_{k}}e^{-it}\right)\\
=&\begin{cases}\langle\absolutevalue{\agl{\lam}{\mu}}\mu\rangle^{-2k}2\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,t^{2k-1}\!\cos(t)&\text{for $s_{k}=0$},\\
\langle\absolutevalue{\agl{\lam}{\mu}}\mu\rangle^{-2k}\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\mu\rangle)\,2i\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,t^{2k-1}\!\sin(t)&\text{for $s_{k}=1$}.\end{cases}\end{split}

(9.7)

Now as usual we need to regularise the weights, so we shift
k\in\frac{1}{2}+i\RR
17
17

17

Since
\Delta\in 1+i\RR
and
J
is pure imaginary the weights
h,\bar{h}\in\frac{1}{2}+i\RR
and similarly
k,\bar{k}\in\frac{1}{2}+i\RR
.
by a small negative parameter
-\delta
such that
-1<\text{Re}(2(k-\delta)-1)<0
. Then sending
\delta\rightarrow 0_{+}
we recover

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}=&\begin{cases}2\,\Gamma(2k)\,\sin(k\pi+\frac{\pi}{2})\langle\absolutevalue{\agl{\lam}{\mu}}\mu\rangle^{-2k}&\text{for $s_{k}=0$},\\
2i\,\Gamma(2k)\,\sin(k\pi)\,\langle\absolutevalue{\agl{\lam}{\mu}}\mu\rangle^{-2k}\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\mu\rangle)&\text{for $s_{k}=1$},\end{cases}\\
=&2\,i^{s_{k}}\Gamma(2k)\sin\bigg(\bigg(k-\frac{s_k}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\bigg)\pi\bigg)\langle\absolutevalue{\agl{\lam}{\mu}}\mu\rangle^{-2k}\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\mu\rangle)^{-s_{k}}.\end{split}

(9.8)

Now we use the Euler reflection formula for gamma functions to yield

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}=&2\pi\,i^{s_{k}}\frac{\Gamma(2k)}{\Gamma(k-\frac{s_{k}}{2}+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-k+\frac{s_{k}}{2})}\langle\absolutevalue{\agl{\lam}{\mu}}\mu\rangle^{-2k}\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\mu\rangle)^{-s_{k}}.\end{split}

(9.9)

Finally we set
k=1-h
and
s_{k}=-s_{h}
(equivalently
s_{k}=s_{h}
) and conclude that from a chiral Mellin transform of a Fourier conjugate state we recover the light transformed state (
8.4
)

\mu,\ket{\mu, \lamt; 1-h, s_h};1-h,s_{h}=\frac{i^{-s_{h}}}{2}\frac{\Gamma(2-2h)}{\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-h+\frac{s_{h}}{2})\Gamma(h-\frac{s_{h}}{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\int_{\RR^{2}}d^{2}\lam\,\langle\absolutevalue{\agl{\lam}{\mu}}\mu\rangle^{2h-2}\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\mu\rangle)^{s_{h}}\ket{\lam, \lamt},\lamt.

(9.10)

Alternatively, we can check the commuting diagram in Figure
3
by inverse Mellin transforming (
8.4
) to recover a half-Fourier transform. That is we compute,

\begin{split}\int_{a-i\infty}^{a+i\infty}\frac{dh}{2\pi i}\sum_{s_{h}=0,1}\frac{i^{-s_{h}}}{2}\frac{\Gamma(2-2h)}{\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-h+\frac{s_{h}}{2})\Gamma(h-\frac{s_{h}}{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\int_{\RR^{2}}d^{2}\lam\,\langle\absolutevalue{\agl{\lam}{\mu}}\mu\rangle^{2h-2}\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\mu\rangle)^{s_{h}}\ket{\lam, \lamt},\lamt,\end{split}

(9.11)

which gives the two terms

\begin{split}=\int_{\RR^{2}}d^{2}\lam\int_{a-i\infty}^{a+i\infty}\frac{dh}{2\pi i}\frac{1}{2\pi}\Gamma(2-2h)\bigg(\sin(h\pi-\frac{\pi}{2})+i\sin(h\pi)\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\mu\rangle)\bigg)\langle\absolutevalue{\agl{\lam}{\mu}}\mu\rangle^{2h-2}\ket{\lam, \lamt},\lamt,\end{split}

(9.12)

which we evaluate the same way by chosing
a<1
and closing the contour to pick up the poles of the gamma function

\begin{split}=&\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\RR^{2}}d^{2}\lam\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!}\bigg(\sin(\pi\frac{n+1}{2})+i\sin(\pi\frac{n+2}{2})\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\mu\rangle)\bigg)\langle\absolutevalue{\agl{\lam}{\mu}}\mu\rangle^{n}\ket{\lam, \lamt},\lamt\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\RR^{2}}d^{2}\lam\bigg(\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{m}}{(2m)!}\langle\absolutevalue{\agl{\lam}{\mu}}\mu\rangle^{2m}+i\,\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\mu\rangle)\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{m}}{(2m+1)!}\langle\absolutevalue{\agl{\lam}{\mu}}\mu\rangle^{2m+1}\bigg)\ket{\lam, \lamt},\lamt\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\RR^{2}}d^{2}\lam\bigg(\cos(\abs{\agl{\lam}{\mu}})+i\,\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\mu\rangle)\sin(\abs{\agl{\lam}{\mu}})\bigg)\ket{\lam, \lamt},\lamt\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\RR^{2}}d^{2}\lam e^{i\langle\lam\mu\rangle}\ket{\lam, \lamt},\lamt.\end{split}

(9.13)

Hence we have recovered a half-Fourier transform.

10
Celestial Twistor Amplitudes

In this section we will show how to compute celestial twistor amplitudes in Yang-Mills and gravity, building on earlier work and the results and formalism developed above. In what follows, we espouse the viewpoint that the most direct way to produce light transformed conformal correlators is to perform a Mellin transform of a twistor amplitude and so traverse the commuting diagrams in Figures
3
and
4
clockwise. Light transformed correlators have the nice property that they no longer have singular support coming from the momentum conserving delta function in the bulk
- see
[
42
,
66
,
63
,
68
,
69
]
for discussions. These recent works have used the methodology of traversing the commuting diagrams counterclockwise, that is Mellin transforming first in momentum space and then light transforming the resultant celestial amplitude. The recent paper
[
63
]
in particular derived the correlators of two holomorphic and two anti-holomorphic light-ray operators, from light transforms of the four gluon celestial amplitude in
(2,2)
signature spacetime. The results were written in terms of integrals of products of Gaussian hypergeometric functions, which when evaluated led to certain special functions which arise in Mellin-Barnes integrals. In our derivation of four-point celestial twistor amplitudes below we find somewhat analogous, albeit simpler, interim integrals.

So far we have only studied asymptotic scattering states and classified the different bases one can take for these. Since an amplitude is a multi-particle object one can ask the important question: what is the most useful prescription for assigning bases to represent each particle leg? In principle, we can use any of the momentum space, twistor, dual twistor or Fourier bases for each leg and we can choose this for each leg independently. In the work of
[
61
]
the choice made was to uniformly associate to each leg a dual twistor. This led to many compact expressions for twistor amplitudes, including
n
-particle MHV,
\overline{\text{MHV}}
and up to eight particle
\text{N}^{2}\text{MHV}
amplitudes in
N=4
SYM, as well as MHV,
\overline{\text{MHV}}
expressions in
N=8
supergravity. The authors also developed a BCFW recursion relation in dual twistor space which resulted in a simple BCFW shift of the dual twistor. We leave the task of Mellin transforming amplitudes from dual twistor space to future work and instead we focus on twistor amplitudes in the ambidextrous bases of
[
24
]
.

As demonstrated in
[
24
]
, performing ambidextrous transforms to twistor space leads to remarkably simple objects - for example, for pure Yang-Mills the three and four-point ambidextrous twistor amplitudes take the constant value equal to one or minus one depending on the particular external kinematics probed. Note that ‘ambidextrously’ here means that we can transform either
\lambda
or
\tilde{\lambda}
for each leg independently and in general the basis chosen will not be uniform. At low particle multiplicity we will use a twistor for a plus helicity leg and a dual twistor for a minus helicity leg.
This choice leads to particularly simple integrals which decouple into products of
sgn
functions.

In the end we are interested in physical
(1,3)
signature scattering amplitudes. The motivation to consider
(2,2)
signature comes from the fact that we can analytically continue expressions for scattering amplitudes in
(1,3)
signature into
(2,2)
signature and consider new representations of those amplitudes in terms of twistors. This is the technique used in
[
61
]
and
[
24
]
to build seed amplitudes to be used in twistor BCFW to build higher point amplitudes and we also follow this methodology. Such analytically continued
(2,2)
signature amplitudes
do not
transform in the expected representations of the
(2,2)
little group. Rather, since they originated in
(1,3)
signature, they are homogeneous under a little group transformation with
half-integer helicity
.

Assuming we start with analytically continued (2,2) signature amplitudes with half-integer helicity (denoted simply as
l
since
s_{l}=2l\mod 2
), we can map to Mellin space in the most practical manner by performing just a half-Mellin transform over the scale of either of the spinors. This method was introduced used in
[
42
]
and is particularly useful when applied to twistor amplitudes. In contrast to
[
42
]
, we ambidextrously perform half-Mellin transforms over the scales of the
\mu,\tilde{\mu}
spinor instead of performing them on the original spinors
\lam,\lamt
. Just as was the case in momentum space, the two choices are equivalent, since under a change of variable given by an inversion
t\mapsto\frac{1}{\bar{u}}
the half-Mellin transformed twistor eigenstate becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\mu,\lamt;k,s_{k},l\rangle:=&\frac{1}{2}\int_{\RR_{*}}\frac{dt}{\absolutevalue{t}}\absolutevalue{t}^{2k}\text{sgn}(t)^{s_{k}}|t\mu,\lamt;l\rangle\\
=&\frac{1}{2}\int_{\RR_{*}}\frac{d\bar{u}}{\absolutevalue{\ub}}\absolutevalue{\ub}^{-2k}\text{sgn}(\bar{u})^{s_{k}}|\bar{u}^{-1}\mu,\lamt;l\rangle\\
=&\frac{1}{2}\int_{\RR_{*}}\frac{d\bar{u}}{\absolutevalue{\ub}}\absolutevalue{\ub}^{-2k}\text{sgn}(\bar{u})^{s_{k}}\absolutevalue{\ub}^{-2(l-1)}\text{sgn}(\bar{u})^{-2(l-1)}|\mu,\bar{u}\lamt;l\rangle\\
=&\frac{1}{2}\int_{\RR_{*}}\frac{d\bar{u}}{\absolutevalue{\ub}}\absolutevalue{\ub}^{2\bar{h}}\text{sgn}(\bar{u})^{s_{\bar{h}}}|\mu,\bar{u}\lamt;l\rangle\,,\\
\end{split}

(10.1)

which is exactly the half-Mellin transform over the
\tilde{\lambda}
spinor, where we have used that
k=1-h
and
h-\bar{h}=l
. A similar equivalence is true for the half-Mellin transformed dual twistor eigenstate.

10.1
Pure Yang-Mills

We start off by deriving the three-point and four-point celestial twistor amplitudes of pure Yang-Mills Theory.

10.1.1
Three Points: Mellin Transform of a Sgn Function

Three-Point

MHV

When we analytically continue to
(2,2)
signature we can write down a three-point MHV amplitude with real external kinematics

\displaystyle A^{--+}_{3}=\frac{\langle 12\rangle^{3}}{\langle 23\rangle\langle 31\rangle}\,\delta^{4}(\lambda^{\alpha}_{1}\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}_{1}+\lambda^{\alpha}_{2}\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}_{2}+\lambda^{\alpha}_{3}\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}_{3})\,.

(10.2)

As in
[
24
]
we perform the twistor or half-Fourier transforms ambidextrously. In our conventions this corresponds to

\displaystyle\tilde{A}^{--+}_{3}:=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int d^{2}\tilde{\lambda}_{1}\,d^{2}\tilde{\lambda}_{2}\,d^{2}\lambda_{3}\,e^{i[\tilde{\mu}_{1}\tilde{\lambda}_{1}]}\,e^{i[\tilde{\mu}_{2}\tilde{\lambda}_{2}]}\,e^{i\langle\lambda_{3}\mu_{3}\rangle}A^{--+}_{3}\,.

(10.3)

To perform the half-Fourier transforms we must regulate the poles appearing from
\langle 23\rangle\langle 31\rangle
in (
10.2
). Following
[
24
]
, we regulate these and subsequent poles with a principle value prescription, which is chosen exactly to preserve the little group properties of the amplitude. With this choice the three-point MHV twistor amplitude is given by

\displaystyle\tilde{A}^{--+}_{3}:=\frac{(\pi i)^{3}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\rangle)\,\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{1}\mu_{3}\rangle+[\tilde{\lambda}_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{1}])\,\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle+[\tilde{\lambda}_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{2}])\,.

(10.4)

In order to map to Mellin space we use ambidextrous half-Mellin transforms over the scales of the spinors
\tilde{\mu}_{1},\tilde{\mu}_{2},\mu_{3}
.

Symmetrised Amplitudes

As explained in Section
7.3
we can separately perform a Mellin transform over positive re-scalings of the spinors and then build odd/even states via linear combination of sums and differences over the parameters
\bar{\epsilon}_{1},\bar{\epsilon}_{2},\epsilon_{3}\in\{1,-1\}
associated with flipping the signs of the spinors
\tilde{\mu}_{1},\tilde{\mu}_{2},\mu_{3}
. We now perform the sums first and then integrate to introduce some of the key points. At four points there are many such linear combinations, so we will perform the reverse method. That is, we integrate over positive rescalings first to derive a compact expression in (
10.27
), from which we can easily (anti-)symmetrise the amplitudes.

For the three-point amplitude there are 8 possible combinations of even/odd states under the flipping of the signs of
\tilde{\mu}_{1},\tilde{\mu}_{2},\mu_{3}
. Following our formalism, we denote these states as
\tilde{A}^{--+}_{3}\{s_{\bar{k}_{1}},s_{\bar{k}_{2}},s_{k_{3}}\}
for
s_{\bar{k}_{1}},s_{\bar{k}_{2}},s_{k_{3}}\in\{0,1\}
. Note that, from the form of the three-point MHV twistor amplitude (
10.4
), when we flip the sign of all the spinors
\tilde{\mu}_{1},\tilde{\mu}_{2},\mu_{3}
the amplitude remains invariant

\displaystyle\tilde{A}^{--+}_{3}(-\tilde{\mu}_{1},-\tilde{\mu}_{2},-\mu_{3})=\tilde{A}^{--+}_{3}(\tilde{\mu}_{1},\tilde{\mu}_{2},\mu_{3})\,.

(10.5)

The symmetrised amplitude

\displaystyle\tilde{A}^{--+}_{3}\{s_{\bar{k}_{1}},s_{\bar{k}_{2}},s_{k_{3}}\}=\frac{1}{2^{3}}\sum_{\bar{\epsilon}_{1},\bar{\epsilon}_{2},\epsilon_{3}}\bar{\epsilon}_{1}^{s_{\bar{k}_{1}}}\bar{\epsilon}_{2}^{s_{\bar{k}_{2}}}\epsilon_{3}^{s_{k_{3}}}\tilde{A}^{--+}_{3}(\bar{\epsilon}_{1}\tilde{\mu}_{1},\bar{\epsilon}_{2}\tilde{\mu}_{2},\epsilon_{3}\mu_{3})\,,

(10.6)

contains pairs related by (
10.5
) which can sum together or subtract to give zero depending on whether
(-1)^{s_{\bar{k}_{1}}+s_{\bar{k}_{2}}+s_{k_{3}}}
equals plus or minus one. This immediately implies if
s_{\bar{k}_{1}}+s_{\bar{k}_{2}}+s_{k_{3}}=1\mod 2
then the amplitude vanishes, which can be checked explicitly. We hence have the following four non-vanishing amplitudes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{A}^{--+}_{3}\{0,0,0\}\,,\,\,\,\tilde{A}^{--+}_{3}\{1,1,0\}\,,\,\,\,\tilde{A}^{--+}_{3}\{1,0,1\}\,,\,\,\,\tilde{A}^{--+}_{3}\{0,1,1\}\,,\end{split}

(10.7)

where using (
10.6
) we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{A}^{--+}_{3}\{s_{\bar{k}_{1}},s_{\bar{k}_{2}},s_{k_{3}}\}=\frac{2(\pi i)^{3}}{2^{3}(2\pi)^{3}}\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\rangle)\,&\bigg(\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{1}\mu_{3}\rangle+[\tilde{\lambda}_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{1}])\,\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle+[\tilde{\lambda}_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{2}])\\
&+(-1)^{s_{\bar{k}_{1}}}\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{1}\mu_{3}\rangle-[\tilde{\lambda}_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{1}])\,\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle+[\tilde{\lambda}_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{2}])\\
&+(-1)^{s_{\bar{k}_{2}}}\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{1}\mu_{3}\rangle+[\tilde{\lambda}_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{1}])\,\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle-[\tilde{\lambda}_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{2}])\\
&+(-1)^{s_{k_{3}}}\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{1}\mu_{3}\rangle-[\tilde{\lambda}_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{1}])\,\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle-[\tilde{\lambda}_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{2}])\bigg)\,,\end{split}

(10.8)

From the amplitudes (
10.7
) given by (
10.8
) we can recover the original amplitude by summing them. These amplitudes can be re-cast as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\tilde{A}^{--+}_{3}\{s_{\bar{k}_{1}},s_{\bar{k}_{2}},s_{k_{3}}\}=\frac{(\pi i)^{3}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\rangle\langle\lambda_{1}\mu_{3}\rangle\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle)\,\tilde{\delta}\bigg(\sum_{i}s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}}\bigg)\,\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times G_{s_{\bar{k}_{2}}+s_{k_{3}}}(\theta_{31}^{-1})G_{s_{\bar{k}_{1}}+s_{k_{3}}}(\theta_{32}^{-1})\,,\end{split}

(10.9)

where we have included a Kronecker delta

\tilde{\delta}\bigg(\sum_{i}s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}}\bigg)\coloneqq\delta_{s_{\bar{k}_{1}}+s_{\bar{k}_{2}}+s_{k_{3}},0}\,,

(10.10)

(with argument evaluated mod 2) which enforces the aforementioned condition for the amplitude to be non-vanishing.
We have also defined the variables

\displaystyle\theta_{ij}=\frac{\langle\lambda_{j}\mu_{i}\rangle}{[\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\tilde{\mu}_{j}]}\,,

(10.11)

and the (anti-)symmetrised
sgn
functions

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{0}(x):=\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\text{sgn}(1+x)+\text{sgn}(1-x)\bigg)\,,\\
G_{1}(x):=\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\text{sgn}(1+x)-\text{sgn}(1-x)\bigg)\,,\end{split}

(10.12)

which can be written in terms of Heaviside step functions as follows

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{0}(x)=&\Theta(1-\absolutevalue{x}),\\
G_{1}(x)=&\text{sgn}(x)\Theta(\absolutevalue{x}-1).\end{split}

(10.13)

We will use the above definitions (
10.13
) many times over when performing our integrals.

The (2,2) signature celestial twistor amplitude in Mellin space, denoted with a calligraphic symbol
\mathcal{A}
, is then given by an integral over
t_{i}
the positive scale of
\mu_{i}
with weight
2k_{i}

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{--+}_{3}\{s_{\bar{k}_{1}},s_{\bar{k}_{2}},s_{k_{3}}\}:=&\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\bar{t}_{1}}{\bar{t}_{1}}\,\frac{d\bar{t}_{2}}{\bar{t}_{2}}\,\frac{dt_{3}}{t_{3}}\,\bar{t}_{1}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}\,\bar{t}_{2}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}\,t_{3}^{2k_{3}}\tilde{A}^{--+}_{3}\{s_{\bar{k}_{1}},s_{\bar{k}_{2}},s_{k_{3}}\}(t_{1}\tilde{\mu}_{1},\bar{t}_{2}\tilde{\mu}_{2},t_{3}\mu_{3}).\end{split}

(10.14)

As such we are lead to consider the following integrals

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{I}(\pm,\pm):=&\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\bar{t}_{1}}{\bar{t}_{1}}\,\frac{d\bar{t}_{2}}{\bar{t}_{2}}\,\frac{dt_{3}}{t_{3}}\,\bar{t}_{1}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}\,\bar{t}_{2}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}\,t_{3}^{2k_{3}}\\
&\quad\times\frac{1}{4}\bigg(\text{sgn}(1+\frac{\bar{t}_{1}}{t_{3}}\theta_{31}^{-1})\pm\text{sgn}(1-\frac{\bar{t}_{1}}{t_{3}}\theta_{31}^{-1})\bigg)\bigg(\text{sgn}(1+\frac{\bar{t}_{2}}{t_{3}}\theta_{32}^{-1})\pm\text{sgn}(1-\frac{\bar{t}_{2}}{t_{3}}\theta_{32}^{-1})\bigg).\end{split}

(10.15)

The general character of
\mathcal{I}(\pm,\pm)
(and corresponding integrals beyond three points) is very simple - it is a Mellin integral (over a positive quadrant) of an integrand which is a piece-wise
constant function
in various regions bounded by the planes
\frac{\bar{t}_{i}}{t_{3}}=\absolutevalue{\theta_{3i}}
.

Another important characteristic of the integral
\mathcal{I}
, and its higher point cousins, is that it is a conformal integral and as such one of the degrees of freedom serves simply to parameterise an overall scale over which we integrate. In addition, the integrand itself is invariant under overall dilatations since it is a pure gluon amplitude. We can fix this scaling redundancy by integrating it out. We choose to write
\bar{t}_{1}=t_{3}x_{1}
and
\bar{t}_{2}=t_{3}x_{2}
and then integrate over the overall scale
t_{3}

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{I}(\pm,\pm)=&\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt_{3}}{t_{3}}t_{3}^{2\bar{k}_{1}+2\bar{k}_{2}+2k_{3}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dx_{1}\,dx_{2}}{x_{1}\,x_{2}}\,x_{1}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}\,x_{2}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}\,\\
&\quad\times\frac{1}{4}\bigg(\text{sgn}(1+x_{1}\theta_{31}^{-1})\pm\text{sgn}(1-x_{1}\theta_{31}^{-1})\bigg)\bigg(\text{sgn}(1+x_{2}\theta_{32}^{-1})\pm\text{sgn}(1-x_{2}\theta_{32}^{-1})\bigg)\\
=&\;2\pi i\,\delta\bigg(\sum_{i}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\bigg)\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dx_{1}\,dx_{2}}{x_{1}\,x_{2}}\,x_{1}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}\,x_{2}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}\\
&\quad\times\frac{1}{4}\bigg(\text{sgn}(1+x_{1}\theta_{31}^{-1})\pm\text{sgn}(1-x_{1}\theta_{31}^{-1})\bigg)\bigg(\text{sgn}(1+x_{2}\theta_{32}^{-1})\pm\text{sgn}(1-x_{2}\theta_{32}^{-1})\bigg),\end{split}

(10.16)

where we have recovered a dilatation invariance delta function using equation (
7.19
) and the fact that the weights
\bar{k}_{1}
,
\bar{k}_{2}
,
k_{3}
are all pure imaginary. This is a special feature of the ambidextrous twistor gluon amplitude and relies on the fact we have analytically continued from (1,3) signature, so all the helicities are plus or minus one.

Now that we have integrated out an overall scale the remaining integrals over
x_{1},x_{2}
are separable and each has manifestly acquired a scale given by
\theta_{31},\theta_{32}
respectively. This separable behaviour persists at four points.

From the three-point MHV celestial amplitude we are led to consider the following Mellin transforms of the (anti-)symmetrised
sgn
functions
G_{0},G_{1}
in (
10.13
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\,x_{1}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}G_{s}(x_{1}\theta_{31}^{-1})=&\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\,x_{1}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}\,\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\text{sgn}(1+x_{1}\theta_{31}^{-1})+(-1)^{s}\text{sgn}(1-x_{1}\theta_{31}^{-1})\bigg)\\
=&\;\text{sgn}(\theta_{31})^{s}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\,x_{1}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}\Theta((-1)^{s}(1-x_{1}\absolutevalue{\theta_{31}}^{-1})).\end{split}

(10.17)

An important property of the function
G_{0}
is that it is zero for large values
\absolutevalue{x_1}>\theta_{31}
and so its Mellin transform has a semi-infinite strip of definition where the integral is defined for
2k_{1}
with real part greater than zero. On the other hand, the function
G_{1}
is zero for small values
\absolutevalue{x_1}<\theta_{31}
and so its semi-infinite strip of definition is where
2k_{1}
has real part less than zero. Note that the original
sgn
function, since it is
\mathcal{O}(1)
everywhere, has a Mellin transform which only marginally converges when its weight lies on the imaginary axis and diverges otherwise. As such we can only re-organise the integral (
10.17
) by integrating each
sgn
function separately if we also give a regularisation prescription. We demonstrate a natural regularisation in the next section in equation (
10.21
), which also commutes with the even/odd projection in (
10.17
).

Continuing with our derivation, we compute the Mellin transform of
G_{0}
and
G_{1}
functions - since all the weights
k_{i}
are pure imaginary we must regularise the weights to ensure they are within the corresponding strip of definition

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\,x_{1}^{2\bar{k}_{1}+\epsilon}\,\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\text{sgn}(1+x_{1}\theta_{31}^{-1})+\text{sgn}(1-x_{1}\theta_{31}^{-1})\bigg)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\,x_{1}^{2\bar{k}_{1}+\epsilon}\,\Theta\bigg(1-x_{1}\absolutevalue{\theta_{31}}^{-1}\bigg)\\
=&\;\Theta(\theta_{31})\frac{\theta_{31}^{2\bar{k}_{1}+\epsilon}}{2\bar{k}_{1}+\epsilon}+\Theta(-\theta_{31})\frac{(-\theta_{31})^{2\bar{k}_{1}+\epsilon}}{2\bar{k}_{1}+\epsilon}=\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{31}}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}}{2\bar{k}_{1}+\epsilon},\\
&\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\,x_{1}^{2\bar{k}_{1}-\epsilon}\,\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\text{sgn}(1+x_{1}\theta_{31}^{-1})-\text{sgn}(1-x_{1}\theta_{31}^{-1})\bigg)=\text{sgn}(\theta_{31})\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\,x_{1}^{2\bar{k}_{1}-\epsilon}\,\Theta\bigg(x_{1}\absolutevalue{\theta_{31}}^{-1}-1\bigg)\\
=&-\Theta(\theta_{31})\frac{\theta_{31}^{2\bar{k}_{1}-\epsilon}}{2\bar{k}_{1}-\epsilon}+\Theta(-\theta_{31})\frac{(-\theta_{31})^{2\bar{k}_{1}-\epsilon}}{2\bar{k}_{1}-\epsilon}=\text{sgn}(-\theta_{31})\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{31}}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}}{2\bar{k}_{1}-\epsilon}.\end{split}

(10.18)

So the even case gives an ‘advanced’ prescription for the pole in weight space and the odd case has the ‘retarded’ prescription. Also the even/odd symmetry is trivially carried by an overall
sgn
function and the remaining integral simply depends on a manifestly positive scale at
\absolutevalue{\theta_{31}}
. Finally, we have removed the appearance of
\epsilon
in the exponents since the only role it should play is to regularise the pole.

The three-point MHV celestial twistor amplitudes are then given by a compact formula

\boxed{\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{--+}_{3}\{s_{\bar{k}_{1}},s_{\bar{k}_{2}},s_{k_{3}}\}=&\frac{\pi}{4}\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\rangle\langle\lambda_{1}\mu_{3}\rangle\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle)\,\delta\bigg(\sum_{i}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\bigg)\tilde{\delta}\bigg(\sum_{i}s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}}\bigg)\\
&\quad\times\text{sgn}(-\theta_{31})^{s_{\bar{k}_{1}}}\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{31}}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}}{2\bar{k}_{1}+(-1)^{s_{\bar{k}_{1}}}\epsilon}\text{sgn}(-\theta_{32})^{s_{\bar{k}_{2}}}\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}}{2\bar{k}_{2}+(-1)^{s_{\bar{k}_{2}}}\epsilon}.\end{split}}

(10.19)

The formula (
10.19
) has similarities with light transformed conformal correlators that have appeared in the literature in
[
42
,
63
]
, up to differences due to the newly defined light transform (
8.3
) with absolute values and sgn functions and the specific normalisation we have chosen. We give a formula for the unsymmetrised celestial twistor amplitude in (
10.27
) which is closer to the light transformed conformal correlators of
[
42
]
.

It is also a simple task to check conformal covariance of (
10.19
) for each leg. Note first that we are explicitly using only the ambidextrous variables, in this case
\bar{k}
for negative helicity legs and
k
for the positive helicity legs. In general, for conformal covariance we require that if we sum the exponents wherever a positive helicity leg
i
appears in the
\theta_{ik}
ratios we have overall weight
-2k_{i}
, while for a negative helicity leg
j
we have overall weight
2\bar{k}_{j}
. Then conformal covariance is guaranteed using the defining relations
k:=1-h
and
\bar{k}:=1-\bar{h}
and the relations
\bar{k}_{1,2}=-h_{1,2}
and
k_{3}=-\bar{h}_{3}
specific to the ambidextrous variables and pure gluon amplitudes. Finally, for some legs we must also use dilatation invariance, for example for leg 3 we use the condition
\bar{k}_{1}+\bar{k}_{2}+k_{3}=0
.

In the derivation of (
10.19
) we first performed the sum over incoming and outgoing parameters in order to define even and odd parity states and afterwards we performed the integrals over positive energies. In general, performing the integrals first and then the sum afterwards will give the same result only if the integrals are finite. That is, the Mellin transform of a sum of two functions is equal to the sum of their Mellin transforms only if there is an overlap in their strip of definition. We saw already that the symmetrised and anti-symmetrised
sgn
functions had a strip of definition in the right and left half planes respectively. However, the
sgn
function itself has a Mellin transform which only marginally converges when the weight lies on the imaginary axis and diverges otherwise. We show in the below section that we can define a natural regularisation prescription to define the Mellin transform of a
sgn
function and that this regularisation commutes with the (anti-)symmetrisation procedure.

Unsymmetrised Amplitude

We now look at the case where we first integrate (
10.4
) over positive re-scalings and then perform the even/odd projections afterwards. This will of course give the same result as in the previous section as long as we are careful in how we regularise marginally convergent Mellin transforms. We hence consider the integral

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{3pt}=&2\pi i\,\delta\bigg(\sum_{i}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\bigg)\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dx_{1}\,dx_{2}}{x_{1}\,x_{2}}\,x_{1}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}\,x_{2}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}\text{sgn}(1+x_{1}\theta_{31}^{-1})\text{sgn}(1+x_{2}\theta_{32}^{-1}),\end{split}

(10.20)

which is the unsymmetrised version of equation (
10.16
).

Again the integrals over
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
separate and are of identical form after the replacement
1\mapsto 2
. Focusing on the
x_{1}
integral, since the
sgn
function is always
\mathcal{O}(1)
its strip of definition is when
2\bar{k}_{1}
is pure imaginary and we must regulate in a similar manner to the integral (
7.20
) for the delta function. Instead of regulating with a factor of
e^{-\epsilon\absolutevalue{y}}
, we use a regularisation prescription which is anchored at the scale set by
\theta_{31}
. That is we define
\absolutevalue{\abs{y}}:=\text{sgn}(y-\ln(\abs{\theta_{31}}))y
which equals
y
when
y>\ln(\abs{\theta_{31}})
and
-y
when
y<\ln(\abs{\theta_{31}})
, then define the regulated integral

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\,x_{1}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}\,\text{sgn}(1+x_{1}\theta_{31}^{-1})=&\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dy\,e^{2\bar{k}_{1}y-\epsilon\absolutevalue{\abs{y}}}\,\text{sgn}(1+e^{y}\theta_{31}^{-1})\\
=&\;\Theta(-\theta_{31})\left(\int_{-\infty}^{\ln(-\theta_{31})}dy\,e^{2\bar{k}_{1}y+\epsilon y}-\int_{\ln(-\theta_{31})}^{\infty}dy\,e^{2\bar{k}_{1}y-\epsilon y}\right)\\
&\;\;+\;\Theta(\theta_{31})\left(\int_{-\infty}^{\ln\theta_{31}}dy\,e^{2\bar{k}_{1}y+\epsilon y}+\int_{\ln\theta_{31}}^{\infty}dy\,e^{2\bar{k}_{1}y-\epsilon y}\right)\\
=&\;\Theta(-\theta_{31})\bigg(\frac{(-\theta_{31})^{2\bar{k}_{1}+\epsilon}}{2\bar{k}_{1}+\epsilon}+\frac{(-\theta_{31})^{2\bar{k}_{1}-\epsilon}}{2\bar{k}_{1}-\epsilon}\bigg)+\Theta(\theta_{31})\bigg(\frac{\theta_{31}^{2\bar{k}_{1}+\epsilon}}{2\bar{k}_{1}+\epsilon}-\frac{\theta_{31}^{2\bar{k}_{1}-\epsilon}}{2\bar{k}_{1}-\epsilon}\bigg)\\
=&\;\absolutevalue{\theta_{31}}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}\bigg(\frac{1}{2\bar{k}_{1}+\epsilon}+\text{sgn}(-\theta_{31})\frac{1}{2\bar{k}_{1}-\epsilon}\bigg),\end{split}

(10.21)

where again we remove the
\epsilon
in the exponents since it plays no regularising role there.

Note that in the case
\theta_{31}>0
the
sgn
gives minus one and so the Mellin transform (
10.21
) is proportional to a delta function as expected. While the case
\theta_{31}<0
the
sgn
function gives plus one and the Mellin transform (
10.21
) is proportional to the principal value pole. These are defined as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta(2\bar{k}_{1})\coloneqq\frac{1}{2\pi i}\bigg(\frac{1}{2\bar{k}_{1}+\epsilon}-\frac{1}{2\bar{k}_{1}-\epsilon}\bigg)\,,\\
P.V.\,\frac{1}{2\bar{k}_{1}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\frac{1}{2\bar{k}_{1}+\epsilon}+\frac{1}{2\bar{k}_{1}-\epsilon}\bigg)\,.\end{split}

(10.22)

So we may write

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\,x_{1}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}\,\text{sgn}(1+x_{1}\theta_{31}^{-1})=\absolutevalue{\theta_{31}}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}\bigg(\pi i\,\Theta(\theta_{31})\delta(\bar{k}_{1})+\Theta(-\theta_{31})\frac{1}{\bar{k}_{1}}\bigg).\end{split}

(10.23)

where it is understood that principal values are always implicit for a
1/\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}
pole.

Thus the above result (
10.21
) is the Mellin transform of a sgn function depending on a single parameter. This corresponds to the decomposition of the sgn function into even and odd parts as

\displaystyle\text{sgn}(1+\theta_{31}^{-1})=\Theta(1-\absolutevalue{\theta_{31}}^{-1})+\text{sgn}(\theta_{31})\Theta(\absolutevalue{\theta_{31}}^{-1}-1).

(10.24)

While it is not in general true that the Mellin transform of a sum is the sum of their Mellin transforms (the strips of definition must also be compatible such that all the integrals are convergent) the regularisation we have used is precisely such that this is true and is motivated as such. In other words, given the Mellin transform of a
sgn
function (
10.21
) it is simple to check that if we take even and odd combinations we recover the results (
10.18
).

To completely confirm our results we perform the inverse Mellin transform

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{d(2\bar{k}_{1})}{2\pi i}\absolutevalue{\theta_{31}}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}\bigg(\frac{1}{2\bar{k}_{1}+\epsilon}+\text{sgn}(-\theta_{31})\frac{1}{2\bar{k}_{1}-\epsilon}\bigg),\end{split}

(10.25)

where we have chosen a contour located at
a=0
corresponding to a strip of definition on the imaginary axis. Now the
\epsilon
-prescription in each term gives poles in the
2\bar{k}_{1}
plane to either the left or right of the imaginary axis - in addition, we must condition this integral on the magnitude of
\theta_{31}
to determine how to close the contour. The case
\absolutevalue{\theta_{31}}>1
requires us to close the contour as an anti-clockwise semicircle to the left such that the real part of
2\bar{k}_{1}
is less than zero and so will only pick up a
2\pi i
residue contribution from the first term, while for
\absolutevalue{\theta_{31}}<1
we must close the contour clockwise to the right and we get a contribution from the second term and an extra minus sign. Hence we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{d(2\bar{k}_{1})}{2\pi i}\bigg(\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{31}}^{2\bar{k}_{1}+\epsilon}}{2\bar{k}_{1}+\epsilon}+\text{sgn}(-\theta_{31})\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{31}}^{2\bar{k}_{1}-\epsilon}}{2\bar{k}_{1}-\epsilon}\bigg)=&\;\Theta(\absolutevalue{\theta_{31}}-1)-\text{sgn}(-\theta_{31})\Theta(1-\absolutevalue{\theta_{31}})\\
=&\;\text{sgn}(1+\theta_{31}^{-1}).\end{split}

(10.26)

Thus we see that the
\epsilon
prescription is crucial for the inverse Mellin transform and corresponds to a particular contour choice for each term done independently. With this specific contour choice in mind we may drop the
\epsilon
prescription in (
10.21
) which corresponds to dropping delta function terms in (
10.23
) which have singular support. The unsymmetrised amplitude then takes a very simple form

\boxed{\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{--+}_{3}=&\frac{\pi}{4}\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\rangle\langle\lambda_{1}\mu_{3}\rangle\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle)\,\delta\bigg(\sum_{i}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\bigg)\Theta(-\theta_{31})\Theta(-\theta_{32})\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{31}}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}}{\bar{k}_{1}}\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}}{\bar{k}_{2}},\end{split}}

(10.27)

from which we may (anti-)symmetrise to give the amplitudes (
10.19
) on the celestial torus. The above properties of Mellin transforms of sgn functions and their regularisation will be used again at four points.

Three-Point

\overline{MHV}

Here we summarise the results of the similar derivation of the three-point
\overline{MHV}
amplitude.
The spacetime amplitude

\displaystyle A^{++-}_{3}=\frac{[12]^{3}}{[23][31]}\,\delta^{4}(\lambda^{\alpha}_{1}\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}_{1}+\lambda^{\alpha}_{2}\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}_{2}+\lambda^{\alpha}_{3}\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}_{3})

(10.28)

is mapped to an ambidextrous twistor amplitude given by the following half-Fourier transforms

\displaystyle\tilde{A}^{++-}_{3}:=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int d^{2}\lambda_{1}\,d^{2}\lambda_{2}\,d^{2}\tilde{\lambda}_{3}\,e^{i\langle\lambda_{1}\mu_{1}\rangle}\,e^{i\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{2}\rangle}\,e^{i[\tilde{\mu}_{3}\tilde{\lambda}_{3}]}A^{++-}_{3},

(10.29)

which gives the result

\displaystyle\tilde{A}^{++-}_{3}=\frac{(\pi i)^{3}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\text{sgn}([\tilde{\lambda}_{1}\tilde{\lambda}_{2}])\,\text{sgn}(\langle\mu_{1}\lambda_{3}\rangle+[\tilde{\mu}_{3}\tilde{\lambda}_{1}])\,\text{sgn}(\langle\mu_{2}\lambda_{3}\rangle+[\tilde{\mu}_{3}\tilde{\lambda}_{2}]).

(10.30)

The (2,2) signature three-point
\overline{MHV}
celestial twistor amplitudes follow from an analogous derivation to that in the section above, and are given by

\boxed{\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{++-}_{3}\{s_{k_{1}},s_{k_{2}},s_{\bar{k}_{3}}\}=&\frac{\pi}{4}\text{sgn}([\tilde{\lambda}_{1}\tilde{\lambda}_{2}][\tilde{\mu}_{3}\tilde{\lambda}_{1}][\tilde{\mu}_{3}\tilde{\lambda}_{2}])\,\delta\bigg(\sum_{i}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\bigg)\tilde{\delta}\bigg(\sum_{i}s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}}\bigg)\\
&\quad\times\text{sgn}(-\theta_{13})^{s_{k_{1}}}\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{13}^{-1}}^{2k_{1}}}{2k_{1}+(-1)^{s_{k_{1}}}\epsilon}\text{sgn}(-\theta_{23})^{s_{k_{2}}}\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{23}^{-1}}^{2k_{2}}}{2k_{2}+(-1)^{s_{k_{2}}}\epsilon}\,,\end{split}}

(10.31)

where now the sums present in the delta functions are

\sum_{i}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}=2k_{1}+2k_{2}+2\bar{k}_{3},\quad\sum_{i}s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}}=s_{k_{1}}+s_{k_{2}}+s_{\bar{k}_{3}}\,.

(10.32)

The unsymmetrised amplitude from which the amplitudes are built is, dropping delta function terms as before,

\boxed{\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{++-}_{3}=\frac{\pi}{4}\text{sgn}([\tilde{\lambda}_{1}\tilde{\lambda}_{2}][\tilde{\mu}_{3}\tilde{\lambda}_{1}][\tilde{\mu}_{3}\tilde{\lambda}_{2}])\,\delta\bigg(\sum_{i}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\bigg)\Theta(-\theta_{13})\Theta(-\theta_{23})\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{13}^{-1}}^{2k_{1}}}{k_{1}}\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{23}^{-1}}^{2k_{2}}}{k_{2}}\,.\end{split}}

(10.33)

10.1.2
Four Points: Mellin Transform of a Product of Sgn Functions

Four-Point ‘Alternating’ Amplitude

We now move on to consider four points. We will discover that much of the same structure appears and identical methods can be used. At four points the
MHV
and
\overline{MHV}
amplitudes are one and the same, however we can consider different ways in which we assign helicity to each leg. We have
A_{4}^{+-+-}
which we call the ‘alternating’ helicity amplitude and
A_{4}^{++--}
which we call the ‘separated’ helicity amplitude. Following
[
24
]
we use the same ambidextrous twistor space for both of these amplitudes, in which case we find that both take their simplest, although qualitatively different form. We will first consider the alternating helicity amplitude and map it to celestial twistor space directly since the integrals are more straightforward. Once the dust has settled, we will be able to write the separated amplitude in terms of the alternating one. The alternating four-point amplitude is given by

\displaystyle A^{+-+-}_{4}=\frac{\langle 24\rangle^{4}}{\langle 12\rangle\langle 23\rangle\langle 34\rangle\langle 41\rangle}\,\delta^{4}(\lambda^{\alpha}_{1}\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}_{1}+\lambda^{\alpha}_{2}\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}_{2}+\lambda^{\alpha}_{3}\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}_{3}+\lambda^{\alpha}_{4}\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}_{4})\,.

(10.34)

Once again, we map to twistor space in an ambidextrous manner by performing the following half-Fourier transforms

\displaystyle\tilde{A}^{+-+-}_{4}:=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{4}}\int d^{2}\lambda_{1}\,d^{2}\tilde{\lambda}_{2}\,d^{2}\lambda_{3}\,d^{2}\tilde{\lambda}_{4}\,e^{i\langle\lambda_{1}\mu_{1}\rangle}\,e^{i[\tilde{\mu}_{2}\tilde{\lambda}_{2}]}\,e^{i\langle\lambda_{3}\mu_{3}\rangle}\,e^{i[\tilde{\mu}_{4}\tilde{\lambda}_{4}]}A^{+-+-}_{4}\,.

(10.35)

After regulating poles using a principal value prescription as before, the resulting amplitude takes the following form
[
24
]

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{A}^{+-+-}_{4}=&\;\frac{(\pi i)^{4}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{1}\rangle+[\tilde{\lambda}_{1}\tilde{\mu}_{2}])\,\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{1}\rangle+[\tilde{\lambda}_{1}\tilde{\mu}_{4}])\\
&\qquad\quad\times\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle+[\tilde{\lambda}_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{2}])\,\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{3}\rangle+[\tilde{\lambda}_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{4}])\\
=&\;\frac{1}{16}\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{1}\rangle\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{1}\rangle\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{3}\rangle)\\
&\qquad\quad\times\text{sgn}(1+\theta_{12}^{-1})\,\text{sgn}(1+\theta_{14}^{-1})\text{sgn}(1+\theta_{32}^{-1})\,\text{sgn}(1+\theta_{34}^{-1})\,.\end{split}

(10.36)

Symmetrised Amplitudes

We are now tasked with mapping this amplitude to Mellin space via ambidextrous Mellin transforms. At four points we sum over the incoming/outgoing parameters and there are 16 combinations of parity under flipping of the sign of the spinors
\mu_{1},\tilde{\mu}_{2},\mu_{3},\tilde{\mu}_{4}
. So we define

\displaystyle\tilde{A}^{+-+-}_{4}\{s_{k_{1}},s_{\bar{k}_{2}},s_{k_{3}},s_{\bar{k}_{4}}\}=\frac{1}{2^{4}}\sum_{\epsilon_{1},\bar{\epsilon}_{2},\epsilon_{3},\bar{\epsilon}_{4}}\epsilon_{1}^{s_{k_{1}}}\bar{\epsilon}_{2}^{s_{\bar{k}_{2}}}\epsilon_{3}^{s_{k_{3}}}\bar{\epsilon}_{4}^{s_{\bar{k}_{4}}}\tilde{A}^{+-+-}_{4}(\epsilon_{1}\mu_{1},\bar{\epsilon}_{2}\tilde{\mu}_{2},\epsilon_{3}\mu_{3},\bar{\epsilon}_{4}\tilde{\mu}_{4})\,,

(10.37)

where the
\{s_{k_{1}},s_{\bar{k}_{2}},s_{k_{3}},s_{\bar{k}_{4}}\}
label the parity for each spinor. As was the case at three points, if we flip the sign of all the spinors
\mu_{1},\tilde{\mu}_{2},\mu_{3},\tilde{\mu}_{4}\mapsto-\mu_{1},-\tilde{\mu}_{2},-\mu_{3},-\tilde{\mu}_{4}
then the amplitude (
10.36
) is invariant thus for the amplitude (
10.37
) to be non-zero we must have the even condition
s_{k_{1}}+s_{\bar{k}_{2}}+s_{k_{3}}+s_{\bar{k}_{4}}=0\mod 2
. There are then eight non-vanishing amplitudes, which by direct calculation are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\tilde{A}^{+-+-}_{4}\{0,0,0,0\}=\frac{1}{16}\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{1}\rangle\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{1}\rangle\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{3}\rangle)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\times\bigg(G_{0}(\theta_{12})G_{0}(\theta_{14})G_{0}(\theta_{32})G_{0}(\theta_{34})+G_{1}(\theta_{12})G_{1}(\theta_{14})G_{1}(\theta_{32})G_{1}(\theta_{34})\bigg),\\
&\tilde{A}^{+-+-}_{4}\{1,1,0,0\}=\frac{1}{16}\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{1}\rangle\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{1}\rangle\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{3}\rangle)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\times\bigg(G_{1}(\theta_{12})G_{0}(\theta_{14})G_{0}(\theta_{32})G_{0}(\theta_{34})+G_{0}(\theta_{12})G_{1}(\theta_{14})G_{1}(\theta_{32})G_{1}(\theta_{34})\bigg),\\
&\qquad\qquad\vdots\\
&\tilde{A}^{+-+-}_{4}\{1,1,1,1\}=\frac{1}{16}\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{1}\rangle\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{1}\rangle\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{3}\rangle)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\times\bigg(G_{1}(\theta_{12})G_{0}(\theta_{14})G_{0}(\theta_{32})G_{1}(\theta_{34})+G_{0}(\theta_{12})G_{1}(\theta_{14})G_{1}(\theta_{32})G_{0}(\theta_{34})\bigg),\end{split}

(10.38)

where we use the expressions (
10.13
) for the
G_{0}
and
G_{1}
functions. The general formula for the symmetrised amplitude is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{A}^{+-+-}_{4}\{s_{k_{1}},s_{\bar{k}_{2}},s_{k_{3}},s_{\bar{k}_{4}}\}=&\frac{1}{16}\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{1}\rangle\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{1}\rangle\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{3}\rangle)\delta\bigg(\sum_{i}s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}}\bigg)\\
&\quad\quad\times\sum_{r_{ij}}G_{r_{12}}(\theta_{12})G_{r_{14}}(\theta_{14})G_{r_{32}}(\theta_{32})G_{r_{34}}(\theta_{34}),\end{split}

(10.39)

where the sum in (
10.39
) gives the two unique terms with
r_{ij}\in{0,1}
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&r_{12}+r_{14}=s_{k_{1}}\mod 2\,,\\
&r_{12}+r_{32}=s_{\bar{k}_{2}}\mod 2\,,\\
&r_{32}+r_{34}=s_{k_{3}}\mod 2\,,\\
&r_{34}+r_{14}=s_{\bar{k}_{4}}\mod 2\,.\end{split}

(10.40)

For example, if we want an amplitude that is odd in
\mu_{1}
(
s_{k_{1}}{=}1
) then we require a product of an odd number of the anti-symmetric
G_{1}
functions evaluated at the variables
\theta_{1i}
, which contain
\mu_{1}
.

One can derive the various symmetrised four-point celestial twistor gluon amplitudes by Mellin transforming (
10.39
), exactly as was done at three points. We are led to consider the integrals

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{I}(r_{12},r_{14},r_{32},r_{34})&:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt_{1}\,d\bar{t}_{2}\,dt_{3}\,d\bar{t}_{4}}{t_{1}\bar{t}_{2}t_{3}\bar{t}_{4}}\,t_{1}^{2k_{1}}\,\bar{t}_{2}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}\,t_{3}^{2k_{3}}\,\bar{t}_{4}^{2\bar{k}_{4}}\\
&\qquad\qquad\times G_{r_{12}}\bigg(\frac{\bar{t}_{2}}{t_{1}}\theta^{-1}_{12}\bigg)G_{r_{14}}\bigg(\frac{\bar{t}_{4}}{t_{1}}\theta^{-1}_{14}\bigg)G_{r_{32}}\bigg(\frac{\bar{t}_{2}}{t_{3}}\theta^{-1}_{32}\bigg)G_{r_{34}}\bigg(\frac{\bar{t}_{4}}{t_{3}}\theta^{-1}_{34}\bigg).\end{split}

(10.41)

These integrals have a similar character to the integrals we met in the three-point case. They are conformal Mellin integrals over a positive quadrant in four dimensions, whose integrands are piece-wise constant functions in regions bounded by hyperplanes through the origin, for example
t_{1}=\bar{t}_{2}\absolutevalue{\theta^{-1}_{12}}
.

Rather than continue with the derivation of the symmetrised amplitudes, we will instead derive the
unsymmetrised
amplitude by Mellin transforming (
10.36
). With this, we can find the symmetrised celestial twistor amplitudes through simple (anti-)symmetrisation.

Unsymmetrised Amplitude

We now calculate the unsymmetrised four-point celestial twistor amplitude in its entirety, extending the three-point results (
10.27
) and (
10.33
). At four points we are led to consider Mellin transforms of a product of
sgn
functions. Once again, we must regularise the integrals such that the Mellin inverse exists and the regularisation commutes with taking even and odd linear combinations.

Performing ambidextrous Mellin transforms on the un-symmetrised amplitude (
10.36
) leads to the integral

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{4pt}&:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt_{1}\,d\bar{t}_{2}\,dt_{3}\,d\bar{t}_{4}}{t_{1}\bar{t}_{2}t_{3}\bar{t}_{4}}\,t_{1}^{2k_{1}}\,\bar{t}_{2}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}\,t_{3}^{2k_{3}}\,\bar{t}_{4}^{2\bar{k}_{4}}\\
&\qquad\qquad\times\text{sgn}\bigg(1+\frac{\bar{t}_{2}}{t_{1}}\theta^{-1}_{12}\bigg)\text{sgn}\bigg(1+\frac{\bar{t}_{2}}{t_{3}}\theta^{-1}_{32}\bigg)\text{sgn}\bigg(1+\frac{\bar{t}_{4}}{t_{1}}\theta^{-1}_{14}\bigg)\text{sgn}\bigg(1+\frac{\bar{t}_{4}}{t_{3}}\theta^{-1}_{34}\bigg).\end{split}

(10.42)

As usual, we can integrate out an overall scale which we choose to be
t_{1}
. The resulting integral has a corresponding dilatation invariance delta function and now manifestly depends on the scales
\absolutevalue{\theta_{ij}}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{4pt}&=2\pi i\,\delta\bigg(\sum_{i}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\bigg)\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dx\,dy\,dz}{xyz}\,x^{2\bar{k}_{2}}y^{2k_{3}}z^{2\bar{k}_{4}}\,\text{sgn}\bigg(1+x\,\theta^{-1}_{12}\bigg)\\
&\qquad\qquad\times\text{sgn}\bigg(1+\frac{x}{y}\theta^{-1}_{32}\bigg)\text{sgn}\bigg(1+z\,\theta^{-1}_{14}\bigg)\text{sgn}\bigg(1+\frac{z}{y}\theta^{-1}_{34}\bigg),\\
&=2\pi i\,\delta\bigg(\sum_{i}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\bigg)\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dy}{y}\,y^{2k_{3}}\,\cJ_{2}(y)\cJ_{4}(y),\end{split}

(10.43)

where we have the Mellin integrals

\displaystyle\begin{split}\cJ_{2}(y):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dx}{x}\,x^{2\bar{k}_{2}}\,\text{sgn}\bigg(1+x\,\theta^{-1}_{12}\bigg)\text{sgn}\bigg(1+\frac{x}{y}\theta^{-1}_{32}\bigg)\\
=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}ds\,e^{2\bar{k}_{2}s-\epsilon\absolutevalue{\abs{s}}}\,\text{sgn}\bigg(1+e^{s}\,\theta^{-1}_{12}\bigg)\text{sgn}\bigg(1+\frac{e^{s}}{y}\theta^{-1}_{32}\bigg),\end{split}

(10.44)

and similarly for
\cJ_{4}(y)
. As in the three-point case two of the integrals separate, and here the third integration over
y
entwines them. In equation (
10.44
) above we also define a specific regularisation which takes into account the two parameters of the integral

\displaystyle\begin{split}\absolutevalue{\abs{s}}:=\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\text{sgn}\bigg(s-\ln(\abs{\theta_{12}})\bigg)+\text{sgn}\bigg(s-\ln(\abs{\theta_{32}})\bigg)\bigg)\,s,\end{split}

(10.45)

such that when
s
is greater than both
\ln(\abs{\theta_{12}})
and
\ln(\abs{\theta_{32}})
we have
\absolutevalue{\abs{s}}=s
, when
s
lies between them we have
\absolutevalue{\abs{s}}=0
, and when
s
is less than both we have
\absolutevalue{\abs{s}}=-s
.

To calculate (
10.44
) we must condition on the relative magnitude of
\absolutevalue{\frac{\theta_{32}}{\theta_{12}}}
. Choosing
y=0
for convenience since it can be reinstated at the end, we compute
\cJ_{2}(0)
to be

\displaystyle\begin{split}\cJ_{2}(0)&:=\Theta\bigg(1-\absolutevalue{\frac{\theta_{32}}{\theta_{12}}}\bigg)\bigg[\frac{(\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}})^{2\bar{k}_{2}}}{2\bar{k}_{2}+\epsilon}+\text{sgn}(\theta_{32})\bigg(\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}}{2\bar{k}_{2}}-\frac{(\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}})^{2\bar{k}_{2}}}{2\bar{k}_{2}}\bigg)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\text{sgn}(\theta_{12}\theta_{32})\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}}{2\bar{k}_{2}-\epsilon}\bigg]\\
&+\Theta\bigg(\absolutevalue{\frac{\theta_{32}}{\theta_{12}}}-1\bigg)\bigg[\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}}{2\bar{k}_{2}+\epsilon}+\text{sgn}(\theta_{12})\bigg(\frac{(\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}})^{2\bar{k}_{2}}}{2\bar{k}_{2}}-\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}}{2\bar{k}_{2}}\bigg)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\text{sgn}(\theta_{12}\theta_{32})\frac{(\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}})^{2\bar{k}_{2}}}{2\bar{k}_{2}-\epsilon}\bigg],\end{split}

(10.46)

where we have also dropped the regulariser
\epsilon
when it appears in the exponents as usual.

Just as for the Mellin transform of a single
sgn
function, when
\theta_{12},\theta_{32}>0
we recover a contribution proportional to a delta function, while if
\theta_{12},\theta_{32}<0
we recover the principal value pole
while the other mixed cases give linear combinations of the two.

To confirm our results we now perform the inverse Mellin transform on (
10.46
). In the result (
10.46
) we have poles with various
\epsilon
-prescriptions - advanced, retarded and principal value - where poles written
\frac{1}{2\bar{k}_{2}}
are principal valued (
10.22
).
These
\epsilon
-prescriptions are crucial for performing the inverse Mellin transform. When we integrate
2\bar{k}_{2}
along the imaginary axis we will close the contour to form a large semi-circle and pick up various possible contributions from each of these regulated poles. The direction in which we close the contour will vary term by term and is dictated by the condition that the integral dies off on the semicircular arc; this depends on the the magnitudes of
\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}
and
\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}}
. For example, for terms involving
\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}
and for
\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}>1
we close to the left (negative real part) and we get a non-zero contribution if this term also has a
+\epsilon
prescription. For terms involving
\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}}
and for
\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}}<1
we close to the right (positive real part) and we get a non-zero contribution if this term also has a
-\epsilon
prescription. The principal value terms require some care. We pick up contributions regardless of which way we close the contour. However, the principal valued poles are multiplied by a difference of terms and the residues from the two terms cancel if we close the contour in the same direction for both terms and their residues sum if we close the contour in opposite directions for each term.

In summary, if we perform an inverse Mellin transform on (
10.46
) we have six cases for the magnitudes of
\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}},\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}}
. Each case picks up a contribution from exactly one of the terms in (
10.46
):

1.

\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}>\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}}>1
gives
\Theta\bigg(1-\absolutevalue{\frac{\theta_{32}}{\theta_{12}}}\bigg)\,\Theta(\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}-1)\,\Theta(\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}}-1)

2.

\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}>1>\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}}
gives
\Theta\bigg(1-\absolutevalue{\frac{\theta_{32}}{\theta_{12}}}\bigg)\,\Theta(\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}-1)\,\Theta(1-\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}})\,\text{sgn}(\theta_{32})

3.

1>\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}>\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}}
gives
\Theta\bigg(1-\absolutevalue{\frac{\theta_{32}}{\theta_{12}}}\bigg)\,\Theta(1-\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}})\,\Theta(1-\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}})\,\text{sgn}(\theta_{12}\theta_{32})

4.

\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}}>\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}>1
gives
\Theta\bigg(\absolutevalue{\frac{\theta_{32}}{\theta_{12}}}-1\bigg)\,\Theta(\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}-1)\,\Theta(\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}}-1)

5.

\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}}>1>\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}
gives
\Theta\bigg(\absolutevalue{\frac{\theta_{32}}{\theta_{12}}}-1\bigg)\,\Theta(1-\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}})\,\Theta(\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}}-1)\,\text{sgn}(\theta_{12})

6.

1>\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}}>\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}
gives
\Theta\bigg(\absolutevalue{\frac{\theta_{32}}{\theta_{12}}}-1\bigg)\,\Theta(1-\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}})\,\Theta(1-\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}})\,\text{sgn}(\theta_{12}\theta_{32})

The sum of the above contributions gives the required result - that is we recover the original function
\text{sgn}(1+\theta_{12}^{-1})\,\text{sgn}(1+\theta_{32}^{-1})
.

Once again we can discard the
\epsilon
prescriptions, and hence delta function terms, with the proviso that there exists a series of contour deformations of our inverse Mellin transform such that the Mellin inversion theorem holds. Then we can find a simpler formula for the
\cJ_{2}(y)
function, which is (reinstating
y
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\cJ_{2}(y)&=2\Theta(-\theta_{12})\Theta(\theta_{32})\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}}{2\bar{k}_{2}}+2\,\Theta(\theta_{12})\Theta(-\theta_{32})\frac{(y\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}})^{2\bar{k}_{2}}}{2\bar{k}_{2}}\\
&+2\,\Theta(-\theta_{12})\Theta(-\theta_{32})\,\text{sgn}\bigg(1-y\absolutevalue{\frac{\theta_{32}}{\theta_{12}}}\bigg)\bigg[\frac{(y\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}})^{2\bar{k}_{2}}}{2\bar{k}_{2}}-\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}}{2\bar{k}_{2}}\bigg].\end{split}

(10.47)

Using this result we can then find the full un-symmetrised integral
\mathcal{I}_{4pt}
from which the unsymmetrised amplitude is simply

\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{4}^{+-+-}=\frac{1}{16}\text{sgn}(\langle\mu_{1}\lambda_{2}\rangle\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle\langle\mu_{3}\lambda_{4}\rangle\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{1}\rangle)\mathcal{I}_{4pt}

(10.48)

We compute
\mathcal{I}_{4pt}
by plugging in the function (
10.47
) into integral (
10.43
). The resulting integral over
y
consists of a collection of products of sgn functions and so is of the exact same form as the
\mathcal{J}
integrals we just calculated. Hence we just iterate the method.

With the usual
\epsilon
regularisation, which we then drop, we find the unsymmetrised celestial twistor amplitude can be written compactly as

\boxed{\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{4}^{+-+-}=&\frac{\pi i}{16}\text{sgn}(\langle\mu_{1}\lambda_{2}\rangle\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle\langle\mu_{3}\lambda_{4}\rangle\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{1}\rangle)\,\delta\bigg(\sum_{i}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\bigg)\;|\theta_{12}|^{2\bar{k}_{2}}|\theta_{14}|^{2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4}}|\theta_{34}|^{-2k_{3}}\\
&\times\bigg[\,\Theta(-\theta_{12})\Theta(-\theta_{32})\Theta(-\theta_{14})\Theta(-\theta_{34})\,\text{sgn}(1-|\theta|)\Big(V_{12}(\theta)+V_{34}(\theta)-V_{32}(\theta)-V_{14}(\theta)\Big)\\
&-\Theta(-\theta_{12})\Theta(-\theta_{32})\Theta(\theta_{34})\Theta(-\theta_{14})\;V_{34}(\theta)-\Theta(\theta_{12})\Theta(-\theta_{32})\Theta(-\theta_{14})\Theta(-\theta_{34})\;V_{12}(\theta)\\
&-\Theta(-\theta_{12})\Theta(\theta_{32})\Theta(-\theta_{34})\Theta(-\theta_{14})\;V_{32}(\theta)-\Theta(-\theta_{12})\Theta(-\theta_{32})\Theta(-\theta_{34})\Theta(\theta_{14})\;V_{14}(\theta)\bigg]\,,\end{split}}

(10.49)

where
\theta
is the
ratio
of conformally invariant cross-ratios

\theta:=\frac{\theta_{12}\theta_{34}}{\theta_{14}\theta_{32}}=\frac{\frac{\langle\lam_{2}\mu_{1}\rangle\langle\lam_{4}\mu_{3}\rangle}{\langle\lam_{4}\mu_{1}\rangle\langle\lam_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle}}{\frac{[\lamt_{1}\tilde{\mu}_{4}][\lamt_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{2}]}{[\lamt_{1}\tilde{\mu}_{2}][\lamt_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{4}]}}=:\frac{r}{\bar{r}}\,,

(10.50)

and the polynomial functions
V_{12}
etc. are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}V_{12}(\theta)=&\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta}^{-2\bar{k}_{2}}}{2\bar{k}_{2}(2\bar{k}_{2}+2k_{3})(2\bar{k}_{2}+2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4})},\quad V_{32}(\theta)=\frac{1}{2\bar{k}_{2}2k_{3}(2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4})},\\
V_{14}(\theta)=&\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta}^{2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4}}}{2\bar{k}_{4}(2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4})(2\bar{k}_{2}+2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4})},\quad V_{34}(\theta)=\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta}^{2k_{3}}}{2k_{3}2\bar{k}_{4}(2\bar{k}_{2}+2k_{3})}.\\
\end{split}

(10.51)

Equation (
10.49
) is the main result of this section.
We have pulled out a conformally covariant factor
\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}\absolutevalue{\theta_{14}}^{2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4}}\absolutevalue{\theta_{34}}^{-2k_{3}}
and the remaining function has support in various ‘channels’ dictated by the signs of the variables
\theta_{ij}
. Since we have accounted for conformal covariance, this function can only depend on the conformally invariant cross-ratios
r,\bar{r}
. In fact, in each of these channels, we have simple polynomial functions (with imaginary powers) depending only on the absolute value of the ratio of conformally invariant cross-ratios.
On the momentum space celestial torus, momentum conservation at four points implied that the conformal cross-ratios
z,\bar{z}
are not independent but are actually equal
z=\bar{z}
. Correspondingly, in twistor space, the cross-ratios
r,\bar{r}
are now independent but the amplitude only depends on their ratio
\theta
.

Now we return to the task of finding the symmetrised celestial twistor amplitudes. We have sixteen parity combinations for the integrals
\mathcal{I}(r_{12},r_{32},r_{34},r_{14})
, two for each amplitude in (
10.37
). From (
10.49
) we can find all of them via (anti-)symmetrisation.
For example, we can find the fully symmetric integral

\begin{split}&\mathcal{I}(0,0,0,0)\\
&=2\pi i\,\delta\bigg(\sum_{i}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\bigg)\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}\absolutevalue{\theta_{14}}^{2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4}}\absolutevalue{\theta_{34}}^{-2k_{3}}\times\\
&\!\bigg(\Theta(\absolutevalue{\theta}-1)\bigg[-\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta}^{2k_{3}}}{(2k_{3}-\epsilon)(2\bar{k}_{4}+\epsilon)(2\bar{k}_{2}+2k_{3})}-\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta}^{-2\bar{k}_{2}}}{(2\bar{k}_{2}+\epsilon)(2\bar{k}_{2}+2k_{3})(2\bar{k}_{2}+2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4}+\epsilon)}\bigg]\\
+&\Theta(1-\absolutevalue{\theta})\bigg[-\frac{1}{(2\bar{k}_{2}+\epsilon)(2k_{3}-\epsilon)(2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4})}-\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta}^{2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4}}}{(2\bar{k}_{4}+\epsilon)(2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4})(2\bar{k}_{2}+2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4}+\epsilon)}\bigg]\bigg).\end{split}

(10.52)

The integral
\mathcal{I}(1,1,1,1)
takes a very similar form,

\begin{split}&\mathcal{I}(1,1,1,1)\\
&=2\pi i\,\delta\bigg(\sum_{i}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\bigg)\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}\absolutevalue{\theta_{14}}^{2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4}}\absolutevalue{\theta_{34}}^{-2k_{3}}\,\text{sgn}(\theta_{12}\theta_{32}\theta_{34}\theta_{14})\times\\
&\!\bigg(\Theta(\absolutevalue{\theta}-1)\bigg[\frac{1}{(2\bar{k}_{2}-\epsilon)(2k_{3}+\epsilon)(2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4})}+\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta}^{2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4}}}{(2\bar{k}_{4}-\epsilon)(2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4})(2\bar{k}_{2}+2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4}-\epsilon)}\bigg]\\
+&\Theta(1-\absolutevalue{\theta})\bigg[\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta}^{2k_{3}}}{(2k_{3}+\epsilon)(2\bar{k}_{4}-\epsilon)(2\bar{k}_{2}+2k_{3})}+\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta}^{-2\bar{k}_{2}}}{(2\bar{k}_{2}-\epsilon)(2\bar{k}_{2}+2k_{3})(2\bar{k}_{2}+2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4}-\epsilon)}\bigg]\bigg).\end{split}

(10.53)

In the above formulae, we have reinstated the
\epsilon
regularisation stemming from the result (
10.46
), which is required to prescribe the correct contour for the inverse Mellin transform.

The two integrals
\mathcal{I}(0,0,0,0)
and
\mathcal{I}(1,1,1,1)
contribute to the fully symmetrised celestial twistor amplitude

\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{+-+-}_{4}\{0,0,0,0\}=\frac{1}{16}\text{sgn}(\langle\mu_{1}\lambda_{2}\rangle\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle\langle\mu_{3}\lambda_{4}\rangle\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{1}\rangle)\tilde{\delta}\bigg(\sum_{i}s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}}\bigg)(\mathcal{I}(0,0,0,0)+\mathcal{I}(1,1,1,1)),

(10.54)

which in turn is a light transformed correlator in the celestial CFT. A similar fully symmetrised light transformed correlator was computed in
[
66
]
by performing light transforms on a (2,2) celestial amplitude analytically continued from (1,3) with summation over incoming/outgoing parameters. In that paper the result was computed in terms of special functions, Fox H-functions and Generalised I-functions, defined by Mellin-Barnes integrals; it would be interesting to understand how these results are related to the discussion here.

We find that the general structure of the formulas for
\mathcal{I}(0,0,0,0)
and
\mathcal{I}(1,1,1,1)
is repeated for all the symmetrised integrals. They all contain the same basic functions
V_{12},V_{32}
etc. inherited from the unsymetrised amplitude (
10.49
). Unlike the unsymetrised amplitude (
10.49
), which has support on a handful of channels dictated by the signs of the
\theta_{ij}
, the symmetrised amplitudes have a clarified structure with only two channels which are given by the magnitude of
\absolutevalue{\theta}
being greater than or less than one.

The only differences between the various symmetrised amplitudes are: the overall sgn functions which control the even and odd symmetry, which polynomial contibutions are probed by the two ‘channels’
\absolutevalue{\theta}>1
or
\absolutevalue{\theta}<1
, and the
\epsilon
regularisation of the poles in the weights. We note that, there are two terms
\mathcal{I}(r_{12},r_{32},r_{34},r_{14})
which have the same even/odd parity and so contribute to the same amplitude - these two terms have opposite arguments
r_{ij}=0\leftrightarrow r_{ij}=1
, opposite sgn function factors present, opposite
\epsilon
prescription for the poles and opposite allocation of terms to each ‘channel’.

Finally we note that all of the symmetrised integrals
\mathcal{I}(r_{12},r_{32},r_{34},r_{14})
(
10.41
) can be encapsulated in the general formula

\boxed{\begin{split}&\mathcal{I}(r_{12},r_{32},r_{34},r_{14})=2\pi i\;\text{sgn}\big[\langle\mu_{1}\lambda_{2}\rangle\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle\langle\mu_{3}\lambda_{4}\rangle\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{1}\rangle\big]\,\delta\bigg(\sum_{i}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\bigg)\;|\theta_{12}|^{2\bar{k}_{2}}|\theta_{14}|^{2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4}}|\theta_{34}|^{-2k_{3}}\\
&\times\prod_{(i,j):r_{ij}=1}\!\!\!\!\!\!(\text{sgn}(\theta_{ij}))\sum_{x,y}\bigg[\Big(\delta^{1}_{x}V_{x}(\theta)-\delta^{0}_{y}V_{y}(\theta)\Big)\Theta(|\theta|-1)+\Big(\delta^{1}_{y}V_{y}(\theta)-\delta^{0}_{x}V_{x}(\theta)\Big)\Theta(1-|\theta|)\bigg],\end{split}}

(10.55)

where in the above the product is over the values of
(i,j)
such that
r_{ij}=1
, we sum over the variables

x\in(r_{32},r_{14}),y\in(r_{12},r_{34})
, and we define
V_{r_{ij}}(\theta):=V_{ij}(\theta)
. As a further example, for the case
\mathcal{I}(1,0,1,0)
we have only a
\Theta(1-|\theta|)
term

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}(1,0,1,0)&=2\pi i\,\delta\bigg(\sum_{i}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\bigg)\,\text{sgn}(\theta_{12}\theta_{34})\,\Theta(1-\absolutevalue{\theta})\,\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}\absolutevalue{\theta_{14}}^{2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4}}\absolutevalue{\theta_{34}}^{-2k_{3}}\\
&\times\bigg(\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta}^{2k_{3}}}{(2k_{3})(2\bar{k}_{4})(2\bar{k}_{2}+2k_{3})}+\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta}^{-2\bar{k}_{2}}}{(2\bar{k}_{2})(2\bar{k}_{2}+2k_{3})(2\bar{k}_{2}+2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4})}\\
&-\frac{1}{(2\bar{k}_{2})(2k_{3})(2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4})}-\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta}^{2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4}}}{(2\bar{k}_{4})(2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4})(2\bar{k}_{2}+2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4})}\bigg)\,,\end{split}

(10.56)

which can be checked directly by performing the Mellin integrals in (
10.41
). Similarly for
\mathcal{I}(0,1,0,1)
we have only a
\Theta(|\theta|-1)
term. The simplicity of the four-point results can be seen to be a consequence of BCFW recursion relations in celestial twistor space which we explore in detail in Section
11
. The result for
\mathcal{I}
decomposed into all the even/odd parts
\mathcal{I}(r_{12},r_{32},r_{34},r_{14})
is given in a companion Mathematica file.

Four-Point ‘Separated’ Amplitude

Finally, the separated four-point amplitude
A^{YM}(++--)
is given by

\displaystyle A^{++--}_{4}=\frac{\langle 34\rangle^{4}}{\langle 12\rangle\langle 23\rangle\langle 34\rangle\langle 41\rangle}\,\delta^{4}(\lambda^{\alpha}_{1}\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}_{1}+\lambda^{\alpha}_{2}\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}_{2}+\lambda^{\alpha}_{3}\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}_{3}+\lambda^{\alpha}_{4}\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}_{4}).

(10.57)

We again perform the same twistor transforms as in (
10.35
) and so the twistor amplitude is in the same ambidextrous basis and takes the following form
[
24
]

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{A}^{++--}_{4}=&\frac{(\pi i)^{4}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{1}\rangle+[\tilde{\lambda}_{1}\tilde{\mu}_{2}])\,\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{1}\rangle+[\tilde{\lambda}_{1}\tilde{\mu}_{4}])\\
&\qquad\quad\times\delta^{(3)}(\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle+[\tilde{\lambda}_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{2}])\,\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{3}\rangle+[\tilde{\lambda}_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{4}])\\
=&\frac{1}{16}\text{sgn}(\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{1}\rangle\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{1}\rangle\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{3}\rangle)\absolutevalue{\agl{ \lambda_2}{\mu_3}}^{-4}\\
&\qquad\quad\times\text{sgn}(1+\theta_{12}^{-1})\,\text{sgn}(1+\theta_{14}^{-1})\delta^{(3)}(1+\theta_{32}^{-1})\,\text{sgn}(1+\theta_{34}^{-1}).\end{split}

(10.58)

The separated amplitude is hence
\absolutevalue{\agl{ \lambda_2}{\mu_3}}^{-4}
multiplied by the fourth derivative with respect to
\theta_{32}^{-1}
of the alternating amplitude above. Under the half-Mellin transforms this operator becomes
\absolutevalue{\agl{ \lambda_2}{\mu_3}}^{-4}(|\theta_{32}|^{2}\partial_{|\theta_{32}|})^{4}\,\exp(-2\partial_{\kb_2})
, which we then apply to (
10.49
).
When acting on
\Theta
functions containing
\theta_{32}
, either directly or via the cross-ratio
\theta
, these derivatives give (derivatives of) delta functions, which have only singular support and so again we drop these terms for generic kinematics. Furthermore, there are no powers of
\theta_{32}
in the first line of (
10.49
). Thus we only need to apply the derivatives to the powers of
\absolutevalue{\theta}
inside the
V
functions. This yields straightforward additional numerator factors, since

\displaystyle(|\theta_{32}|^{2}\partial_{|\theta_{32}|})^{4}\absolutevalue{\theta}^{n}=n(n-1)(n-2)(n-3)|\theta_{32}|^{4}|\theta|^{n}.

(10.59)

Hence we can easily find the separated amplitude from the alternating one.

We leave the task of explicitly computing celestial twistor amplitudes beyond four points to future work.
