However, we note that the method of producing twistor amplitudes by directly half-Fourier transforming momentum space amplitudes becomes more difficult as the number of particles and MHV degree grows. In particular in the ambidextrous basis of
[
24
]
at five points and beyond one encounters Grassmann integrals which do not disentangle into sgn functions. We can Mellin transform these integrals and we recover a class of integrals related to hyper-geometric functions and their generalisations studied in
[
66
,
63
]
. Nevertheless, the simplicity of ambidextrous twistor amplitudes extends well beyond four points, see for example the six-point ‘alternating’
(+,-,+,-,+,-)
amplitude in
[
24
]
. This simplicity is largely due to the existence of powerful BCFW formulae. The topic of BCFW recursion relations is something we explore in Section
11
and is a promising road to higher multiplicity celestial twistor amplitudes.

10.2
Pure Gravity

For the gravity case, we may follow similar arguments to those used above for celestial twistor gluon amplitudes.

10.2.1
Three Points: Mellin Transform of a Mod Function

Three-Point

MHV

For the three-point gravity amplitude we begin with the momentum space expression

\displaystyle M^{--+}_{3}=\frac{\langle 12\rangle^{6}}{\langle 23\rangle^{2}\langle 31\rangle^{2}}\,\delta^{4}(\lambda^{\alpha}_{1}\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}_{1}+\lambda^{\alpha}_{2}\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}_{2}+\lambda^{\alpha}_{3}\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}_{3}).

(10.60)

The ambidextrous twistor transform of this is

\displaystyle\tilde{M}^{--+}_{3}:=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int d^{2}\tilde{\lambda}_{1}\,d^{2}\tilde{\lambda}_{2}\,d^{2}\lambda_{3}\,e^{i[\tilde{\mu}_{1}\tilde{\lambda}_{1}]}\,e^{i[\tilde{\mu}_{2}\tilde{\lambda}_{2}]}\,e^{i\langle\lambda_{3}\mu_{3}\rangle}M^{--+}_{3},

(10.61)

which leads to
[
24
]

\displaystyle\tilde{M}^{--+}_{3}=\frac{(\pi i)^{3}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\Big|\langle\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\rangle\Big|\,\Big|\langle\lambda_{1}\mu_{3}\rangle+[\tilde{\lambda}_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{1}]\Big|\,\Big|\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle+[\tilde{\lambda}_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{2}]\Big|.

(10.62)

Analogous arguments to those given above for the YM case lead us to consider only the amplitudes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{M}^{--+}_{3}\{0,0,0\},\,\,\,\tilde{M}^{--+}_{3}\{1,1,0\},\,\,\,\tilde{M}^{--+}_{3}\{1,0,1\},\,\,\,\tilde{M}^{--+}_{3}\{0,1,1\},\end{split}

(10.63)

where the (anti-)symmetrised amplitudes take the general form

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\tilde{M}^{--+}_{3}\{s_{\bar{k}_{1}},s_{\bar{k}_{2}},s_{k_{3}}\}=\frac{(\pi i)^{3}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\Big|\langle\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\rangle\langle\lambda_{1}\mu_{3}\rangle\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle\Big|\,\delta\bigg(\sum_{i}s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}}\bigg)\,H_{s_{\bar{k}_{2}}+s_{k_{3}}}(\theta_{31}^{-1})H_{s_{\bar{k}_{1}}+s_{k_{3}}}(\theta_{32}^{-1}),\end{split}

(10.64)

where we recall the definition used above

\displaystyle\theta_{ij}=\frac{\langle\lambda_{j}\mu_{i}\rangle}{[\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\tilde{\mu}_{j}]}

(10.65)

and define the (anti-)symmetrised mod functions

\displaystyle\begin{split}H_{0}(x):=\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\Big|1+x\Big|+\Big|1-x\Big|\bigg),\\
H_{1}(x):=\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\Big|1+x\Big|-\Big|1-x\Big|\bigg).\end{split}

(10.66)

We then compute the following Mellin transforms

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\,x_{1}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}\,\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\Big|1+x_{1}\theta_{31}^{-1}\Big|+\Big|1-x_{1}\theta_{31}^{-1}\Big|\bigg)=\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{31}}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}}{2\bar{k}_{1}(2\bar{k}_{1}+1)}\,,\\
&\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\,x_{1}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}\,\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\Big|1+x_{1}\theta_{31}^{-1}\Big|-\Big|1-x_{1}\theta_{31}^{-1}\Big|\bigg)=\text{sgn}(-\theta_{31})\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{31}}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}}{2\bar{k}_{1}(2\bar{k}_{1}+1)}\,.\end{split}

(10.67)

We are again, by analogy with the Yang-Mills case, adopting a regularisation
\epsilon>0
of the weights such that boundary terms at infinity do not contribute. In the case of the Mellin transform of the antisymmetrised mod function
H_{1}
, the function is
\mathcal{O}(x)
for small
x
and
\mathcal{O}(1)
for large values. Hence the strip of definition is for
-1<\text{Re}(2\bar{k}_{1})<0
. In gravity, the helicity is now
-2
for leg one and the weight has real part
\text{Re}(2\bar{k}_{1})=-1
. Hence we can regularise the weight with
2\bar{k}_{1}\rightarrow 2\bar{k}_{1}+\epsilon
as usual. In other words, to invert the Mellin transform we can choose a straight line contour which goes to the right of the pole at minus one and to left of the pole at zero.

However, the case of the symmetrised
H_{0}
function in gravity is more subtle. The
H_{0}
function is
\mathcal{O}(1)
for small
x
but is
\mathcal{O}(x)
for large
x
and so the strip of definition does not exist and the integral is strongly divergent regardless of the value of the weights. This fact has been noted elsewhere in the literature e.g.
[
70
,
71
]
- in gravity the Mellin transform does not in general converge and this had led to the definition of a ‘regularised Mellin transform’ which importantly includes an explicit factor of
e^{-\delta x}
in its definition
[
70
,
72
]
. We implicitly use such a regularised Mellin transform and note that when we take
\delta\rightarrow 0_{+}
we recover the symmetric result of (
10.67
). One can also consider taking an inverse Mellin transform of the result by integrating over the weight
2\bar{k}_{1}
to recover the original function
H_{0}
. In this case, a suitable contour does exist, although it is no longer a vertical straight line since there is no such strip of definition. Instead, a contour which snakes to the left of the pole at minus one and then back around to the right of the pole at zero allows us to pick up the correct residues and recover
H_{0}
.

The three-point MHV celestial gravity twistor amplitude is then given by

\boxed{\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{--+}_{3}\{s_{\bar{k}_{1}},s_{\bar{k}_{2}},s_{k_{3}}\}=&\frac{\pi}{4}\Big|\langle\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\rangle\langle\lambda_{1}\mu_{3}\rangle\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle\Big|\,\delta\bigg(\sum_{i}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\,+2\bigg)\delta\bigg(\sum_{i}s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}}\bigg)\\
&\quad\times\text{sgn}(-\theta_{31})^{s_{\bar{k}_{1}}}\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{31}}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}}{2\bar{k}_{1}(2\bar{k}_{1}+1)}\text{sgn}(-\theta_{32})^{s_{\bar{k}_{2}}}\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}}{2\bar{k}_{2}(2\bar{k}_{2}+1)}\,.\end{split}}

(10.68)

Formulae (
10.67
) together imply that the unsymmetrised integral is given by

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\,x_{1}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}\,\Big|1+x_{1}\theta_{31}^{-1}\Big|=2\,\Theta(-\theta_{31})\frac{|\theta_{31}|^{2\bar{k}_{1}}}{2\bar{k}_{1}(2\bar{k}_{1}+1)}\,,

(10.69)

where as usual we drop terms proportional to a delta function in weight space. Similar considerations mentioned in the above cases also apply here regarding the regularisation of (
10.69
). Finally, we conclude that the unsymmetrised amplitude is

\boxed{\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{--+}_{3}=\frac{\pi}{4}\Big|\langle\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\rangle\langle\lambda_{1}\mu_{3}\rangle\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle\Big|\,\delta\bigg(\sum_{i}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\,+2\bigg)\,\Theta(-\theta_{31})\Theta(-\theta_{32})\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{31}}^{2\bar{k}_{1}}}{2\bar{k}_{1}(2\bar{k}_{1}+1)}\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}}{2\bar{k}_{2}(2\bar{k}_{2}+1)}.}

(10.70)

Three-Point

\overline{MHV}

The
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{++-}_{3}
amplitude is obtained by similar manipulations, and is

\boxed{\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{++-}_{3}\{s_{k_{1}},s_{k_{2}},s_{\bar{k}_{3}}\}=&\frac{\pi}{4}\Big|[\tilde{\lambda}_{1}\tilde{\lambda}_{2}][\tilde{\mu}_{3}\tilde{\lambda}_{1}][\tilde{\mu}_{3}\tilde{\lambda}_{2}]\Big|\,\delta\bigg(\sum_{i}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\,+2\bigg)\delta\bigg(\sum_{i}s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}}\bigg)\\
&\quad\times\text{sgn}(-\theta_{13})^{s_{k_{1}}}\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{13}^{-1}}^{2k_{1}}}{2k_{1}(2k_{1}+1)}\;\text{sgn}(-\theta_{23})^{s_{k_{2}}}\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{23}^{-1}}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}}{2\bar{k}_{2}(2\bar{k}_{2}+1)},\end{split}}

(10.71)

while the unsymmetrised amplitude is

\boxed{\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{++-}_{3}=\frac{\pi}{4}\Big|[\tilde{\lambda}_{1}\tilde{\lambda}_{2}][\tilde{\mu}_{3}\tilde{\lambda}_{1}][\tilde{\mu}_{3}\tilde{\lambda}_{2}]\Big|\,\delta\bigg(\sum_{i}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\,+2\bigg)\,\Theta(-\theta_{13})\Theta(-\theta_{23})\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{13}^{-1}}^{2k_{1}}}{2k_{1}(2k_{1}+1)}\;\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{23}^{-1}}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}}{2\bar{k}_{2}(2\bar{k}_{2}+1)}.}

(10.72)

10.2.2
Four Points: Mellin Transform of a Product of Mod Functions

Four-Point ‘Alternating’ Amplitude

Turning to four points, the ‘alternating’ four-point twistor amplitude takes the following form
[
24
]

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{M}^{+-+-}_{4}=&\frac{(\pi i)^{4}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\Big|\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{1}\rangle+[\tilde{\lambda}_{1}\tilde{\mu}_{2}]\Big|\,\Big|\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{1}\rangle+[\tilde{\lambda}_{1}\tilde{\mu}_{4}]\Big|\Big|\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle+[\tilde{\lambda}_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{2}]\Big|\,\Big|\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{3}\rangle+[\tilde{\lambda}_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{4}]\Big|\\
=&\frac{1}{16}\Big|\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{1}\rangle\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{1}\rangle\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{3}\rangle\Big|\Big|1+\theta_{12}^{-1}\Big|\,\Big|1+\theta_{14}^{-1}\Big|\Big|1+\theta_{32}^{-1}\Big|\,\Big|1+\theta_{34}^{-1}\Big|.\end{split}

(10.73)

We now perform the Mellin transforms first, following the Yang-Mills case for guidance, and only (anti-)symmetrise afterward. In evaluating the integrals below, we will drop boundary terms at zero and infinity by using suitable regularisations as per the discussion above.
We will need the integral

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{(a,b)}(y,k):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dx}{x}x^{2k}\Big|1+\frac{x}{a}\Big|\Big|1+\frac{x}{y\,b}\Big|,

(10.74)

which is the gravity analogue of the
\cJ_{2}(y)
and
\cJ_{4}(y)
Yang-Mills integrals (
10.44
). Defining the indefinite integral

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(a,b)}(x,y,k):=\int\frac{dx}{x}x^{2k}\Big(1+\frac{x}{a}\Big)\Big(1+\frac{x}{y\,b}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{x^{2k}}{2k}+\frac{x^{2k+1}}{2k+1}\bigg(\frac{1}{yb}+\frac{1}{a}\bigg)+\frac{x^{2k+2}}{2k+2}\frac{1}{yab}

(10.75)

we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{K}^{(a,b)}(y,k)=&2\,\Theta(b)\Theta(-a)\mathcal{L}^{(a,b)}(|a|,y,k)+2\,\Theta(-b)\Theta(a)\mathcal{L}^{(a,b)}(|b|y,y,k)\\
&+\Theta(-b)\Theta(-a)\,\text{sgn}\bigg(1-y\absolutevalue{\frac{b}{a}}\bigg)\Big(2\,\mathcal{L}^{(a,b)}(|b|y,y,k)-2\,\mathcal{L}^{(a,b)}(|a|,y,k)\Big).\end{split}

(10.76)

We note the similarity with the analogous Yang-Mills result
(
10.47
) the only difference being the form of the indefinite integral (
10.75
).

The gravity amplitude is then given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{+-+-}_{4}=&2\pi i\;\delta\bigg(\sum_{i}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\,+4\bigg)\;\big|\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{1}\rangle\big|\big|\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{1}\rangle\big|\big|\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle\big|\big|\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{3}\rangle\big|\;\\
&\qquad\times\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\;y^{2k_{3}-1}\mathcal{K}^{(\theta_{12},\theta_{32})}(y,\bar{k}_{2})\mathcal{K}^{(\theta_{14},\theta_{34})}(y,\bar{k}_{4}),\end{split}

(10.77)

where just like at three points we have a shifted dilatation invariance delta function. Dilatation invariance for both Yang-Mills and gravity is linked to the mass dimension of a
n
-point amplitude which must be
-n
in both cases. However, for gravity not just the momentum but also the coupling,
\kappa\coloneqq\sqrt{32\pi G_{N}}
, carries mass dimension minus one. Hence, for an
n
point amplitude with
n-2
powers of
\kappa
, the total power of spinors appearing is shifted by
2(n-2)
, and so the dilatation invariance delta function for gravity is shifted by
2(n-2)
, matching our results at three and four points.

Before stating the integrated result of (
10.77
), we first define the general form of the weight dependent coefficients that appear in the gravity case

\displaystyle C_{k,l,m}=-\frac{1}{2k(2k+1)}\;\frac{1}{2l(2l+1)}\;\frac{1}{2m(2m+1)}\,.

(10.78)

We also define the following functions of the conformal cross-ratios

\displaystyle\begin{split}W^{\pm}_{12}(\theta)=&\;C_{\bar{k}_{2},\bar{k}_{2}+k_{3}-\frac{1}{2},\bar{k}_{2}+k_{3}+\bar{k}_{4}}\;|\theta|^{-2\bar{k}_{2}}\pm C_{\bar{k}_{2}+\frac{1}{2},\bar{k}_{2}+k_{3},\bar{k}_{2}+k_{3}+\bar{k}_{4}+\frac{1}{2}}\;\absolutevalue{\theta}^{-2\bar{k}_{2}-1},\\
W^{\pm}_{32}(\theta)=&\;\pm C_{\bar{k}_{2}+\frac{1}{2},k_{3}-1,k_{3}+\bar{k}_{4}-\frac{1}{2}}\;|\theta|\;+C_{\bar{k}_{2},k_{3}-\frac{1}{2},k_{3}+\bar{k}_{4}}\,,\\
W^{\pm}_{14}(\theta)=&\;\pm C_{\bar{k}_{4}+\frac{1}{2},k_{3}+\bar{k}_{4},\bar{k}_{2}+k_{3}+\bar{k}_{4}+\frac{1}{2}}\;|\theta|^{2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4}+1}+C_{\bar{k}_{4},k_{3}+\bar{k}_{4}-\frac{1}{2},\bar{k}_{2}+k_{3}+\bar{k}_{4}}\;|\theta|^{2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4}}\,,\\
W^{\pm}_{34}(\theta)=&\;C_{\bar{k}_{4},k_{3}-\frac{1}{2},\bar{k}_{2}+k_{3}}\;|\theta|^{2k_{3}}\pm C_{\bar{k}_{4}+1/2,k_{3}-1,\bar{k}_{2}+k_{3}-\frac{1}{2}}\;|\theta|^{2k_{3}-1}\,.\end{split}

(10.79)

Finally, computing (
10.77
) the four-point ‘alternating’ unsymmetrised celestial twistor gravity amplitude is then

\boxed{\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{+-+-}_{4}&=\;\pi i\;\delta\bigg(\sum_{i}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\,+4\bigg)\;\big|(\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{1}\rangle\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{1}\rangle\langle\lambda_{2}\mu_{3}\rangle\langle\lambda_{4}\mu_{3}\rangle\big|\;|\theta_{12}|^{2\bar{k}_{2}}|\theta_{14}|^{2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4}}|\theta_{34}|^{-2k_{3}}\\
&\times\bigg[\,\Theta(-\theta_{12})\Theta(-\theta_{32})\Theta(-\theta_{14})\Theta(-\theta_{34})\,\text{sgn}(1-|\theta|)\Big(W^{-}_{12}(\theta)+W^{-}_{34}(\theta)-W^{-}_{32}(\theta)-W^{-}_{14}(\theta)\Big)\\
&\,\,\,\,-\Theta(-\theta_{12})\Theta(-\theta_{32})\Theta(\theta_{34})\Theta(-\theta_{14})\;W^{+}_{34}(\theta)-\Theta(\theta_{12})\Theta(-\theta_{32})\Theta(-\theta_{14})\Theta(-\theta_{34})\;W^{+}_{12}(\theta)\\
&\,\,\,\,-\Theta(-\theta_{12})\Theta(\theta_{32})\Theta(-\theta_{34})\Theta(-\theta_{14})\;W^{+}_{32}(\theta)-\Theta(-\theta_{12})\Theta(-\theta_{32})\Theta(-\theta_{34})\Theta(\theta_{14})\;W^{+}_{14}(\theta)\bigg].\end{split}}

(10.80)

From this, we can derive the symmetrised amplitudes by considering (anti-)symmetrisations which satisfy
\sum s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}}=0\mod 2
; the others are zero. Note the remarkably similar structure
to that of the Yang-Mills amplitude (
10.49
). All the symmetrised amplitudes in gravity may be captured by an analogous formula to (
10.55
) in the Yang-Mills case.
Finally, the ‘separated’ amplitude with helicities
(++--)
is then obtained from this by derivatives with respect to
|\theta_{32}|
, as discussed in the Yang-Mills case above.

11
BCFW Recursion

The BCFW-type recursion relations of
[
73
]
have been developed in twistor space in
[
24
]
, and here we will apply them in the celestial context. In this section, we will generate celestial twistor recursion relations and show how these can be used in practice to re-derive our results at four points.

For an
n
-point twistor amplitude, where the deformed legs
i
and
j
have helicities
(+,-)
, the recursion relation of
[
24
]
is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{A}_{n}=&\sum_{L,R}\text{sgn}(\langle\mu_{i}\lambda_{j}\rangle+[\tilde{\mu}_{j}\tilde{\lambda}_{i}])\int d^{2}\lambda\;d^{2}\lamt\;d^{2}\mu\;d^{2}\tilde{\mu}\;e^{i[\tilde{\lambda}\tilde{\lambda}_{i}]}e^{i\langle\lambda\lambda_{j}\rangle}\;\delta^{{}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\!\big(\langle\lam\mu\rangle+[\lamt\tilde{\mu}]\big)\;\\
&\qquad\qquad\;\Bigg(\tilde{A}_{L}\Big[(\mu_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i},+);(\lambda,\tilde{\mu},-)\Big]\tilde{A}_{R}\Big[(\lambda_{j},\tilde{\mu}_{j},-);(\mu,\tilde{\lambda},+)\Big]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\tilde{A}_{L}\Big[(\mu_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i},+);(\mu,\tilde{\lambda},+)\Big]\tilde{A}_{R}\Big[(\lambda_{j},\tilde{\mu}_{j},-);(\lambda,\tilde{\mu},-)\Big]\Bigg),\end{split}

(11.1)

where we are writing the twistors in terms of spinors as
W=(\mu,\tilde{\lambda}),Z=(\lambda,\tilde{\mu})
and any external leg variables that are not shifted in the amplitudes are not written explicitly. Note we work in an ambidextrous basis which follows the helicity of the particles, that is we use
W_{i},Z_{j}
for the
(+,-)
deformed legs. We also sum over the helicity of the glued leg which gives the two terms in (
11.1
) with different twistor variables -
(\mu,\tilde{\lambda})
for plus helicity and
(\lambda,\tilde{\mu})
for minus helicity.

We will focus on the case of Yang-Mills in the following. To obtain the recursion relation for the Mellin-transformed twistor amplitudes, we perform Mellin transforms on all the external variables, and inverse Mellin transforms on all the variables inside the amplitudes on the right-hand side of the above equation. Using the scaling properties of the amplitudes, the Mellin and inverse Mellin transforms cancel for all the external variables on the right-hand side except those labelled by
i
and
j
. We also have the inverse Mellin transform over
K,\bar{K}
for the glued legs of each left and right amplitude, we can choose a single pair of weights
K,\bar{K}
since the integral transform acts linearly on the BCFW expression. We thus obtain the following BCFW relation

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}=&\sum_{L,R}\int d^{2}\lambda\;d^{2}\tilde{\lambda}\;d^{2}\mu\;d^{2}\tilde{\mu}\int\;\frac{dK\;d\bar{K}}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\sum_{s_{K},s_{\bar{K}}\in\{0,1\}}\;e^{i[\tilde{\lambda}\tilde{\lambda}_{i}]}e^{i\langle\lambda\lambda_{j}\rangle}\;\delta^{{}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\Big(\langle\lam\mu\rangle+[\lamt\tilde{\mu}]\Big)\;\\
&\;\int\;\frac{d\kappa_{i}\;d\bar{\kappa}_{j}}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\!\!\sum_{s_{\kappa_{i}},s_{\bar{\kappa}_{j}}\in\{0,1\}}\!\!\!\!\!\!I(i,j)\Bigg(\;\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{L}\Big[(\mu_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i},\kappa_{i});(\lambda,\tilde{\mu},\bar{K})\Big]\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{R}\Big[(\lambda_{j},\tilde{\mu}_{j},\bar{\kappa}_{j});(\mu,\tilde{\lambda},K)\Big]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\;\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{L}\Big[(\mu_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i},\kappa_{i});(\mu,\tilde{\lambda},K)\Big]\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{R}\Big[(\lambda_{j},\tilde{\mu}_{j},\bar{\kappa}_{j});(\lambda,\tilde{\mu},\bar{K})\Big]\Bigg),\end{split}

(11.2)

where for notational simplicity we have left implicit the discrete weights
s_{K}
etc. which follow suit with the continuous ones. We have also defined the Mellin integrals
I(i,j)
for legs
i
and
j
which we evaluate first using the scale covariance of the celestial amplitudes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{I}(i,j)\coloneqq&\int_{\RR_{*}}\frac{dt_{i}}{\absolutevalue{t_i}}\;\absolutevalue{t_i}^{2(k_{i}-\kappa_{i})}\text{sgn}(t_{i})^{s_{k_{i}}-s_{\kappa_{i}}}\;\int_{\RR_{*}}\frac{d\bar{t}_{j}}{\absolutevalue{\tb_j}}\;\absolutevalue{\tb}_{j}^{2(\bar{k}_{j}-\bar{\kappa}_{j})}\text{sgn}(\bar{t}_{j})^{s_{\bar{k}_{j}}-s_{\bar{\kappa}_{j}}}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{sgn}\big(t_{i}\langle\mu_{i}\lambda_{j}\rangle+\bar{t}_{j}[\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\tilde{\mu}_{j}]\big)\\
=&\;2\pi i\,\delta(2(k_{i}+\bar{k}_{j}-\kappa_{i}-\bar{\kappa}_{j}))\,\tilde{\delta}(s_{k_{i}}+s_{\bar{k}_{j}}-s_{\kappa_{i}}-s_{\bar{\kappa}_{j}}+1)\,\text{sgn}(\langle\mu_{i}\lam_{j}\rangle)\absolutevalue{\theta_{ij}}^{2(\bar{k}_{j}-\bar{\kappa}_{j})}\\
&\qquad\qquad\text{sgn}(-\theta_{ij})^{s_{\bar{k}_{j}}-s_{\bar{\kappa}_{j}}}\frac{1}{2(\bar{k}_{j}-\bar{\kappa}_{j})+(-1)^{s_{\bar{k}_{j}}-s_{\bar{\kappa}_{j}}}\epsilon}\\
=&:2\pi i\,\delta(2(k_{i}+\bar{k}_{j}-\kappa_{i}-\bar{\kappa}_{j}))\,\tilde{\delta}(s_{k_{i}}+s_{\bar{k}_{j}}-s_{\kappa_{i}}-s_{\bar{\kappa}_{j}}+1)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\,\text{sgn}(\langle\mu_{i}\lam_{j}\rangle)\,\mathcal{G}(\bar{k}_{j}-\bar{\kappa}_{j},s_{\bar{k}_{j}}-s_{\bar{\kappa}_{j}},\theta_{ij}).\end{split}

(11.3)

which we have evaluated in terms of the function
\mathcal{G}(k,s,x)
which is just defined to be the Mellin transform over
\RR_{+}
of
G_{s}(x)
appearing in (
10.17
). Note again that, for readability, we are using the notation
\tilde{\delta}
for a Kronecker delta evaluated mod 2.

We continue performing all the Mellin transforms such that the BCFW relation is purely in terms of projective integrals. We first write
\delta^{{}^{\prime\prime\prime}}(x)\sim\int ds\;s^{3}\;e^{isx}
and then by rescaling
\mu,\tilde{\mu}
by
1/s
we perform the integral over
s
which generates the delta functions below in (
11.4
). In addition, we also break up the BCFW measure as
d^{2}\lam=du\absolutevalue{u}\langle\lam d\lam\rangle
,
d^{2}\mu=dt\absolutevalue{t}\langle\mu d\mu\rangle
etc., and perform the integrals over the scales
u,\bar{u},t,\bar{t}
which we can extract from the left and right celestial subamplitudes. Now recall that we are using ambidextrous variables for the glued legs and so we always have the relations
\bar{h}=-k
or
h=-\bar{k}
which we used at three and four-point and are specific to gluon amplitudes. Therefore we pull out the following factors in the integrals over
u,\bar{u},t,\bar{t}

18
18

18

Performing these integrals requires analytic continuation. That is we relax the conditions from the delta function and compute the integrals in the region of
K,\bar{K}
space where they converge. We then analytically continue the final expression to the region supported by the delta functions.

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}(\bar{K},K)\coloneqq&\;2\pi i\,\delta(2K+2\bar{K})\,\tilde{\delta}(s_{K}+s_{\bar{K}}+1)\int_{\RR_{*}}\!du\,d\bar{u}\,dt\,d\bar{t}\,\absolutevalue{u}^{1+2\bar{K}}\text{sgn}(u)^{-s_{\bar{K}}}\absolutevalue{\ub}^{1+2K}\text{sgn}(\bar{u})^{-s_{K}}\\
&\qquad\times\absolutevalue{\tb}^{1-2\bar{K}}\text{sgn}(\bar{t})^{-s_{\bar{K}}}\absolutevalue{t}^{1-2K}\text{sgn}(t)^{-s_{K}}\,e^{iu\langle\lam\lam_{j}\rangle}\,e^{i\bar{u}[\lamt\lamt_{i}]}\,e^{i\,tu\langle\lam\mu\rangle}\,e^{i\,\bar{t}\bar{u}[\lamt\tilde{\mu}]}.\end{split}

(11.4)

These integrals are of an identical form to (
9.6
), encountered when relating a half-Fourier transform to a light transform. From (
9.6
) we define

\mathcal{N}(a,b,x):=\int_{\RR_{*}}\,dt\,\absolutevalue{t}^{a-1}\text{sgn}(t)^{b}e^{itx}=2\pi\,i^{-b}\frac{\Gamma(a)}{\Gamma(\frac{a+b+1}{2})\Gamma(\frac{1-a-b}{2})}\absolutevalue{x}^{-a}\text{sgn}(x)^{b},

(11.5)

then we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}(\bar{K},K)=&\;2\pi i\,\delta(2K+2\bar{K})\,\tilde{\delta}(s_{K}+s_{\bar{K}}+1)\mathcal{N}(2-2K,-s_{K},\langle\lam\mu\rangle)\mathcal{N}(2-2\bar{K},-s_{\bar{K}},[\lamt\tilde{\mu}])\\
&\times\int_{\RR_{*}}\!du\,d\bar{u}\,\absolutevalue{u}^{2K+2\bar{K}-1}\text{sgn}(u)^{-s_{K}-s_{\bar{K}}}\absolutevalue{\ub}^{2\bar{K}+2K-1}\text{sgn}(\bar{u})^{-s_{K}-s_{\bar{K}}}\,e^{iu\langle\lam\lam_{j}\rangle}\,e^{i\bar{u}[\lamt\lamt_{i}]}.\end{split}

(11.6)

Now we perform the final integrals and simplify on the constraint of the delta functions,

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}(\bar{K},K)=&\;(2\pi i)^{3}\,\delta(2K+2\bar{K})\,\tilde{\delta}(s_{K}+s_{\bar{K}}+1)\,\mathcal{N}(2-2K,-s_{K},\langle\lam\mu\rangle)\\
&\qquad\times\mathcal{N}(2-2\bar{K},-s_{\bar{K}},[\lamt\tilde{\mu}])\,\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\lam_{j}\rangle[\lamt\lamt_{i}])\\
=&:(2\pi i)^{3}\,\delta(2K+2\bar{K})\,\tilde{\delta}(s_{K}+s_{\bar{K}}+1)\,\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\lam_{j}\rangle[\lamt\lamt_{i}])\,\mathcal{R}(2\bar{K},2K,s_{\bar{K}},s_{K}).\end{split}

(11.7)

Thus we obtain a BCFW relation given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}=&\;(2\pi i)^{4}\text{sgn}(\langle\mu_{i}\lam_{j}\rangle)\sum_{L,R}\int\frac{dK\;d\bar{K}}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\!\!\sum_{s_{K},s_{\bar{K}}\in\{0,1\}}\!\!\!\!\delta(2K+2\bar{K})\,\tilde{\delta}(s_{K}+s_{\bar{K}}+1)\\
&\times\int\!\frac{d\kappa_{i}\;d\bar{\kappa}_{j}}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\!\!\!\!\sum_{s_{\kappa_{i}},s_{\bar{\kappa}_{j}}\in\{0,1\}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\delta(2(k_{i}\!+\!\bar{k}_{j}\!-\!\kappa_{i}\!-\!\bar{\kappa}_{j}))\,\tilde{\delta}(s_{k_{i}}\!+\!s_{\bar{k}_{j}}\!-\!s_{\kappa_{i}}\!-\!s_{\bar{\kappa}_{j}}\!+1)\\
&\times\mathcal{G}(\bar{k}_{j}-\bar{\kappa}_{j},s_{\bar{k}_{j}}-s_{\bar{\kappa}_{j}},\theta_{ij})\int\,\langle\lam d\lam\rangle\,[\lamt d\lamt]\,\langle\mu\,d\mu\rangle\,[\tilde{\mu}\,d\tilde{\mu}]\,\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\lam_{j}\rangle[\lamt\lamt_{i}])\\
&\times\Bigg(\mathcal{R}(2\bar{K},2K,s_{\bar{K}},s_{K})\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{L}\Big[(\mu_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i},\kappa_{i});(\lambda,\tilde{\mu},\bar{K})\Big]\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{R}\Big[(\lambda_{j},\tilde{\mu}_{j},\bar{\kappa}_{j});(\mu,\tilde{\lambda},K)\Big]\\
&+\mathcal{R}(2K,2\bar{K},s_{K},s_{\bar{K}})\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{L}\Big[(\mu_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i},\kappa_{i});(\mu,\tilde{\lambda},K)\Big]\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{R}\Big[(\lambda_{j},\tilde{\mu}_{j},\bar{\kappa}_{j});(\lambda,\tilde{\mu},\bar{K})\Big]\Bigg).\end{split}

(11.8)

Having done all the scale integrals we now have a projective form of the BCFW recursion relations in celestial twistor space. We can actually simplify the above expression greatly since the integrals which glue the weights can be performed against the delta functions. The left and right Yang-Mills subamplitudes come with dilatation invariance delta functions and also Kronecker deltas enforcing overall evenness. For example, for the first product of amplitudes we have the delta functions

\begin{split}\delta\bigg(2\bigg(\kappa_{i}+\sum_{m\in L,m\not=i}\!\!\!\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\negmedspace\negmedspace{}_{m}+\bar{K}\bigg)\bigg)\,\tilde{\delta}\bigg(s_{\kappa_{i}}+\sum_{m\in L,m\not=i}\!\!s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}\!{}_{m}}+s_{\bar{K}}\bigg)\\
\times\delta\bigg(2\bigg(\bar{\kappa}_{j}+\sum_{m\in R,m\not=j}\!\!\!\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\negmedspace\negmedspace{}_{m}+K\bigg)\bigg)\,\tilde{\delta}\bigg(s_{\bar{\kappa}_{j}}+\sum_{m\in R,m\not=j}\!\!s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}\!{}_{m}}+s_{K}\bigg),\end{split}

(11.9)

while for the second term we simply swap
K
and
\bar{K}
. We now use the delta functions in (
11.9
) to localise the sums and integrals over
\kappa_{i},\bar{\kappa}_{j},s_{\kappa_{i}},s_{\bar{\kappa}_{j}}
. In addition we have the four external delta functions in (
11.8
) and plugging in the localised values of
\kappa_{i},\bar{\kappa}_{j},s_{\kappa_{i}},s_{\bar{\kappa}_{j}}
these give the overall dilatation invariance delta functions of the amplitude
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}

\begin{split}&\delta(2K+2\bar{K})\,\tilde{\delta}(s_{K}+s_{\bar{K}}+1)\delta\bigg(\sum_{a=1}^{n}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\negmedspace\negmedspace{}_{a}+2\!K+2\bar{K}\bigg)\,\tilde{\delta}\bigg(\sum_{a=1}^{n}s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}\!{}_{a}}+\!s_{K}\!+\!s_{\bar{K}}\!+1\bigg)\\
=&\;\delta\bigg(\sum_{a=1}^{n}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\negmedspace\negmedspace{}_{a}\bigg)\,\tilde{\delta}\bigg(\sum_{a=1}^{n}s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}\!{}_{a}}\bigg)\delta\bigg(2\!K+2\bar{K}\bigg)\,\tilde{\delta}\bigg(\!s_{K}\!+\!s_{\bar{K}}\!+1\bigg).\end{split}

(11.10)

Hence, we have the following BCFW recursion relation

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}=&(2\pi i)^{2}\text{sgn}(\langle\mu_{i}\lam_{j}\rangle)\delta\bigg(\sum_{a}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\negmedspace\negmedspace{}_{a}\bigg)\,\tilde{\delta}\bigg(\sum_{a}s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}\!{}_{a}}\bigg)\sum_{L,R}\int\frac{dK\;d\bar{K}}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\!\!\!\sum_{s_{K},s_{\bar{K}}\in\{0,1\}}\\
&\times\delta\bigg(2\!K+2\bar{K}\bigg)\,\tilde{\delta}\bigg(\!s_{K}\!+\!s_{\bar{K}}\!+1\bigg)\,\mathcal{G}(\bar{k}_{j}+k_{R}+\bar{K},s_{\bar{k}_{j}}+s_{k_{R}}+s_{\bar{K}},\theta_{ij})\\
&\times\int\,\langle\lam d\lam\rangle\,[\lamt d\lamt]\,\langle\mu\,d\mu\rangle\,[\tilde{\mu}\,d\tilde{\mu}]\,\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\lam_{j}\rangle[\lamt\lamt_{i}])\,\mathcal{R}(2K,2\bar{K},s_{K},s_{\bar{K}})\\
&\times\Bigg(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{L}\Big[(\mu_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i},-k_{L}-K);(\lambda,\tilde{\mu},K)\Big]\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{R}\Big[(\lambda_{j},\tilde{\mu}_{j},-k_{R}-\bar{K});(\mu,\tilde{\lambda},\bar{K})\Big]\\
&\quad+\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{L}\Big[(\mu_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i},-k_{L}-K);(\mu,\tilde{\lambda},K)\Big]\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{R}\Big[(\lambda_{j},\tilde{\mu}_{j},-k_{R}-\bar{K});(\lambda,\tilde{\mu},\bar{K})\Big]\Bigg),\end{split}

(11.11)

where in the above we have sub-amplitudes that are stripped of their dilatation invariance delta functions and also we have redefined summation and integration variables
(K,\bar{K},s_{K},s_{\bar{K}})
for each term to pull out common factors. We would also like to reiterate that all the weights in the above are for the
\mu,\tilde{\mu}
variables - the weights for the
\lam,\lamt
being found using the fact the helicity is
\pm 1
which implies the relations
h=-\bar{k}
and
\bar{h}=-k
. Although not written explictly above, the localised values of the discrete weights follow that of the continuous ones.

We now perform the inverse Mellin transform over
K
and
s_{K}
using the delta functions and arrive at the following BCFW recursion relation for celestial twistor Yang-Mills amplitudes

\boxed{\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}=&\;2\pi i\,\text{sgn}(\langle\mu_{i}\lam_{j}\rangle)\delta\bigg(\sum_{a}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\negmedspace\negmedspace{}_{a}\bigg)\,\tilde{\delta}\bigg(\sum_{a}s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}\!{}_{a}}\bigg)\sum_{L,R}\int\frac{d\bar{K}}{2\pi i}\!\!\sum_{s_{\bar{K}}\in\{0,1\}}\\
&\times\int\,\langle\lam d\lam\rangle\,[\lamt d\lamt]\,\langle\mu\,d\mu\rangle\,[\tilde{\mu}\,d\tilde{\mu}]\,\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\lam_{j}\rangle[\lamt\lamt_{i}])\\
&\qquad\times\mathcal{R}\bigg(-2\bar{K},2\bar{K},-s_{K}-1,s_{K}\bigg)\,\mathcal{G}(\bar{k}_{j}+k_{R}+\bar{K},s_{\bar{k}_{j}}+s_{k_{R}}+s_{\bar{K}},\theta_{ij})\\
&\times\Bigg(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{L}\Big[(\mu_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i},-k_{L}+\bar{K});(\lambda,\tilde{\mu},-\bar{K},-\hat{s}_{\bar{K}})\Big]\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{R}\Big[(\lambda_{j},\tilde{\mu}_{j},-k_{R}-\bar{K});(\mu,\tilde{\lambda},\bar{K},s_{\bar{K}})\Big]\\
&+\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{L}\Big[(\mu_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i},-k_{L}+\bar{K});(\mu,\tilde{\lambda},-\bar{K},-\hat{s}_{\bar{K}})\Big]\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{R}\Big[(\lambda_{j},\tilde{\mu}_{j},-k_{R}-\bar{K});(\lambda,\tilde{\mu},\bar{K},s_{\bar{K}})\Big]\Bigg),\end{split}}

(11.12)

where we have again left the discrete weights implicit in the above expression but they follow the same pattern as the continuous weights with one exception - in the left sub-amplitudes the weight
s_{K}
is localised to the shifted value
-\hat{s}_{\bar{K}}:=-s_{\bar{K}}-1
.

We are left with a contour integral over
\bar{K}
and a sum over the weights
s_{\bar{K}}
, together with four projective integrals over
\lam,\lamt,\mu,\tilde{\mu}
. Carrying these out will generate the full amplitude - we demonstrate this in an example below by computing the four-point amplitude from a product of two three-point amplitudes, after which we will make some general comments valid at
n
-points.

In fact we can carry out the integrals over
\mu,\tilde{\mu}
above immediately, since the pre-factor
\mathcal{R}(-2\bar{K},2\bar{K},-s_{\bar{K}}-1,s_{\bar{K}})
is given by

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}&=4\pi^{2}i\frac{\Gamma(2+2\bar{K})\Gamma(2-2\bar{K})\langle\absolutevalue{\agl{\lam}{\mu}}\mu\rangle^{2\bar{K}-2}\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\mu\rangle)^{s_{\bar{K}}}[\absolutevalue{\sqr{\lamt}{\mut}}\tilde{\mu}]^{-2\bar{K}-2}\text{sgn}([\lamt\tilde{\mu}])^{s_{\bar{K}}+1}}{\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-\bar{K}-\frac{s_{\bar{K}}}{2})\Gamma(\bar{K}+\frac{s_{\bar{K}}}{2}-\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(2+\bar{K}+\frac{s_{\bar{K}}}{2})\Gamma(-\bar{K}-\frac{s_{\bar{K}}}{2}-1)},\end{split}

(11.13)

and contains exactly the correct factors of
\langle\absolutevalue{\agl{\lam}{\mu}}\mu\rangle
etc. and the correct normalisation such that the
\mu
(respectively
\tilde{\mu}
) integral is a light transform (
8.3
) (respectively dual light transform (
8.5
)) of the glued legs. The light and dual light transforms are self inverse so we conclude that after the
\mu,\tilde{\mu}
integrals, the glued legs are expressed with variables
\lam,\lamt
and are only half-Mellin transformed without any light transforms acting at all. We can hence write down an alternative form of celestial BCFW recursion which takes as input amplitudes with
all but one leg light transformed
, and glues the un-transformed legs resulting in an amplitude with all legs light transformed. From (
11.12
) the alternative BCFW recursion relation is

\boxed{\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}=&\;2\pi i\,\text{sgn}(\langle\mu_{i}\lam_{j}\rangle)\delta\bigg(\sum_{a}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\negmedspace\negmedspace{}_{a}\bigg)\,\tilde{\delta}\bigg(\sum_{a}s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}\!{}_{a}}\bigg)\sum_{L,R}\int\frac{d\bar{K}}{2\pi i}\!\!\sum_{s_{\bar{K}}\in\{0,1\}}\\
&\times\mathcal{G}(\bar{k}_{j}+k_{R}+\bar{K},s_{\bar{k}_{j}}+s_{k_{R}}+s_{\bar{K}},\theta_{ij})\int\,\langle\lam d\lam\rangle\,[\lamt d\lamt]\,\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\lam_{j}\rangle[\lamt\lamt_{i}])\\
&\times\Bigg(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{L}\Big[(\mu_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i},-k_{L}+\bar{K},+);(\lambda,\lamt,\bar{h}_{L},s_{\bar{h}_{L}},-)\Big]\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{R}\Big[(\lambda_{j},\tilde{\mu}_{j},-k_{R}-\bar{K},-);(\lam,\tilde{\lambda},h_{R},s_{h_{R}},+)\Big]\\
+&\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{L}\Big[(\mu_{i},\tilde{\lambda}_{i},-k_{L}+\bar{K},+);(\lam,\tilde{\lambda},h_{L},s_{h_{L}},+)\Big]\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{R}\Big[(\lambda_{j},\tilde{\mu}_{j},-k_{R}-\bar{K},-);(\lambda,\lamt,\bar{h}_{R},s_{\bar{h}_{R}},-)\Big]\Bigg),\end{split}}

(11.14)

where the weights of the glued legs are

\begin{split}\bar{h}_{L}=1+\bar{K}=h_{L}\quad\bar{h}_{R}=1-\bar{K}=h_{R}\,,\\
s_{\bar{h}_{L}}=-s_{\bar{K}}-1=s_{h_{L}}\quad s_{\bar{h}_{R}}=s_{\bar{K}}=s_{h_{R}}.\end{split}

(11.15)

We conclude that the most economical BCFW recursion relation for celestial twistor amplitudes happens to involve gluing sub-amplitudes with one leg in the Mellin basis and the rest in the light transformed basis. This form of the recursion may not necessarily be the most useful at high multiplicity since it requires recursion on an additional set of amplitudes beyond those we are immediately interested in. Nevertheless, the recursion relation (
11.14
) proves very useful for demonstrating the four-point BCFW recursion relation as we will see below, since the three-point sub-amplitudes can be easily computed directly.

The BCFW recursion relations for gravity amplitudes take a very similar form. The only difference to the BCFW expression comes from the changed dilatation invariance delta function which, as we saw at three and four points, is shifted by
2(n-2)
due to the mass dimension carried by the powers of the coupling
\kappa
in an
n
point gravity amplitude. Hence, in the gravity case the delta functions of the sub-amplitudes (
11.9
) are shifted and so the localised values of the continuous weights of legs
i
and
j
on the left and right subamplitudes are shifted by
2(n_{L,R}-2)
also, while the localised values for the discrete weights are the same as for the Yang-Mills case.

Four-Point BCFW

To see how the recursion relation works in an explicit example, consider the four-point amplitude
A(1^{+},2^{-},3^{+},4^{-})
with a BCFW shift of legs
(1^{+},4^{-})
. In this case the amplitude pairs that appear in the BCFW recursion are

A^{\overline{MHV}}_{L}(1^{+},2^{-},P^{+})A^{MHV}_{R}(P^{-},3^{+},4^{-})+A^{MHV}_{L}(1^{+},2^{-},P^{-})A^{\overline{MHV}}_{R}(P^{+},3^{+},4^{-}).

(11.16)

As usual the second pair of amplitudes do not contribute since they vanish for generic kinematics and so we consider just the first pair.
The relevant three-point
MHV
and
\overline{MHV}
celestial amplitudes with two legs light transformed are derived in Appendix
A
and for the case at hand are given by

\begin{split}&\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{L}^{\overline{MHV}}\Big[(\mu_{1},\lamt_{1},-\bar{k}_{2}+\bar{K},+),(\lam_{2},\tilde{\mu}_{2},\bar{k}_{2},-),(\lam,\lamt,1+\bar{K},-s_{\bar{K}}-1,+)\Big]\\
=&\;\delta(\langle\lam\lam_{2}\rangle)\,\text{sgn}([\lamt_{1}\lamt][\lamt_{1}\tilde{\mu}_{2}])\,\text{sgn}(-\theta_{12})^{-s_{\bar{k}_{2}}+s_{\bar{K}}+1}\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}^{-1}}^{-2\bar{k}_{2}+2\bar{K}}}{-2\bar{k}_{2}+2\bar{K}+(-1)^{-s_{\bar{k}_{2}}+s_{\bar{K}}+1}\epsilon}\\
&\qquad\qquad\times\mathcal{N}\big(2\bar{K},-s_{\bar{K}},[\lamt\tilde{\mu}_{2}]\big),\end{split}

(11.17)

and

\begin{split}&\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{R}^{MHV}\Big[(\mu_{3},\lamt_{3},k_{3},+),(\lam_{4},\tilde{\mu}_{4},-k_{3}-\bar{K},-),(\lam,\lamt,1-\bar{K},s_{\bar{K}},-)\Big]\\
=&\;\delta([\lamt\lamt_{3}])\,\text{sgn}(\langle\lam_{4}\lam\rangle\langle\mu_{3}\lam_{4}\rangle)\,\text{sgn}(-\theta_{34})^{-s_{k_{3}}-s_{\bar{K}}}\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{34}}^{-2k_{3}-2\bar{K}}}{-2k_{3}-2\bar{K}+(-1)^{-s_{k_{3}}-s_{\bar{K}}}\epsilon}\\
&\qquad\qquad\times\mathcal{N}\big(-2\bar{K},s_{\bar{K}}+1,\langle\mu_{3}\lam\rangle\big).\end{split}

(11.18)

where we have stripped off the dilatation invariance delta functions since they have already been used to localise the weights.

We now consider the product of these two amplitudes and perform the integrals over
\lam,\lamt
. Using the delta functions which come from three-point momentum conservation, we identify collinear pairs
\lam=\lam_{2}
and
\lamt=\lamt_{3}
19
19

19

The other BCFW pair gives vanishing contribution for four particle kinematics. We can see this directly since it has delta functions which give collinear pairs
\lam=\lam_{4}
and
\lamt=\lamt_{1}
which forces the vanishing of the
\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\lam_{4}\rangle)\,\text{sgn}([\lamt\lamt_{1}])
factors in the BCFW relation.
. The four-point amplitude is then given by

\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{4}^{+-+-}=&\;2\pi i\,\text{sgn}(\langle\mu_{1}\lam_{4}\rangle)\delta\bigg(\sum_{a}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\negmedspace\negmedspace{}_{a}\bigg)\,\tilde{\delta}\bigg(\sum_{a}s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}\!{}_{a}}\bigg)\int\frac{d\bar{K}}{2\pi i}\!\!\sum_{s_{\bar{K}}\in\{0,1\}}\\
&\times\mathcal{G}(k_{3}+\bar{k}_{4}+\bar{K},s_{k_{3}}+s_{\bar{k}_{4}}+s_{\bar{K}},\theta_{14})\,\text{sgn}(\langle\lam_{2}\lam_{4}\rangle)\,\text{sgn}([\lamt_{3}\lamt_{1}])\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{L}^{\overline{MHV}}\,\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{R}^{MHV}\\
=&\;2\pi i\,\text{sgn}([\lamt_{1}\tilde{\mu}_{2}]\langle\mu_{3}\lam_{4}\rangle\langle\mu_{1}\lam_{4}\rangle)\delta\bigg(\sum_{a}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\negmedspace\negmedspace{}_{a}\bigg)\,\tilde{\delta}\bigg(\sum_{a}s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}\!{}_{a}}\bigg)\int\frac{d\bar{K}}{2\pi i}\!\!\sum_{s_{\bar{K}}\in\{0,1\}}\\
&\times\mathcal{G}(k_{3}+\bar{k}_{4}+\bar{K},s_{k_{3}}+s_{\bar{k}_{4}}+s_{\bar{K}},\theta_{14})\,\text{sgn}(-\theta_{12})^{-s_{\bar{k}_{2}}+s_{\bar{K}}+1}\text{sgn}(-\theta_{34})^{-s_{k_{3}}-s_{\bar{K}}}\\
&\quad\times\,\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}^{-1}}^{-2\bar{k}_{2}+2\bar{K}}}{-2\bar{k}_{2}+2\bar{K}+(-1)^{-s_{\bar{k}_{2}}+s_{\bar{K}}+1}\epsilon}\,\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{34}}^{-2k_{3}-2\bar{K}}}{-2k_{3}-2\bar{K}+(-1)^{-s_{k_{3}}-s_{\bar{K}}}\epsilon}\\
&\quad\quad\times\,\mathcal{N}\big(2\bar{K},-s_{\bar{K}},[\lamt_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{2}]\big)\,\mathcal{N}\big(-2\bar{K},s_{\bar{K}}+1,\langle\mu_{3}\lam_{2}\rangle\big).\end{split}

(11.19)

Now, using the Legendre Duplication Formula for gamma functions (
B.16
) we find that

\begin{split}&\mathcal{N}\big(2\bar{K},-s_{\bar{K}},[\lamt_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{2}]\big)\,\mathcal{N}\big(-2\bar{K},s_{\bar{K}}+1,\langle\mu_{3}\lam_{2}\rangle\big)\\
=&\;2\pi i\frac{1}{2\bar{K}+(-1)^{s_{\bar{K}}}\epsilon}[\absolutevalue{\sqr{\lamt_3}{\mut_2}}_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{2}]^{-2\bar{K}}\text{sgn}([\lamt_{3}\tilde{\mu}_{2}])^{-s_{\bar{K}}}\langle\mu_{3}\absolutevalue{\agl{\mu_3}{\lam_2}}_{2}\rangle^{2\bar{K}}\text{sgn}(\langle\mu_{3}\lam_{2}\rangle)^{s_{\bar{K}}+1}\\
=&\;2\pi i\frac{1}{2\bar{K}+(-1)^{s_{\bar{K}}}\epsilon}\text{sgn}(\langle\mu_{3}\lam_{2}\rangle)\,\text{sgn}(-\theta_{32})^{s_{\bar{K}}}\absolutevalue{\theta_{32}}^{2\bar{K}},\end{split}

(11.20)

where we have also included a regularisation of the pole in
2\bar{K}
. As usual, when we integrate over
\bar{K}
, this regularisation corresponds to a contour along the imaginary axis which avoids the pole either to the left or right. Using the above and the definition of the
\mathcal{G}
function in (
11.3
), the four-point amplitude is given by

\boxed{\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{4}^{+-+-}=&\;4\pi^{2}\,\text{sgn}(\langle\mu_{1}\lam_{2}\rangle\langle\mu_{3}\lam_{2}\rangle\langle\mu_{3}\lam_{4}\rangle\langle\mu_{1}\lam_{4}\rangle)\delta\bigg(\sum_{a}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\negmedspace\negmedspace{}_{a}\bigg)\,\tilde{\delta}\bigg(\sum_{a}s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}\!{}_{a}}\bigg)\int\frac{d\bar{K}}{2\pi i}\!\!\sum_{s_{\bar{K}}\in\{0,1\}}\\
&\times\text{sgn}(\theta_{12})^{s_{\bar{k}_{2}}+s_{\bar{K}}}\text{sgn}(\theta_{32})^{s_{\bar{K}}}\text{sgn}(\theta_{34})^{s_{k_{3}}+s_{\bar{K}}}\text{sgn}(\theta_{14})^{s_{k_{3}}+s_{\bar{k}_{4}}+s_{\bar{K}}}\\
&\quad\times\,\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}\absolutevalue{\theta_{14}}^{2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4}}\absolutevalue{\theta_{34}}^{-2k_{3}}}{2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4}+2\bar{K}+(-1)^{s_{k_{3}}+s_{\bar{k}_{4}}+s_{\bar{K}}}\epsilon}\,\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta}^{-2\bar{K}}}{2\bar{K}+(-1)^{s_{\bar{K}}}\epsilon}\\
&\quad\quad\times\frac{1}{2k_{3}+2\bar{K}+(-1)^{s_{k_{3}}+s_{\bar{K}}+1}\epsilon}\,\frac{1}{-2\bar{k}_{2}+2\bar{K}+(-1)^{s_{\bar{k}_{2}}+s_{\bar{K}}+1}\epsilon}.\end{split}}

(11.21)

The sum over
s_{\bar{K}}
gives two terms which exactly correspond to the decomposition (
10.54
) which we previously found. The two terms are the unique
\mathcal{I}(r_{12},r_{32},r_{34},r_{14})
with the correct even/odd symmetry and, as already noticed, these terms have the opposite
\epsilon
prescriptions and sgn function factors.

The contour integral over
\bar{K}
has contour along the pure imaginary axis and the expression has poles at
\bar{K}=(0,\bar{k}_{2},-k_{3}-\bar{k}_{4},-k_{3})
with varying
\epsilon
prescription away from the imaginary axis. It is straightforward to check that the residues on these poles generate four terms involving the functions
V_{32},V_{12},V_{14},V_{34}
given in (
10.51
) which make up all four-point symmetrised amplitudes. With regard to the choice of contours, the
\bar{K}
dependence in the above is
|\theta|^{-\bar{2}K}
, where
\theta
is the ratio of cross-ratios. In order to avoid contributions at infinity the contour must be chosen to close to the right with large positive
\bar{K}
for
|\theta|>1
, while for
|\theta|<1
it must close to the left with large negative
\bar{K}
. So for each case we pick up a subset of the poles, namely those with
\epsilon
prescription such that the poles lie in either the left or right hand half plane. In this way the various functions
V_{32},V_{12},V_{14},V_{34}
are assigned to the channels
|\theta|>1
or
|\theta|<1
.

For example, the fully symmetrised amplitude has all the
s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}\!{}_{a}}
equal to zero and so we have two terms

\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{4}^{+-+-}\{0,0,0,0\}&=4\pi^{2}\,\text{sgn}(\langle\mu_{1}\lam_{2}\rangle\langle\mu_{3}\lam_{2}\rangle\langle\mu_{3}\lam_{4}\rangle\langle\mu_{1}\lam_{4}\rangle)\delta\bigg(\sum_{a}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}\negmedspace\negmedspace{}_{a}\bigg)\\
&\times\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}}^{2\bar{k}_{2}}\absolutevalue{\theta_{14}}^{2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4}}\absolutevalue{\theta_{34}}^{-2k_{3}}[I(0,0,0,0)+\text{sgn}(\theta_{12}\theta_{32}\theta_{34}\theta_{14})\,I(1,1,1,1)]\end{split}

(11.22)

where, focusing on the contour integral
I(0,0,0,0)
we have

\begin{split}I(0,0,0,0)&:=\int\frac{d\bar{K}}{2\pi i}\,\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta}^{-2\bar{K}}}{2k_{3}+2\bar{k}_{4}+2\bar{K}+\epsilon}\,\frac{1}{2\bar{K}+\epsilon}\frac{1}{2k_{3}+2\bar{K}-\epsilon}\,\frac{1}{-2\bar{k}_{2}+2\bar{K}-\epsilon}\end{split}

(11.23)

which gives four terms, one for each residue, with two terms appearing in each channel as dictated by the
\pm\epsilon
prescription. This exactly matches the four terms (
10.52
) appearing in the function
\mathcal{I}(0,0,0,0)
found in the four-point amplitude. The
I(1,1,1,1)
contour integral has opposite
\pm\epsilon
prescription and takes a similar form which matches the terms in
\mathcal{I}(1,1,1,1)
in (
10.53
).

Beyond four points we will encounter similar steps and the BCFW recursion relations will allow us to iteratively build amplitudes using previous results as the inputs. Given the amplitudes with up to
n
particles, we can build the
n+1
-point amplitude by a sum of terms involving the gluing of two sub-amplitudes, with integrals over the homogeneous coordinates of the glued legs on the celestial torus. Given the similarity of these integrals to the integrals in the twistor BCFW recursion relations of
[
24
]
, we might expect a diagrammatic formalism à la Hodges
[
74
]
to exist. Once the gluing is complete the contour integral would simply build terms in a combinatorial fashion by evaluating residues on the poles of the BCFW expression. We leave the exploration of such higher multiplicity questions to future work.

12
Conclusions

In this paper we have computed ambidextrous light transformed correlators by deriving celestial twistor amplitudes. This involved the formulation and understanding of the Fourier, twistor, shadow, light and Mellin transforms in their appropriate signature spacetimes. We proved relations between these transforms, summarised by the commuting diagrams in Figures
1
,
3
,
4
, before their application to derive three and four-point YM and gravity amplitudes, and a general BCFW recursion relation in sections
10
and
11
. In concluding we would like to highlight some areas for further research which are prompted by our results.

We found a concise expression for the four-point YM amplitude in (
10.55
) and it would be interesting to relate this to the four-point results of
[
66
]
for four light-transformed fields. For gravity, the four-point alternating amplitude, given in (
10.80
), shows a striking similarity in structure to its YM analogue - clarifying the physics behind this may lead to insights applicable to more general cases. The application of the double copy for celestial twistor amplitudes (c.f.
[
75
,
42
]
) should also be explored in this context.

Extending these results to more general amplitudes is clearly a next step. Higher point results could naturally be derived from the twistor celestial recursion relation, given in (
11.12
) or (
11.14
). The structure of contour integrals and cross-ratios that was apparent in our BCFW derivation of the four-point amplitude would be expected to persist at higher points and it would be interesting to pursue this via direct calculations. In particular the
n
-point MHV amplitudes may be amenable to this approach.
It would also be of interest to understand if the integrands in these recursion relations have some geometric interpretation, perhaps analogous to the
diagrammatic rules and identities described in
[
24
]
for the twistor amplitudes.

Developing the OPEs of light transformed correlators via celestial twistor amplitudes should provide insights into the construction of higher point amplitudes as well as elucidate the structure of celestial conformal field theory, along with factorisation, conformal blocks, etc. These OPEs should follow from the consequences of collinear limits for the amplitudes presented here. The extension of this work to supersymmetry can be done using the results in
[
12
,
13
]
- since the anticommuting superspace coordinate scales under the little group, the chiral Mellin transform is modified for each of the superspace component fields and hence amplitudes. The twistor space super-amplitudes described in
[
24
]
neatly package together their component sub-amplitudes and one might expect similar simplifications for their celestial analogues. There would then exist super-shadow and super-light transforms that are related to super-Fourier and super-twistor transforms. Thus the celestial twistor superamplitudes could be found by Mellin transforming twistor space superamplitudes.

The relative simplicity and interesting structure of the twistor celestial amplitudes derived in this paper would seem to provide encouraging signs for developing this approach in these directions.

The work of GRB and JG is supported by STFC quota studentships.

Appendix A
All But One Leg Celestial Twistor Amplitudes

In this appendix, we present three-point amplitudes with all but one leg half-Fourier transformed and also their celestial versions found via ambidextrous half-Mellin transforms. These amplitudes feature in a form of the BCFW recursion relations (
11.14
) for celestial twistor amplitudes and we have used them above to derive the four-point ambidextrous celestial twistor amplitude via BCFW recursion.

Ambidextrously half-Fourier transforming legs one and two of an
\overline{MHV}
three-point amplitude we have

\begin{split}A^{\overline{MHV}}\big[(\mu_{1},\lamt_{1},+),(\lam_{2},\tilde{\mu}_{2},-),(\lam,\lamt,+)\big]=&\;\delta(\langle\lam\lam_{2}\rangle)\,\text{sgn}([\lamt_{1}\lamt])\,\text{sgn}(\langle\mu_{1}\lam_{2}\rangle+[\tilde{\mu}_{2}\lamt_{1}])\\
\qquad&\times\frac{\langle\lam_{2}\xi\rangle}{\langle\xi\lam\rangle}\,i\frac{\langle\exp(i\frac{\agl{\lam}{\xi}}{\agl{\xi}{\lam_2}}\sqr{\mut_2}{\lamt})\xi\rangle}{\langle\xi\lam_{2}\rangle}[\tilde{\mu}_{2}\lamt]\end{split}

(A.1)

which can be derived by an identical method to that laid out in
[
24
]
. We have written the result in terms of a reference spinor
\xi
on which the amplitude does not depend. This property is a result of the presence of the delta function
\delta(\langle\lam\lam_{2}\rangle)
which is the remnant of three-point momentum conservation. We now half-Mellin transform over the scales of the spinors
\mu_{1},\tilde{\mu}_{2},\lam
with weights
k_{1},\bar{k}_{2},h
and their corresponding discrete weights. Since we have already performed many such Mellin transforms of exponentials and sgn functions we can refer to previous calculations, for example (
10.18
) and (
9.6
), and we find

\begin{split}&A^{\overline{MHV}}\big[(\mu_{1},\lamt_{1},k_{1},+),(\lam_{2},\tilde{\mu}_{2},\bar{k}_{2},-),(\lam,\lamt,h,+)\big]\\
\qquad=&\;\delta(\langle\lam\lam_{2}\rangle)\,\text{sgn}([\lamt_{1}\lamt][\tilde{\mu}_{2}\lamt_{1}])\,\frac{\langle\lam_{2}\xi\rangle}{\langle\xi\lam\rangle}\text{sgn}(-\theta_{12})^{s_{k_{1}}}\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}^{-1}}^{2k_{1}}}{2k_{1}+(-1)^{s_{k_{1}}}\epsilon}\\
&\qquad\times\delta\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{2}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}+2-2h\bigg)\,\tilde{\delta}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{2}s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}}+s_{h}\bigg)\mathcal{N}\Big(2h-2,s_{h}+1,\frac{\langle\lam\xi\rangle}{\langle\xi\lam_{2}\rangle}[\tilde{\mu}_{2}\lamt]\Big),\end{split}

(A.2)

where the function
\mathcal{N}(a,b,x)
is defined in the main text (
11.5
).

Since in a celestial amplitude the spinors act as homogeneous coordinates, the delta function
\delta(\langle\lam\lam_{2}\rangle)
actually implies
\lam,\lam_{2}
are equivalent as homogeneous coordinates and so we may write

\begin{split}&A^{\overline{MHV}}\big[(\mu_{1},\lamt_{1},k_{1},+),(\lam_{2},\tilde{\mu}_{2},\bar{k}_{2},-),(\lam,\lamt,h,+)\big]\\
=\;&\delta(\langle\lam\lam_{2}\rangle)\,\text{sgn}([\lamt_{1}\lamt][\lamt_{1}\tilde{\mu}_{2}])\,\text{sgn}(-\theta_{12})^{s_{k_{1}}}\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{12}^{-1}}^{2k_{1}}}{2k_{1}+(-1)^{s_{k_{1}}}\epsilon}\\
&\qquad\times\delta\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{2}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}+2-2h\bigg)\,\tilde{\delta}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{2}s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}}+s_{h}\bigg)\mathcal{N}\Big(2h-2,s_{h}+1,[\lamt\tilde{\mu}_{2}]\Big),\end{split}

(A.3)

and the apparent dependence on the reference spinor drops out. The
MHV
amplitude is completely analogous. We half-Fourier transform legs 3 and 4 and then ambidextrously half-Mellin transform and find

\begin{split}&A^{MHV}\big[(\mu_{3},\lamt_{3},k_{3},+),(\lam_{4},\tilde{\mu}_{4},\bar{k}_{4},-),(\lam,\lamt,\bar{h},-)\big]\\
=\;&\delta([\lamt\lamt_{3}])\,\text{sgn}(\langle\lam_{4}\lam\rangle\langle\mu_{3}\lam_{4}\rangle)\,\text{sgn}(-\theta_{34})^{s_{\bar{k}_{4}}}\frac{\absolutevalue{\theta_{34}}^{2\bar{k}_{4}}}{2\bar{k}_{4}+(-1)^{s_{\bar{k}_{4}}}\epsilon}\\
&\qquad\times\delta\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{2}2\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 5.63496pt)}$}\vss}}}+2-2\bar{h}\bigg)\,\tilde{\delta}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{2}s_{\mathrel{\mathop{\bar{k}_{i}}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.7pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\!\!\textbf{(\hskip 4.02498pt)}$}\vss}}}}+s_{\bar{h}}\bigg)\mathcal{N}\Big(2\bar{h}-2,s_{\bar{h}}+1,\langle\mu_{3}\lam\rangle\Big).\end{split}

(A.4)

Appendix B
From Shadow to Fourier

In this Appendix we complete the proof that a chiral Mellin transform of a Fourier transform gives the shadow transform. This requires us to compute the integral
\mathcal{I}
in (
5.11
) given by

\mathcal{I}:=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}\frac{d\bar{\sigma}}{\bar{\sigma}}\wedge\frac{d\sigma}{\sigma}\sigma^{2-2h}\bar{\sigma}^{2-2\bar{h}}e^{i(\sigma+\bar{\sigma})}\,.

(B.1)

Computing
\mathcal{I}
completes the proof of the commuting diagram Figure (
1
). However, as an additional check, we also prove the diagram in the reverse direction by inverse chiral Mellin transforming the shadowed conformal primary to recover a Fourier transformed momentum eigenstate.

Computing

\mathcal{I}

To compute
\mathcal{I}
we first change variables
\sigma=Re^{i\phi}

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}=&\;2i\int_{0}^{\infty}dR\,R^{3-2\Delta}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\phi\,\left(e^{i\phi}\right)^{-2J}e^{2iR\cos{\phi}}\\
=&\;2i\int_{0}^{\infty}dR\,R^{3-2\Delta}\mathcal{I}_{\phi}\,.\end{split}

(B.2)

We can compute the angular integral as a contour integral using
z=e^{i\phi}
, giving

\mathcal{I}_{\phi}=-i\oint\frac{dz}{z}\,z^{-2J}e^{iR(z+z^{-1})}=-i\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(iR)^{n}}{n!}\oint\frac{dz}{z}\,z^{-2J}\left(z+z^{-1}\right)^{n}\,.

(B.3)

This is also a modified Bessel function of the first kind. Now we use the binomial theorem and find that the contour integal has a residue contribution of
2\pi i
at
z=0
only when
n+2J
is even and
n-|2J|\geq 0
and for the single term in the binomial sum with
k=J+\frac{n}{2}\geq 0
. The integral is then given by the sum

\mathcal{I}_{\phi}=2\pi\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geq\lvert 2J\lvert\\
n+2J\,\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\!\!\frac{(iR)^{n}}{(\frac{n}{2}+J)!(\frac{n}{2}-J)!}=2\pi i^{2\absolutevalue{J}}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{l}\frac{R^{2l+2\absolutevalue{J}}}{l!(l+2\absolutevalue{J})!}\,,

(B.4)

which we have rewritten with
l:=\frac{n}{2}-\absolutevalue{J}
. This sum corresponds to a generalised hypergeometric function, since the ratio of any two consecutive terms is a rational function in
l
. Labelling the terms in the above sum by
a_{l}
we have

\frac{a_{l+1}}{a_{l}}=\frac{-R^{2}}{(l+1)(l+2\absolutevalue{J}+1)},\quad a_{0}=2\pi i^{2\absolutevalue{J}}R^{2\absolutevalue{J}},

(B.5)

hence the angular integral is given by

\mathcal{I}_{\phi}=2\pi i^{2\absolutevalue{J}}R^{2\absolutevalue{J}}\,{}_{0}F_{1}(\_;2\absolutevalue{J}+1;-R^{2})\,.

(B.6)

The sum (
B.4
) converges for all finite values of
R^{2}
by the ratio test.

To evaluate the
R
integral we will find it easier to work with the explicit sum form of
\mathcal{I}_{\phi}

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}=4\pi i\,i^{2\absolutevalue{J}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dRR^{3-2\Delta}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{l}\frac{R^{2l+2\absolutevalue{J}}}{l!(l+2\absolutevalue{J})!}\,.\end{split}

(B.7)

We now regulate the
R
integral using
e^{-\delta R}
and then take
\delta\rightarrow 0^{+}
20
20

20

Ramanujan’s master theorem also gives the same answer
[
76
]
.
. We also commute the infinite sum with the integral since both now converge

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}=&\;4\pi i\,i^{2\absolutevalue{J}}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{l}}{l!(l+2\absolutevalue{J})!}\int_{0}^{\infty}R^{3-2\Delta+2l+2\absolutevalue{J}}e^{-\delta R}\\
=&\;4\pi i\,i^{2\absolutevalue{J}}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{l}}{l!(l+2\absolutevalue{J})!}\frac{\Gamma(4-2\Delta+2l+2\absolutevalue{J})}{\delta^{4-2\Delta+2l+2\absolutevalue{J}}}\,.\end{split}

(B.8)

Once again this sum corresponds to a hypergeometric function: labelling the terms in the sum by
b_{l}
we have

\frac{b_{l+1}}{b_{l}}=\frac{(5-2\Delta+2l+2\absolutevalue{J})(4-2\Delta+2l+2\absolutevalue{J})}{(l+1)(l+2\absolutevalue{J}+1)}\frac{-1}{\delta^{2}},\quad b_{0}=\frac{4\pi i\,i^{2\absolutevalue{J}}\Gamma(4-2\Delta+2\absolutevalue{J})}{(2\absolutevalue{J})!\delta^{4-2\Delta+2\absolutevalue{J}}}\,.

(B.9)

Thus the integral
\mathcal{I}
is given by a type
{}_{2}F_{1}
hypergeometric function. The sum converges for
\absolutevalue{\frac{-4}{\delta^2}}<1/2
and is on the principal branch

\mathcal{I}=\frac{4\pi i\,i^{2\absolutevalue{J}}\Gamma(4-2\Delta+2\absolutevalue{J})}{(2\absolutevalue{J})!\delta^{4-2\Delta+2\absolutevalue{J}}}\;{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\frac{5}{2} -\Delta+\abs{J},\,\, 2-\Delta+ \abs{J}}{2\abs{J}+1};\frac{-4}{\delta^{2}}\right).

(B.10)

In order to tame this expression we must use a series of transformations and identities. The first of these is a Pfaff transformation,

{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a,b}{c};z\right)=(1-z)^{-b}{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{b,c-a}{c};\frac{z}{z-1}\right)\,,

(B.11)

and applying this to (
B.10
) gives

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}=\frac{4\pi i\,i^{2\absolutevalue{J}}\Gamma(4-2\Delta+2\absolutevalue{J})}{(2\absolutevalue{J})!(\delta+2)^{4-2\Delta+2\absolutevalue{J}}}{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{2-\Delta+ \abs{J},\,\, -\frac{3}{2}+ \Delta+\abs{J}}{2\abs{J}+1};\frac{1}{1+\frac{\delta^{2}}{4}}\right)\,.\end{split}

(B.12)

At this point we are safe to take the limit
\delta\rightarrow 0
and we obtain

\mathcal{I}=4\pi i\,\frac{i^{\absolutevalue{J}}}{(2\absolutevalue{J})!}2^{2\Delta-2\absolutevalue{J}-4}\Gamma(4-2\Delta+2\absolutevalue{J}){}_{2}F_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{2-\Delta+ \abs{J},\,\, -\frac{3}{2}+ \Delta+\abs{J}}{2\abs{J}+1};1\right)\,.

(B.13)

We can now evaluate the
{}_{2}F_{1}
at the point
1
in terms of objects we are very familiar with: gamma functions

{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a,b}{c};1\right)=\frac{\Gamma(c)\Gamma(c-a-b)}{\Gamma(c-a)\Gamma(c-b)}\,,

(B.14)

which holds if
\real(c-a-b)>0
. This condition is satisfied for us since
c-a-b=1/2
, hence we can write

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}=&\;4\pi i\,\frac{i^{2\absolutevalue{J}}}{(2\absolutevalue{J})!}2^{2\Delta-2\absolutevalue{J}-4}\Gamma(4-2\Delta+2\absolutevalue{J})\frac{\Gamma(2\absolutevalue{J}+1)\Gamma(\half)}{\Gamma(-1+\Delta+\absolutevalue{J})\Gamma(\frac{5}{2}+\absolutevalue{J}-\Delta)}\\
=&\;4\pi i\,i^{2\absolutevalue{J}}2^{2\Delta-2\absolutevalue{J}-4}\frac{\Gamma(4-2\Delta+2\absolutevalue{J})\sqrt{\pi}}{\Gamma(-1+\Delta+\absolutevalue{J})\Gamma(\frac{5}{2}+\absolutevalue{J}-\Delta)}\,.\end{split}

(B.15)

Further, we can simplify this expression using the Legendre duplication formula
[
77
, Eq. 5.5(iii)]

\sqrt{\pi}\,2^{1-2z}\Gamma(2z)=\Gamma(z)\Gamma(z+\half),

(B.16)

and the Euler reflection formula

\Gamma(z)\Gamma(1-z)=\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi z)}.

(B.17)

Using these we arrive at

\mathcal{I}=2\pi i\,i^{2\absolutevalue{J}}\frac{\Gamma(2-\Delta+\absolutevalue{J})}{\Gamma(-1+\Delta+\absolutevalue{J})}=2\pi i\,i^{2J}\frac{\Gamma(2-2\bar{h})}{\Gamma(2h-1)}=2\pi i\,i^{-2J}\frac{\Gamma(2-2h)}{\Gamma(2\bar{h}-1)}.

(B.18)

Each of the three expressions above is equivalent and we are free to use whichever we prefer. We then use this result in the main text to recover the shadow transform.

From Shadow to Fourier

We begin with our expression for the shadow transformed state (
4.5
) which we will rewrite in terms of
k=1-h
and
\bar{h}=1-\bar{h}

\begin{split}|\mu,\tilde{\mu};k,\bar{k}\rangle=\frac{i^{-2k+2\bar{k}}\Gamma(2\bar{k})}{4\pi^{2}\Gamma(1-2k)}\int_{\mathbb{C}_{*}^{2}}d^{2}\lambda\wedge d^{2}\!\lamt\,\langle\lambda\mu\rangle^{-2k}[\tilde{\mu}\lamt]^{-2\bar{k}}|\lam,\lamt\rangle\,,\end{split}

(B.19)

and perform an inverse chiral Mellin transform (
G.3
) to recover a Fourier transformed state. We can choose to integrate with respect to
\Delta^{\prime}=k+\bar{k}
and
J^{\prime}=k-\bar{k}
since this is just a choice of integration variables. We then have

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2\pi i}\sum_{2J^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{a-i\infty}^{a+i\infty}d\Delta^{\prime}\,|\mu,\tilde{\mu};k,\bar{k}\rangle\\
=&\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{C}_{*}^{2}}\!d^{2}\lambda\wedge d^{2}\!\lamt\,\sum_{2J^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}i^{-2J^{\prime}}(-1)^{|J^{\prime}|+J^{\prime}}\left(\frac{[\tilde{\mu}\lamt]}{\langle\lam\mu\rangle}\right)^{J^{\prime}}\int_{a-i\infty}^{a+i\infty}d\Delta^{\prime}\,\frac{\Gamma(\Delta^{\prime}+|J^{\prime}|)(\langle\lam\mu\rangle[\tilde{\mu}\lamt])^{-\Delta}}{2\pi i\,\Gamma(1-\Delta^{\prime}+|J^{\prime}|)}|\lam,\lamt\rangle,\end{split}

(B.20)

where we have used the form of (
B.18
) written in terms of
\Delta
and
J
. The integrand has poles at
\Delta^{\prime}=-|J^{\prime}|-n
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
due to the gamma function
\Gamma(\Delta^{\prime}+|J^{\prime}|)
. Thus the integral over
\Delta
can be performed by choosing the contour with
a>-|J|
closed anticlockwise. Hence we have

\begin{split}=&\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{\CC_{*}^{2}}\!d^{2}\lambda\wedge d^{2}\!\lamt\,\sum_{2J^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}i^{-2J^{\prime}}(-1)^{|J^{\prime}|+J^{\prime}}\left(\frac{[\tilde{\mu}\lamt]}{\langle\lam\mu\rangle}\right)^{J^{\prime}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\,\frac{(-1)^{n}(\langle\lam\mu\rangle[\tilde{\mu}\lamt])^{|J^{\prime}|+n}}{n!\Gamma(1+2|J^{\prime}|+n)}\,|\lam,\lamt\rangle\\
=&\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{\CC_{*}^{2}}\!d^{2}\lambda\wedge d^{2}\!\lamt\,\sum_{2J^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}(i[\tilde{\mu}\lamt])^{|J^{\prime}|+J^{\prime}}(i\langle\lam\mu\rangle)^{|J^{\prime}|-J^{\prime}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\,\frac{((i\langle\lam\mu\rangle)(i[\tilde{\mu}\lamt]))^{n}}{n!(2|J^{\prime}|+n)!}\,|\lam,\lamt\rangle\,.\end{split}

(B.21)

These two sums look slightly unwieldy but we can massage them in to something more familiar as follows

\begin{split}=&\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{\CC_{*}^{2}}\!d^{2}\lambda\wedge d^{2}\!\lamt\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{((i\langle\lam\mu\rangle)(i[\tilde{\mu}\lamt]))^{n}}{n!}\left(\frac{1}{n!}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{(i[\tilde{\mu}\lamt])^{m}}{(m+n)!}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{(i\langle\lam\mu\rangle)^{m}}{(m+n)!}\right)|\lam,\lamt\rangle\\
=&\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{\CC_{*}^{2}}\!d^{2}\lambda\wedge d^{2}\!\lamt\bigg(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(i\langle\lam\mu\rangle)^{n}}{n!}\frac{(i[\tilde{\mu}\lamt])^{n}}{n!}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{(i[\tilde{\mu}\lamt])^{m+n}}{(m+n)!}\frac{(i\langle\lam\mu\rangle)^{n}}{n!}+\frac{(i[\tilde{\mu}\lamt])^{n}}{n!}\frac{(i\langle\lam\mu\rangle)^{m+n}}{(m+n)!}\right)\bigg)|\lam,\lamt\rangle\\
=&\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{\CC_{*}^{2}}\!d^{2}\lambda\wedge d^{2}\!\lamt\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(i\langle\lam\mu\rangle)^{n-k}}{(n-k)!}\frac{(i[\tilde{\mu}\lamt])^{k}}{k!}\right)|\lam,\lamt\rangle\\
=&\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{\CC_{*}^{2}}\!d^{2}\lambda\wedge d^{2}\!\lamt e^{i\langle\lam\mu\rangle}e^{i[\tilde{\mu}\lamt]}|\lam,\lamt\rangle\end{split}

(B.22)

which is exactly the Fourier transformed state (
5.1
).

Appendix C
Bulk Conformal Generators

In this Appendix we provide formulae for the conformal generators of the four-dimensional bulk spacetime. We present these generators in a variety of bases: momentum space, Mellin space, Fourier and twistor space and finally shadow and light space. These different bases for the generators reflect the various integral transforms that we have at our disposal. The generators themselves are always defined in the same way - we simply commute them with the integral transform which defines the basis and we always use
\sim
to denote this relation. We also in general favor presenting the celestial generators in homogeneous coordinates since they are much more compact, nevertheless we also give some examples in terms of affine coordinates.

To check the generators satisfy the conformal algebra it is easiest to check that the spinor and spinor derivative from which we build generators satisfy the canonical commutation relations. The only non-vanishing commutator is

[\partial_{\alpha},\lambda_{\beta}]=\epsilon_{\beta\alpha}

(C.1)

and similarly for the conjugate spinor and its derivative.

The generators of the four-dimensional conformal algebra written in terms of spinors, and the commutator relations they satisfy are given in
[
78
]
. The form of these generators is the same in either Lorentzian or split signature with the only difference being the form of the metric
\eta^{\mu\nu}
in the structure constants of the algebra. This then informs the reality conditions on the spinors.

Mellin Space

The generators of the bulk conformal algebra in the Mellin basis in affine coordinates were presented in
[
79
]
and re-derived from the (1,3) signature chiral Mellin transform in
[
12
]
along with the
N=4
superconformal generators. In Table
1
we give the (1,3) celestial conformal generators in homogeneous coordinates; an example derivation of
\partial_{\alpha}e^{-\partial_{h}}
is given in the main text, equation (
3.16
).

Fourier Space

We define conformal generators in the Fourier basis by commuting them with the Fourier transform (
5.1
) using integration by parts. The basic building block operators have the following Fourier conjugates

\begin{split}&\mu_{\alpha}\sim i\partial_{\alpha},\,\mu^{\alpha}\sim-i\partial^{\alpha},\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{\alpha}}\sim-i\lambda_{\alpha},\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{\alpha}}\sim i\lambda^{\alpha},\\
&\tilde{\mu}_{\dot{\alpha}}\sim i\tilde{\partial}_{\dot{\alpha}},\,\,\tilde{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}}\sim-i\tilde{\partial}^{\dot{\alpha}},\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}}}\sim-i\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}},\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\mu}_{\dot{\alpha}}}\sim i\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}},\end{split}

(C.2)

such that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\sim-\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}-\mu_{\beta}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{\alpha}},\quad\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\tilde{\partial}_{\dot{\beta}}\sim-\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}-\tilde{\mu}_{\dot{\beta}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}}},

(C.3)

implying

\displaystyle\lambda^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\sim-2-\mu^{\alpha}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{\alpha}},\quad\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}\tilde{\partial}_{\dot{\alpha}}\sim-2-\tilde{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}}},

(C.4)

\displaystyle\lam_{(\alpha}\partial_{\beta)}\sim-\mu_{(\alpha}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{\beta)}},\quad\lamt_{(\dot{\alpha}}\partial_{\dot{\beta})}\sim-\tilde{\mu}_{(\dot{\alpha}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\mu}^{\dot{\beta})}},

(C.5)

from which the helicity and dilatation operator featuring in the main text, as well as all the other conformal generators in Table
1
, are derived.

In Table
1
we also define another set of operators
\cJ_{F},d_{F},p_{F},k_{F},m_{F}
which generate the conformal symmetries of
\mu,\tilde{\mu}
space directly and without reference to their origin as conjugate spinors to
\lam,\lamt
. We find that the original set are related to these by an automorphism of the conformal algebra given by

\begin{split}&d\mapsto-d,\quad\cJ\mapsto-\cJ,\quad p\mapsto-k,\\
&k\mapsto-p,\quad m\mapsto-m,\quad\bar{m}\mapsto-\bar{m}.\end{split}

(C.6)

One can check that this preserves the commutator relations of the conformal algebra. This automorphism of the conformal algebra is generated by conjugation with the discrete operator which generates a conformal inversion
I
in position space. For example, we have the well known description of special conformal transformations as
k_{\mu}=-I\,p_{\mu}I
. We can make this more concrete by considering the conformal symmetry generators in position space defined by the Fourier transform

\int d^{4}x\,e^{-ip\cdot x}

(C.7)

giving the conformal generators

\begin{split}p_{\mu}\sim-i\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}},\quad k_{\mu}\sim i\bigg(x^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}-x_{\mu}x^{\nu}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\nu}}\bigg),\\
d\sim-ix^{\mu}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}},\quad m_{\mu\nu}\sim i\bigg(x_{\mu}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\nu}}-x_{\nu}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}\bigg).\end{split}

(C.8)

We can then take these expression and consider their representation in a new copy of Minkowski space with coordinate
y^{\mu}
(with units of inverse length) and related to
x^{\mu}
by a conformal inversion
y^{\mu}=\frac{x^{\mu}}{x^{2}}
. Then, by changing variables in (
C.8
) we find exactly the relations (
C.6
). This shows that the special conformal dual
k_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=\mu_{\alpha}\tilde{\mu}_{\dot{\alpha}}
can be regarded as the ‘momentum’ associated to a conformally inverted position space. This situation is summarised in the diagram contained in Figure
5
.

Shadow Space

Since the shadow basis is simply given by a chiral Mellin transform of the Fourier basis, the shadowed bulk conformal generators in Table
1
are just celestial versions of the Fourier transformed generators. This can be checked using the formula for the shadowed celestial state (
4.5
) and the relations
k=1-h
and
\bar{k}=1-\bar{h}
. For the spinor derivative
\partial_{\alpha}
, using integration by parts we find

\begin{split}&\frac{i^{2h-2\bar{h}}\Gamma(2-2\bar{h})}{(2\pi)^{2}\Gamma(2h-1)}\int_{\mathbb{C}_{*}^{2}}d^{2}\lamt\wedge\,d^{2}\lam\,\langle\lam\mu\rangle^{2h-2}[\tilde{\mu}\lamt]^{2\bar{h}-2}\partial_{\alpha}\ket{\lam, \lamt},\lamt\\
=&\frac{i^{2h-2\bar{h}}\Gamma(2-2\bar{h})}{(2\pi)^{2}\Gamma(2h-1)}\int_{\mathbb{C}_{*}^{2}}d^{2}\lamt\wedge\,d^{2}\lam\,(-\mu_{\alpha}(2h-2))\langle\lam\mu\rangle^{2h-3}[\tilde{\mu}\lamt]^{2\bar{h}-2}\ket{\lam, \lamt},\lamt\\
=&\frac{i^{2h-2\bar{h}}\Gamma(2-2\bar{h})}{(2\pi)^{2}\Gamma(2h-1)}(-\mu_{\alpha}(2h-2)e^{-\partial_{h}/2})\int_{\mathbb{C}_{*}^{2}}d^{2}\lamt\wedge\,d^{2}\lam\,\langle\lam\mu\rangle^{2h-2}[\tilde{\mu}\lamt]^{2\bar{h}-2}\ket{\lam, \lamt},\lamt\\
=&-i\mu_{\alpha}e^{-\partial_{h}/2}\frac{i^{2h-2\bar{h}}\Gamma(2-2\bar{h})}{(2\pi)^{2}\Gamma(2h-1)}\int_{\mathbb{C}_{*}^{2}}d^{2}\lamt\wedge\,d^{2}\lam\,\langle\lam\mu\rangle^{2h-2}[\tilde{\mu}\lamt]^{2\bar{h}-2}\ket{\lam, \lamt},\lamt,\end{split}

(C.9)

so the shadowed spinor derivative is given by
\partial_{\alpha}\sim-i\mu_{\alpha}e^{-\partial_{h}/2}=-i\mu_{\alpha}e^{\partial_{k}/2}
.
