(2,2) Mellin

Conformal generators derived from the (2,2) signature chiral Mellin transform are only slightly different from those in (1,3) signature, since we have discrete weights
s_{h},s_{\bar{h}}
which are also carried by spinors and spinor derivatives. As such we must also have discrete weight shifting operators which shift
s_{h},s_{\bar{h}}
; by an abuse of notation we also denote these by exponentiated differential operators although, since
s_{h},s_{\bar{h}}
are discrete, they clearly they do not take such a form. Consequently, split signature celestial generators take the same form as their Lorentzian signature cousins but with the universal replacement
e^{\frac{1}{2}\partial_{h}}\rightarrow e^{\frac{1}{2}\partial_{h}}e^{\partial_{s_{h}}}
. The inclusion of the
e^{\partial_{s_{h}}}
operators has no effect on the closing of the conformal algebra but is needed to properly shift the (2,2) weights.

For example, consider the spinor derivative
\partial_{\alpha}
and act with the (2,2) chiral Mellin transform (
7.13
)

\int_{\RR_{*}\times\RR_{*}}\frac{d\tilde{u}}{\tilde{u}}\wedge\frac{du}{u}\,\absolutevalue{u}^{2h}\,\absolutevalue{\ut}^{2\bar{h}}\,\text{sgn}(u)^{s_{h}}\text{sgn}(\tilde{u})^{s_{\bar{h}}}\frac{1}{u}\partial_{\alpha}u\ket{u \lam,\ut\lamt},\tilde{u}\lamt=\partial_{\alpha}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{h}}e^{-\partial_{s_{h}}}\ket{\lam, \lamt;h, s_h, \hb, s_{\hb}},\lamt;h,s_{h},\bar{h},s_{\bar{h}}\,.

(C.10)

Hence the spinor derivative acting in Mellin space is simply
\partial_{\alpha}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{h}-\partial_{s_{h}}}
.

Compare this with the affine coordinate version

\begin{split}&\partial_{\alpha}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{h}}e^{-\partial_{s_{h}}}\ket{\ld{\alpha} , \ltd{\alpha};h, s_h, \hb, s_{\hb}}\\
=&\;\partial_{\alpha}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{h}}e^{-\partial_{s_{h}}}\absolutevalue{\ld{2}}^{-2h}\absolutevalue{\ltd{2}}^{-2\bar{h}}\text{sgn}(\lambda_{2})^{-s_{h}}\text{sgn}(\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{2}})^{-s_{\bar{h}}}\ket{\begin{pmatrix}
z \\
1
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}
\zb\\
1
\end{pmatrix};h, s_h, \hb, s_{\hb}}\\
=&\frac{1}{\lambda_{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{2}}-z\partial_{z}\\
-\partial_{z}\end{pmatrix}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{h}}e^{-\partial_{s_{h}}}\absolutevalue{\ld{2}}^{-2h}\absolutevalue{\ltd{2}}^{-2\bar{h}}\text{sgn}(\lambda_{2})^{-s_{h}}\text{sgn}(\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{2}})^{-s_{\bar{h}}}\ket{\begin{pmatrix}
z \\
1
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}
\zb\\
1
\end{pmatrix}; h, s_h, \hb, s_{\hb}}\\
=&\;\absolutevalue{\ld{2}}^{-2h}\absolutevalue{\ltd{2}}^{-2\bar{h}}\text{sgn}(\lambda_{2})^{-s_{h}}\text{sgn}(\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{2}})^{-s_{\bar{h}}}\begin{pmatrix}-2h+1-z\partial_{z}\\
-\partial_{z}\end{pmatrix}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{h}-\partial_{s_{h}}}\ket{\begin{pmatrix}
z \\
1
\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}
\zb\\
1
\end{pmatrix}; h, s_h, \hb, s_{\hb}}\,,\end{split}

(C.11)

where we changed variables
\{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\}\rightarrow\{z,\lambda_{2}\}
with
z=\lambda_{1}/\lambda_{2}=-\lambda^{2}/\lambda^{1}
and recovered an affine expression for the spinor derivative
\begin{pmatrix}-2h+1-z\partial_{z}\\
-\partial_{z}\end{pmatrix}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{h}}e^{-\partial_{s_{h}}}
. Note that the derivative
\lambda_{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{2}}
gave a factor
-2h+1
. In principle it also acted on the factors
\text{sgn}(\lambda_{2})^{-s_{h}}
but these give terms proportional to
\lambda_{2}\,\delta(\lambda_{2})
which vanish. In other words, unlike the continuous weights
h,\bar{h}
, the discrete weights cannot be measured with a differential operator
\lambda_{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{2}}
.

Twistor Space

The conformal generators in the twistor and dual twistor bases are listed in Table
2
and are found by using the replacements (
C.2
), but only for either the
\lam
or
\lamt
spinor and its derivative. In Table
2
we also define two additional sets of conformal generators:
\cJ_{T},d_{T},p_{T},k_{T},m_{T}
which gives the conformal symmetry generators which treat
\mu_{\alpha},\lamt_{\dot{\alpha}}
in a manner based purely on whether they transform under the left or right hand factor of
\slR\times\slR
. That is the
\cJ_{T},d_{T},p_{T},k_{T},m_{T}
are simply the original momentum space conformal generators but with the naïve replacement
\lam\rightarrow\mu
and similarly for the
\cJ_{\bar{T}},d_{\bar{T}},p_{\bar{T}},k_{\bar{T}},m_{\bar{T}}
for dual twistor space via
\lamt\rightarrow\tilde{\mu}
. These are distinct operators from the original set and are used as a comparison to study the action of the twistor and dual twistor transforms. We find that these new operators are related to each other by the automorphism (
C.6
) which can be understood as being generated from the full Fourier transformation from twistor to dual twistor space

\int d^{2}\mu\wedge d^{2}\lamt e^{i(\langle\lam\mu\rangle+[\tilde{\mu}\lamt])},

(C.12)

which has the same kernel as the Fourier transformation (
5.1
) but is integrated over an orthogonal region of the full phase space
\lam,\lamt,\mu,\tilde{\mu}
. This correspondence is summarised in the diagram in Figure
5
. Note that unlike the Fourier transform from
\lam,\lamt
to
\mu,\tilde{\mu}
space the measure of the transform (
C.12
) from twistor to dual twistor space does not carry any dilatation weight and so does not change the dilatation/mass dimension of the object it acts on. As discussed in Section
6
, this means the full twistor transform (
C.12
) does not correspond to an active conformal inversion. As explained in
[
23
,
80
,
81
]
, an active conformal inversion of the moduli coordinate associated to an amplitude supported on a set of twistor lines, or equivalently an active inversion of the dual coordinates, generates an inversion
within
twistor space which exchanges
\lambda_{\alpha}\leftrightarrow\tilde{\mu}_{\dot{\alpha}}
in our conventions.

Light Space

The light basis can be thought of as a celestial twistor basis and so the bulk conformal generators in the light basis in Table
2
are simply Mellin transformed versions of the twistor conformal generators. We can also derive them by directly commuting with the light transform given in say (
8.4
); for example for the spinor derivative we find

\begin{split}&\frac{i^{-s_{h}}}{2}\frac{\Gamma(2-2h)}{\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-h+\frac{s_{h}}{2})\Gamma(h-\frac{s_{h}}{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}d^{2}\lam\,\langle\absolutevalue{\agl{ \lam}{\mu}}\mu\rangle^{2h-2}\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\mu\rangle)^{s_{h}}\partial_{\alpha}|\lambda_{\alpha},\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\rangle\\
=&\frac{i^{-s_{h}}}{2}\frac{\Gamma(2-2h)}{\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-h+\frac{s_{h}}{2})\Gamma(h-\frac{s_{h}}{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}d^{2}\lam\,(-\mu_{\alpha}\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\mu\rangle)(2h-2))\langle\absolutevalue{\agl{ \lam}{\mu}}\mu\rangle^{2h-3}\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\mu\rangle)^{s_{h}}|\lambda_{\alpha},\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\rangle\\
=&\frac{i^{-s_{h}}}{2}\frac{\Gamma(2-2h)}{\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-h+\frac{s_{h}}{2})\Gamma(h-\frac{s_{h}}{2}-\frac{1}{2})}(-\mu_{\alpha}(2h-2)e^{-\frac{\partial_{h}}{2}-\partial_{s_{h}}})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}d^{2}\lam\,\langle\absolutevalue{\agl{ \lam}{\mu}}\mu\rangle^{2h-2}\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\mu\rangle)^{s_{h}}|\lambda_{\alpha},\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\rangle\\
=&(-i\mu_{\alpha}e^{-\frac{\partial_{h}}{2}-\partial_{s_{h}}})\frac{i^{-s_{h}}}{2}\frac{\Gamma(2-2h)}{\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-h+\frac{s_{h}}{2})\Gamma(h-\frac{s_{h}}{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}d^{2}\lam\,\langle\absolutevalue{\agl{ \lam}{\mu}}\mu\rangle^{2h-2}\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\mu\rangle)^{s_{h}}|\lambda_{\alpha},\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\rangle,\end{split}

(C.13)

where in the second line we used integration by parts and dropped the term where the derivative acts on the sgn function
\partial_{\alpha}\text{sgn}(\langle\lam\mu\rangle)\sim\delta(\langle\lam\mu\rangle)
, with vanishing contribution due to the delta function.

A ‘bulk diamond’ of relationships between twistor and Fourier transforms, including the action of conformal inversions on position space. A chiral Mellin transform maps this to a celestial Diamond a lá

.

Appendix D
Proofs of
S^{2}=Id
and
L^{2}=Id

Since
S^{2}
(and
L^{2}
) are entwining operators between the same irreducible representations then they must be proportional to the identity. In this appendix we prove directly that the shadow transform (
4.3
) and light transform (
8.3
) are actually
self-inverse
. This is already implied by their relationship with the self-inverse Fourier transform (
5.1
) and half-Fourier (
9.1
). Indeed the normalisation chosen such that
S
and
L
are self-inverse was exactly that which appeared when linking to the Fourier and half-Fourier transforms. Note, however, that the self-inverse property is not just due to our choice of normalisation. Take the shadow transform for example, since by the square of the shadow transform we mean
S^{2}:=S_{1-h,1-\bar{h}}\circ S_{h,\bar{h}}
, then normalising
S
by any factor at all can only change the normalisation of
S^{2}
by a function invariant under
h\rightarrow 1-h,\,\bar{h}\rightarrow 1-\bar{h}
. We find that such a function is enough to normalise
S^{2}=Id
and that this is a consequence of the structure of the shadow transform in two dimensions, in particular the use of the two point structure
(w-z)^{2h-2}(\bar{w}-\bar{z})^{2\bar{h}-2}
(inherited from the anti-symmetric spinor brackets) which picks up a factor of
(-1)^{-2J}
when we swap
w\leftrightarrow z
, as we shall now explain.

Self Inverse Shadow

We consider the square of the shadow transform in affine coordinates (
4.3
) acting on a function with weights
h,\bar{h}

\begin{split}S^{2}\{f(z,\bar{z};h,\bar{h})\}:=&\frac{i^{-2h+2\bar{h}}\Gamma(2\bar{h})}{2\pi i\,\Gamma(1-2h)}\int_{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}}d\bar{w}\wedge dw\,(z^{\prime}-w)^{-2h}(\bar{z}^{\prime}-\bar{w})^{-2\bar{h}}\\
&\times\frac{i^{2h-2\bar{h}}\Gamma(2-2\bar{h})}{2\pi i\,\Gamma(2h-1)}\int_{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}}d\bar{z}\wedge dz\,(w-z)^{2h-2}(\bar{w}-\bar{z})^{2\bar{h}-2}f(z,\bar{z};h,\bar{h})\,.\end{split}

(D.1)

So we are lead to compute the integral,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{I}:=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int d\bar{w}\wedge dw\,(y-w)^{-2h}(\bar{y}-\bar{w})^{-2\bar{h}}(w-z)^{2h-2}(\bar{w}-\bar{z})^{2\bar{h}-2}.\end{split}

(D.2)

A similar integral was studied in
[
84
]
denoted
I_{2}
and for
\sum\alpha_{i}=\sum\bar{\alpha}_{i}=2
gave the result
21
21

21

We have written the result of
[
84
]
using our complex measure
d\bar{w}\wedge dw=2i\,d(\text{Re}(w))d(\text{Im}(w))
which explains the additional normalisation by a factor
\frac{1}{2i}
. Similarly we write the result using a delta function of the complex variable
z^{\prime}-z
with an extra factor of
2i
.

I_{2}:=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int d\bar{w}\wedge dw\prod_{i=1,2}(w-w_{i})^{-\alpha_{i}}(\bar{w}-\bar{w}_{i})^{-\bar{\alpha}_{i}}=2\pi i\,(-1)^{\alpha_{1}-\bar{\alpha}_{1}}K\delta^{2}(z^{\prime}-z),

(D.3)

where we have defined

K:=\frac{\Gamma(1-\alpha_{1})\Gamma(1-\alpha_{2})}{\Gamma(\bar{\alpha}_{1})\Gamma(\bar{\alpha}_{2})}.

(D.4)

Solving the constraints on the
\alpha_{i}
with
\alpha_{1}=2h,\,\alpha_{2}=2-2h
and similarly for the barred variables, we then have an integral very close to
\mathcal{I}
, and
K
is such that it exactly cancels the normalisation factors in (
D.1
). It remains to relate
I_{2}
to our integral
\mathcal{I}
. The crucial difference between
\mathcal{I}
and
I_{2}
is that the ordering of the variables in the brackets in
\mathcal{I}
is flipped since they reflect the order in which the shadow transforms were applied. We write
\mathcal{I}
in a manner such that the branch cut structure is transparent

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{I}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int d\bar{w}\wedge dw\,(y-w)^{-2\bar{h}-2J}(\bar{y}-\bar{w})^{-2\bar{h}}(w-z)^{2\bar{h}+2J-2}(\bar{w}-\bar{z})^{2\bar{h}-2}\\
=&\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int d\bar{w}\wedge dw\,(y-w)^{-2J}(w-z)^{2J}(|y-w|^{2})^{-2\bar{h}}(|w-z|^{2})^{2\bar{h}-2}\\
=&(-1)^{2J}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int d\bar{w}\wedge dw\,(w-y)^{-2J}(w-z)^{2J}(|w-y|^{2})^{-2\bar{h}}(|w-z|^{2})^{2\bar{h}-2}=(-1)^{2J}I_{2},\end{split}

(D.5)

where we have extracted a factor of
(-1)^{2J}
and recovered an integral with an ordering of variables that matches
I_{2}
. Now, using the result (
D.3
) we conclude that
S^{2}=\text{Id}
. We would like to emphasise again that this result is specific to the shadow transform in two dimensions with the two point structure
(w-z)^{2h-2}(\bar{w}-\bar{z})^{2\bar{h}-2}

22
22

22

Other two point structures are available in
d
-dimensions, for example in
[
52
]
, whose corresponding shadow transform may not be self-inverse.
.

We can also show that
S^{2}=\text{Id}
in an independent and revealing way without the computation of the integral
\mathcal{I}
. We first note that the shadow transform in affine coordinates (
4.3
) is a convolution of a conformal primary with a kernel given by the two point structure
(w-z)^{2h-2}(\bar{w}-\bar{z})^{2\bar{h}-2}
. In particular, the kernel is translation invariant. This means that in Fourier space conjugate to
z
the shadow transform is not an integral transform at all, but a purely multiplicative operation. This insight is thanks to the authors of
[
51
]
. Here the Fourier transform we will use is a
two-dimensional
one and again we will employ the Fourier kernel which is better adapted to the two-dimensional complex nature of the CFT. For a function
f(z,\bar{z};h,\bar{h})
with weights
h,\bar{h}
on the celestial sphere we define the 2d Fourier transform

f(z,\bar{z};h,\bar{h}):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\CC}d\bar{s}\wedge ds\,\,e^{-i(sz+\bar{s}\bar{z})}\tilde{f}(s,\bar{s};h,\bar{h}).

(D.6)

Note that this is distinct from the Fourier transform we considered in Section
5
, since the above is only defined in the affine patch
\lam_{2}\neq 0
.

Now let us consider a shadow transform of the conformal primary
f(z,\bar{z};h,\bar{h})
as written in (
D.6
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}&S\{f(z,\bar{z};h,\bar{h})\}\\
=&\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{i^{2J}}{2\pi i}\frac{\Gamma(2-2\bar{h})}{\Gamma(2h-1)}\int_{\CC}d\bar{s}\wedge ds\int_{\CC}d\bar{z}\wedge dz\,\,(\bar{w}-\bar{z})^{2\bar{h}-2}(w-z)^{2h-2}e^{-i(sz+\bar{s}\bar{z})}\tilde{f}(s,\bar{s};h,\bar{h})\\
=&\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{i^{2J}}{2\pi i}\frac{\Gamma(2-2\bar{h})}{\Gamma(2h-1)}\int_{\CC}d\bar{s}\wedge ds\,\,e^{-i(sw+\bar{s}\bar{w})}s^{1-2h}\bar{s}^{1-2\bar{h}}\int_{\CC}d\bar{z}\wedge dz\,\,\bar{z}^{2\bar{h}-2}\,z^{2h-2}e^{i(z+\bar{z})}\tilde{f}(s,\bar{s};h,\bar{h}),\end{split}

(D.7)

where in the second line we have performed a simple change of variables, shifting
z
by
w
and re-scaling by
s
. We can now recycle our calculation of (
5.11
) to perform the integral over
z,\bar{z}
, noting the different exponents appearing in the case at hand

\displaystyle\begin{split}S\{f(z,\bar{z};h,\bar{h})\}&=\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{i^{2J}\Gamma(2-2\bar{h})i^{-2J}\Gamma(2\bar{h}-1)}{\Gamma(2h-1)\Gamma(2-2h)}\int_{\CC}d\bar{s}\wedge ds\,\,e^{-i(sw+\bar{s}\bar{w})}s^{1-2h}\bar{s}^{1-2\bar{h}}\tilde{f}(s,\bar{s};h,\bar{h})\\
&=\frac{(-1)^{2J}}{2\pi}\int_{\CC}d\bar{s}\wedge ds\,\,e^{-i(sw+\bar{s}\bar{w})}s^{1-2h}\bar{s}^{1-2\bar{h}}\tilde{f}(s,\bar{s};h,\bar{h}),\end{split}

(D.8)

so we see that in Fourier space the shadow transform acts in the simplest manner possible as multiplicative operation,

\tilde{f}(s,\bar{s};h,\bar{h})\overset{S}{\longmapsto}(-1)^{2J}s^{1-2h}\bar{s}^{1-2\bar{h}}\tilde{f}(s,\bar{s};h,\bar{h}).

(D.9)

The factors of
s,\bar{s}
appearing in (
D.9
) match the results of
[
51
]
up differences in the definition of the two point structure used in the shadow transform.

From (
D.9
) it is immediate that the shadow transform is self inverse. That is, by performing a shadow transform again with the weights given now by
1-h,1-\bar{h}
we will cancel the above multiplicative factors and so return the original function. We can check this explicitly by performing a second shadow transform on the function given by the final line of (
D.8
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{S}^{2}\{f(z,\bar{z};h,\bar{h})\}=(-1)^{2J}\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{i^{-2J}\Gamma(2\bar{h})}{\Gamma(1-2h)}\int_{\CC}d\bar{s}\wedge ds\int_{\CC}d\bar{w}\wedge dw\\
&\times(\bar{y}-\bar{w})^{-2\bar{h}}(y-w)^{-2h}e^{-i(sw+\bar{s}\bar{w})}s^{1-2h}\bar{s}^{1-2\bar{h}}\tilde{f}(s,\bar{s};h,\bar{h})\\
=&(-1)^{2J}\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{i^{-2J}\Gamma(2\bar{h})}{\Gamma(1-2h)}\int_{\CC}d\bar{s}\wedge ds\,\,e^{-i(sy+\bar{s}\bar{y})}\int_{\CC}d\bar{w}\wedge dw\,\,\bar{w}^{-2\bar{h}}\,w^{-2h}e^{i(w+\bar{w})}\tilde{f}(s,\bar{s};h,\bar{h}),\end{split}

(D.10)

where in the second line we have performed the same simple change of variables as before. Again we can now recycle our calculation of (
5.11
) to do the integrals over
w,\bar{w}
, noting the different exponents appearing,

\displaystyle\begin{split}S^{2}\{f(z,\bar{z};h,\bar{h})\}&=\frac{1}{2\pi}(-1)^{2J}\frac{i^{-2J}\Gamma(2\bar{h})i^{2J}\Gamma(1-2\bar{h})}{\Gamma(1-2h)\Gamma(2h)}\int_{\CC}d\bar{s}\wedge ds\,\,e^{-i(sy+\bar{s}\bar{y})}\tilde{f}(s,\bar{s};h,\bar{h})\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\CC}d\bar{s}\wedge ds\,\,e^{-i(sy+\bar{s}\bar{y})}\tilde{f}(s,\bar{s};h,\bar{h})=f(y,\bar{y};h,\bar{h}).\end{split}

(D.11)

Self Inverse Light

We now show that the light transform is also self inverse (the dual light transform case is completely analogous). The light transform acting in affine coordinates on a function with weights
h,s_{h},\bar{h},s_{\bar{h}}
on the celestial torus is given by

\begin{split}L\{f(z,\bar{z};h,s_{h},\bar{h},s_{\bar{h}})\}=&\;i^{-s_{h}}\frac{\Gamma(2-2h)}{\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-h+\frac{s_{h}}{2})\Gamma(h-\frac{s_{h}}{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\int_{\mathbb{RP}^{1}}dz\,\absolutevalue{w-z}^{2h-2}\text{sgn}(w-z)^{s_{h}}f(z,\bar{z};h,s_{h},\bar{h},s_{\bar{h}}),\end{split}

(D.12)

and so is also a convolution with translation invariant kernel
(w-z)^{2h-2}\text{sgn}(w-z)^{s_{h}}
. In Fourier space with coordinate
s
conjugate to
z
the function becomes

f(z,\bar{z};h,s_{h},\bar{h},s_{\bar{h}}):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\RR}ds\,e^{-isz}f(s,\bar{z};h,s_{h},\bar{h},s_{\bar{h}})

(D.13)

and the light transform acting on this gives after the usual change of variable

\begin{split}L\{f(z,\bar{z};h,s_{h},\bar{h},s_{\bar{h}})\}=&\;\kappa_{h,s_{h},\bar{h},s_{\bar{h}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\RR}ds\,\absolutevalue{s}^{1-2h}\text{sgn}(s)^{-s_{h}}e^{-isw}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\int_{\RR}dz\,\absolutevalue{z}^{2h-2}\text{sgn}(z)^{s_{h}}e^{iz}f(s,\bar{z};h,s_{h},\bar{h},s_{\bar{h}}),\end{split}

(D.14)

where
\kappa_{h,s_{h},\bar{h},s_{\bar{h}}}
is the normalisation pre factor in (
D.12
). The
z
integral we calculated already in (
9.6
), note however we have different exponents in the case at hand and so we find

\begin{split}L\{f(z,\bar{z};h,s_{h},\bar{h},s_{\bar{h}})\}=&\;i^{-s_{h}}\frac{\Gamma(2-2h)}{\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-h+\frac{s_{h}}{2})\Gamma(h-\frac{s_{h}}{2}-\frac{1}{2})}2\pi i^{s_{h}}\frac{\Gamma(2h-1)}{\Gamma(h-\frac{s_{h}}{2})\Gamma(1-h+\frac{s_{h}}{2})}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\RR}ds\,\absolutevalue{s}^{1-2h}\text{sgn}(s)^{-s_{h}}e^{-isw}f(s,\bar{z};h,s_{h},\bar{h},s_{\bar{h}}).\end{split}

(D.15)

Now, using the Euler reflection formula and various trigonometric identities, the prefactor is given by

\begin{split}&2\pi\frac{\Gamma(2-2h)}{\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-h+\frac{s_{h}}{2})\Gamma(h-\frac{s_{h}}{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\frac{\Gamma(2h-1)}{\Gamma(h-\frac{s_{h}}{2})\Gamma(1-h+\frac{s_{h}}{2})}\\
=&\;2\frac{\sin((h-\frac{s_h}{2}-\frac{1}{2})\pi)\sin((h-\frac{s_h}{2})\pi)}{\sin((2h-1)\pi)}=-\frac{2\,\cos((h-\frac{s_h}{2})\pi)\sin((h-\frac{s_h}{2})\pi)}{\sin((2h-1)\pi)}\\
=&-\frac{\sin(2(h-\frac{s_h}{2})\pi)}{\sin((2h-1)\pi)}=(-1)^{s_{h}}\end{split}

(D.16)

and so in Fourier space with coordinate
s
the light transform acts multiplicatively via

\tilde{f}(s,\bar{z};h,s_{h},\bar{h},s_{\bar{h}})\overset{L}{\longmapsto}(-1)^{s_{h}}\absolutevalue{s}^{1-2h}\text{sgn}(s)^{s_{h}}\tilde{f}(s,\bar{z};h,s_{h},\bar{h},s_{\bar{h}}),

(D.17)

where we would like to highlight the similarity of the form of this action with that of the shadow in (
D.9
). Now if we act with a second light transform, with shifted weights
h\rightarrow 1-h
in its definition, we cancel the above factors and recover the original state.

Appendix E
All Leg Fourier Gluon Amplitudes in Split Signature

In this appendix, we perform two half-Fourier transforms on every leg of a tree-level momentum space gluon amplitude. This is the split signature analogue of the all-leg Fourier transform considered in
\phi^{4}
theory in Section
6
. We will perform these transformations in two steps by first performing ambidextrous half-Fourier transforms on each leg of the amplitude according to its helicity and then performing the remaining set of half-Fourier transforms. We will find that the amplitude in the Fourier space
\mu,\tilde{\mu}
takes an identical form. We begin with the
n
-point
N^{m}MHV
tree-level amplitude in split signature expressed using the CHY formula
[
85
,
86
,
87
]

A_{n}=\int\prod_{a=1}^{n}\frac{d^{2}\sigma_{a}}{(a\,a+1)}\frac{1}{GL_{2}}\prod_{I=1}^{m}\delta^{2}\bigg(\lamt_{I}-\sum_{i^{\prime}=m+1}^{n}\frac{\lamt_{i^{\prime}}}{(I\,i^{\prime})}\bigg)\,\prod_{i=m+1}^{n}\delta^{2}\bigg(\lam_{i}-\sum_{I^{\prime}=1}^{m}\frac{\lam_{I^{\prime}}}{(i\,I^{\prime})}\bigg)

(E.1)

where legs
I=1,...,m
have negative helicity and legs
i=m+1,...,n
have positive helicity and
(ab):=\sigma_{a}-\sigma_{b}
. We can write the CHY formula as

\begin{split}A_{n}=&\int\prod_{a=1}^{n}\frac{d^{2}\sigma_{a}}{(a\,a+1)}\frac{1}{GL_{2}}\prod_{I=1}^{m}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int d^{2}\tilde{\chi}_{I}\exp[i\tilde{\chi}_{I}\bigg(\lamt_{I}-\sum_{i^{\prime}=m+1}^{n}\frac{\lamt_{i^{\prime}}}{(I\,i^{\prime})}\bigg)\bigg]\\
&\qquad\times\prod_{i=m+1}^{n}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int d^{2}\chi_{i}\exp\langle i\bigg(\lam_{i}-\sum_{I^{\prime}=1}^{m}\frac{\lam_{I^{\prime}}}{(i\,I^{\prime})}\bigg)\chi_{i}\bigg\rangle.\end{split}

(E.2)

and then perform ambidextrous half-Fourier transforms over the coordinates
\lamt_{I}
for
I=1,...,m
and
\lam_{i}
for
i=m+1,...,n
. The half-Fourier integrals can be done trivially and we find

\begin{split}T\{A_{n}\}=&\int\prod_{a=1}^{n}\frac{d^{2}\sigma_{a}}{(a\,a+1)}\frac{1}{GL_{2}}\prod_{I=1}^{m}\frac{1}{2\pi}i\,\bigg[\tilde{\mu}_{I}\!\sum_{i^{\prime}=m+1}^{n}\frac{\exp\bigg(i\,\bigg[\mut_I\!\sum_{i'=m+1}^n \frac{\lamt_{i'}}{(I\,i')}\bigg]\bigg)_{i^{\prime}}}{(I\,i^{\prime})}\bigg]\bigg.\\
&\qquad\times\prod_{i=m+1}^{n}\frac{1}{2\pi}i\bigg\langle\sum_{I^{\prime}=1}^{m}\frac{\exp\bigg(i\bigg\langle\sum_{I'=1}^m \frac{\lam_{I'}}{(i\,I')}\mu_i\bigg\rangle\bigg)_{I^{\prime}}}{(i\,I^{\prime})}\mu_{i}\bigg\rangle\bigg..\end{split}

(E.3)

and so the CHY formula in an ambidextrous twistor basis consists of just exponentials with no delta function support. We can now ambidextrous half-Mellin transform this expression and we recover the
n
-point formula for ambidextrous light transformed celestial amplitudes found in
[
42
]
. This is further demonstration of the commuting diagram Figure
3
.

To find the all-leg Fourier amplitude in
\mu,\tilde{\mu}
space we need to perform the complementary half-Fourier transforms over the variables
\lam_{I}^{\prime}
for
I=1^{\prime},...,m
and
\lamt_{i}^{\prime}
for
i^{\prime}=m+1,...,n
. We first re-write the above formula by noting that we can reorganise the exponentials using the trivial relation

\sum_{I=1}^{m}\bigg[\tilde{\mu}_{I}\sum_{i^{\prime}=m+1}^{n}\frac{\lamt_{i^{\prime}}}{(I\,i^{\prime})}\bigg]=\sum_{i^{\prime}=m+1}^{n}\bigg[\sum_{I=1}^{m}\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{I}}{(I\,i^{\prime})}\lamt_{i^{\prime}}\bigg]\

(E.4)

such that

\begin{split}T\{A_{n}\}=&\int\prod_{a=1}^{n}\frac{d^{2}\sigma_{a}}{(a\,a+1)}\frac{1}{GL_{2}}\prod_{i^{\prime}=m+1}^{n}\frac{1}{2\pi}i\bigg[\sum_{I=1}^{m}\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{I}}{(I\,i^{\prime})}\exp\bigg(i\bigg[\sum_{I=1}^{m} \frac{\mut_I}{(I\,i')}\lamt_{i'}\bigg]\bigg)_{i^{\prime}}\bigg]\bigg.\\
&\qquad\times\prod_{I^{\prime}=1}^{m}\frac{1}{2\pi}i\bigg\langle\exp\bigg(i\bigg\langle\lam_{I'}\sum_{i=m+1}^{n} \frac{\mu_i}{(i\,I')}\bigg\rangle\bigg)_{I^{\prime}}\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}\frac{\mu_{i}}{(i\,I^{\prime})}\bigg\rangle\bigg.,\end{split}

(E.5)

from which we can half-Fourier transform the remaining
\lam,\lamt
variables to give

\begin{split}F\{A_{n}\}=&\int\prod_{a=1}^{n}\frac{d^{2}\sigma_{a}}{(a\,a+1)}\frac{1}{GL_{2}}\prod_{i^{\prime}=m+1}^{n}\delta^{2}\bigg(\tilde{\mu}_{i^{\prime}}-i\sum_{I=1}^{m}\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{I}}{(i^{\prime}\,I)}\bigg)\\
&\qquad\times\prod_{I^{\prime}=1}^{m}\delta^{2}\bigg(\mu_{I^{\prime}}-i\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}\frac{\mu_{i}}{(I^{\prime}\,i)}\bigg),\end{split}

(E.6)

where we have used the antisymmetry of the bracket
(\cdot,\cdot)
. We conclude that for all tree-level gluon amplitudes in (2,2) signature the Fourier transform acting on all legs leaves the structure of the amplitude invariant, and simply replaces
\lam\rightarrow\mu,\lamt\rightarrow\tilde{\mu}
. Using the commuting diagram Figure
1
, the same conclusion applies to the all leg shadow transformed amplitudes in (2,2) signature - they take the same form as the usual Mellin space celestial amplitudes. Whether or not this result can be extended to gluon amplitudes in Lorentzian signature is not clear. From our own initial investigations, performing Fourier transforms directly on gluon amplitudes in (1,3) signature results in different integrals, due to the different integration measure for complex spinors, and these require careful regularisation.
In addition, it is not obvious how one would “analytically continue” the above result to (1,3) signature. We leave the further study of these questions to later work.

Appendix F
Complexified Spacetime

In this appendix we provide some more motivation for the form of the Lorentzian and split signature transforms we have encountered in the main text, highlighting their differences and similarities. We do this by exploring a conceptually important technology that is often used in the study of scattering amplitudes, namely that of complexifying spacetime. This provides a unified view of spacetime of any signature - we can then restrict to a slice of complexified spacetime in order to hone in on (1,3) or (2,2) signature spacetimes. In what follows we present a condensed version of the general methodology we have already applied to Lorentzian and split signature.

In complexified spacetime we have independent complex spinors
\lambda_{\alpha},\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}
such that the momentum
p_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=\lambda_{\alpha}\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}
is complex. The little group of a complex null momenta is
\CC_{*}=U(1)\times\RR_{+}
and representations are labelled by two helicities:

1.

For the compact
U(1)
subgroup
c=e^{i\theta}
we have a discrete label
J_{1,3}\in\mathbb{Z}/2
corresponding to the little group and helicity in (1,3) signature.

2.

While for the non-compact
\RR_{+}
subgroup where
c
is real we have a continuous imaginary label
J_{2,2}\in i\RR
corresponding to the little group and helicity in (2,2) signature.

So we write asymptotic particle states in complexified spacetime as

\ket{\lam, \lamt; J_{1,3}, J_{2,2}},\lamt;J_{1,3},J_{2,2}\,,

(F.1)

and they transform under the complexified little group for
c=\absolutevalue{c}e^{i\theta}
as

\boxed{c\,\ket{c\,\lam, c^{-1}\lamt; J_{1,3}, J_{2,2}},c^{-1}\lamt;J_{1,3},J_{2,2}=(e^{i\theta})^{-2\,J_{1,3}}\absolutevalue{c}^{-2\,J_{2,2}}\,\ket{\,\lam, \lamt; J_{1,3}, J_{2,2}},\lamt;J_{1,3},J_{2,2}\,.}

(F.2)

The complexified Lorentz group is
\slC\times\slC
and the transformation of these states under this group is encoded in the spinors through

\ket{\ld{\alpha} , \ltd{\alpha};J_{1,3}, J_{2,2}}\rightarrow\ket{M_\alpha^{\,\,\beta}\ld{\beta} , \tilde{M}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{\,\,\dot{\beta}}\ltd{\beta}; J_{1,3}, J_{2,2} }\,,

(F.3)

where
M_{\alpha}^{\,\,\beta}
and
\tilde{M}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{\,\,\dot{\beta}}
are independent
\slC
matrices.

To restrict to
(1,3)
signature we make the slice (
3.1
) and this cuts the little group down to
c\in U(1)
with states labelled by just
J_{1,3}
.

For split signature something more subtle happens, we enforce the reality condition (
7.1
) and this time the
(2,2)
restriction of the little group is
\RR_{*}=\RR_{+}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
since we require that
c
in (
F.2
) be real. The
\RR_{+}
representation of the complexified little group survives and is still labelled by
J_{2,2}
but now
U(1)
degenerates into
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. This is the origin of the discrete helicity
s_{J}\in{0,1}
labelling even/odd representations of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
in split signature. The discrete helicity
s_{J}
is directly related to
J_{1,3}
by
s_{J}:=2\,J_{1,3}\mod 2
) and so naturally corresponds to bosons and fermions in (2,2).

Returning now to complex momentum, we can also consider extending the complexified little group to include a complex boost symmetry. In complexified momentum space these boosts scale the momentum by a non-zero complex number,
b\in\CC_{*}

p_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\rightarrow b\,p_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\,.

(F.4)

Hence we see that the extended little group of a complex null direction is
\mathbb{C}_{*}\times\mathbb{C}_{*}
, and corresponds to rescaling each spinor independently by any non-zero complex numbers
y,\tilde{y}

\lamt\rightarrow y\lamt,\quad\lamt\rightarrow\tilde{y}\lamt\,.

(F.5)

This leads to asymptotic states defined on a complexified celestial torus
\mathbb{CP}^{1}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1}
, also discussed in
[
88
]
. Such states are covariant under the extended little group
\CC_{*}\times\CC_{*}
and are labelled by its representations. As well as the two helicities
J_{1,3},J_{2,2}
we have two conformal dimensions associated to the boost transformation by
b=\absolutevalue{b}e^{i\phi}

1.

For the non-compact
\RR_{+}
subgroup we have a continuous parameter
\Delta\in 1+i\RR
which is the usual conformal dimension in both
(1,3)
and
(2,2)
signature,

2.

While for the compact
U(1)
subgroup we have a discrete parameter
\Delta_{c}\in\mathbb{Z}/2
. Note this is only present because the momenta is complex but as one might expect it descends to the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry in (2,2) signature which flips the sign of the momentum.

The splitting of the extended little group into rotations and boosts is conceptually very clear, however we can obtain more compact formulae if we combine these transformations into the complex rescalings (
F.5
). We define new weights as follows

\begin{split}H&:=\frac{1}{4}\big(\Delta+\Delta_{c}+J_{1,3}+J_{2,2}\big),\\
\tilde{H}&:=\frac{1}{4}\big(\Delta-\Delta_{c}+J_{1,3}-J_{2,2}\big),\\
\bar{H}&:=\frac{1}{4}\big(\Delta+\Delta_{c}-(J_{1,3}+J_{2,2})\big),\\
\bar{\tilde{H}}&:=\frac{1}{4}\big(\Delta-\Delta_{c}-(J_{1,3}-J_{2,2})\big).\end{split}

(F.6)

and write celestial states as

|\lam,\lamt;\mathbf{H}\rangle.

(F.7)

Now under the extended little group, for any
(y,\tilde{y})\in\mathbb{C}_{*}\times\mathbb{C}_{*}
we have the homogeneity property:

\boxed{|y\lam,\tilde{y}\lamt;\mathbf{H}\rangle=y^{-2H}(y^{*})^{-2\bar{H}}\tilde{y}^{-2\tilde{H}}(\tilde{y}^{*})^{-2\bar{\tilde{H}}}|\lam,\lamt;\mathbf{H}\rangle\,,}

(F.8)

where
\mathbf{H}
is shorthand for the four weights defined in (
F.6
) and are labels for representations of the little group
\mathbb{C}_{*}\times\mathbb{C}_{*}
which acts independently on the
\lambda
spinor and the ‘conjugate’ spinor
\tilde{\lambda}
. These celestial states transform as conformal primaries under
\slC\times\slC
.

The slices to Lorentzian signature require that
\tilde{y}=\bar{y}
and so we recover the extended little group
\CC_{*}
and homogeneity law (
3.10
). While in (2,2) we require that both
y,\tilde{y}
are real and so we recover the homogeneity property (
7.10
) for the extended little group
\RR_{*}\times\RR_{*}
and states are labelled by weights
h,\bar{h},s_{h},s_{\bar{h}}
.

We can go further and apply the general logic set out in the prelude (Section
2
) to define a complexified chiral Mellin transform which integrates over the complex scales of the independent spinors with weights
\mathbf{H}
. Similarly we can define a shadow transform exactly analogous to (
4.4
) and which acts on the spinor
\lam
, as well as an independent dual shadow which acts on
\lamt
. Each of these is then immediately related by the commuting diagram
1
to an independent bulk Fourier transform over either
\lam
and
\lamt
. An appropriate slicing procedure to (1,3) and (2,2) signature would then give the transforms that we have already met in the main text, for example a real (2,2) slice of the dual shadow would give the dual light transform. We leave the further exploration of these ideas to future work.

Appendix G
Inverse Chiral Mellin Transform in Homogeneous Coordinates

In this appendix we consider the (1,3) chiral Mellin transform in (
3.14
) and find its inverse. This can be written in terms of Fourier transforms by changing variables as
u=e^{\frac{x}{2}}e^{i\theta}
. This gives

\begin{split}&|\lambda,\tilde{\lambda},h,\bar{h}\rangle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\theta\,\left(e^{i\theta}\right)^{2(h-\bar{h})}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dx\,e^{x\Delta}|e^{\frac{x}{2}}e^{i\theta}\lambda,e^{\frac{x}{2}}e^{-i\theta}\tilde{\lambda}\rangle\\
=&\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\theta\,\left(e^{i\theta}\right)^{2(h-\bar{h})}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dx\,e^{ix\beta}\left(e^{xa}|e^{\frac{x}{2}}e^{i\theta}\lambda,e^{\frac{x}{2}}e^{-i\theta}\tilde{\lambda}\rangle\right)\,,\end{split}

(G.1)

where in the last line we used
\Delta=a+i\beta
. This is just the composition of two Fourier transforms acting on
e^{xa}|e^{\frac{x}{2}}e^{i\theta}\lambda,e^{\frac{x}{2}}e^{-i\theta}\tilde{\lambda}\rangle
- a continuous/non-compact one in
x
and a discrete/compact one in
\theta
. The compact Fourier transform over a circle always converges, however the non-compact Fourier transform converges only as long as the function
e^{xa}|e^{\frac{x}{2}}e^{i\theta}\lambda,e^{\frac{x}{2}}e^{-i\theta}\tilde{\lambda}\rangle
dies off sufficiently fast for large values of
x
. This defines a strip of definition for allowed values of
a
for the Mellin transform of a state.

We can now invert (
G.1
) using the standard inversion theorems for Fourier transforms to find

e^{ra}|e^{\frac{r}{2}}e^{i\theta}\lambda,e^{\frac{r}{2}}e^{-i\theta}\tilde{\lambda}\rangle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{2(h-\bar{h})\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(e^{-i\theta}\right)^{2(h-\bar{h})}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}db\,e^{-ir\beta}|\lambda,\tilde{\lambda},h,\bar{h}\rangle.

(G.2)

Thus, setting
r=0
,
\theta=0
we recover our original function

|\lambda,\tilde{\lambda}\rangle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{2(h-\bar{h})\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}db\,|\lambda,\tilde{\lambda},h,\bar{h}\rangle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\sum_{2(h-\bar{h})\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{a-i\infty}^{a+i\infty}d\Delta\,|\lambda,\tilde{\lambda},h,\bar{h}\rangle.

(G.3)

Equation (
G.3
) is the inversion formula in homogeneous coordinates. This formula, as expected, follows the general form of inverse transforms given in Section
2
: a sum over representations of
\mathbb{C}
. The choice of the value of
a\in\mathbb{R}
is in principle free within the strip of definition, however the choice
a=1
is selected as giving a basis of conformal primary wavefunctions that are normaliseable with respect to the Klein Gordon product
[
21
]
.

If we denote the chiral Mellin transform by
\mathcal{C}
then we can check directly that
\mathcal{C}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{C}=\text{Id}
,

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}^{-1}\{|\lam,\lamt;h,\bar{h}\rangle\}&:=\sum_{h-\bar{h}\in\frac{\mathbb{Z}}{2}}\int_{a-i\infty}^{a+i\infty}\frac{d\Delta}{2\pi i}|\lam,\lamt;h,\bar{h}\rangle\\
=&\sum_{h-\bar{h}\in\frac{\mathbb{Z}}{2}}\int_{a-i\infty}^{a+i\infty}\frac{d\Delta}{2\pi i}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}\,\left(e^{i\theta}\right)^{2(h-\bar{h})}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!d\omega\,\omega^{\Delta-1}|\sqrt{\omega}e^{i\theta}\lam,\sqrt{\omega}e^{-i\theta}\lamt\rangle\\
=&\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\theta\delta(\theta)\int_{0}^{\infty}\!d\omega\delta(\omega-1)|\sqrt{\omega}\lam,\sqrt{\omega}\lamt\rangle=|\lam,\lamt\rangle.\end{split}

(G.4)

Here we have used that

\displaystyle\int_{a-i\infty}^{a+i\infty}\frac{d\Delta}{2\pi i}\,\omega^{\Delta-1}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d\beta}{2\pi}\,e^{(a-1+i\beta)\text{ln}(\omega)}=e^{(a-1)\text{ln}(\omega)}\delta(\text{ln}(\omega))=\delta(\text{ln}(\omega))

(G.5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\partial_{\omega}\text{ln}(\omega)|^{-1}_{\omega=1}\delta(\omega-1)=\delta(\omega-1)\,,

(G.6)

and

\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{h-\bar{h}\in\frac{\mathbb{Z}}{2}}\left(e^{i\theta}\right)^{2(h-\bar{h})}=\delta(\theta).

(G.7)

We can also check
\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{C}^{-1}=\text{Id}
:

\begin{split}&\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{C}^{-1}\{|\lam,\lamt;h,\bar{h}\rangle\}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\!\!\int_{\CC}d\bar{u}\wedge duu^{2k-1}\bar{u}^{2\bar{k}-1}\!\!\!\!\sum_{h-\bar{h}\in\frac{\mathbb{Z}}{2}}\int_{a-i\infty}^{a+i\infty}\frac{d\Delta}{2\pi i}|u\lam,\bar{u}\lamt;h,\bar{h}\rangle\\
=&\sum_{h-\bar{h}\in\frac{\mathbb{Z}}{2}}\int_{a-i\infty}^{a+i\infty}\frac{d\Delta}{2\pi i}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\!\!\int_{\CC}d\bar{u}\wedge duu^{2k-2h-1}\bar{u}^{2\bar{k}-2\bar{h}-1}\,|\lam,\lamt;h,\bar{h}\rangle\\
=&\sum_{h-\bar{h}\in\frac{\mathbb{Z}}{2}}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{d\beta}{2\pi}\frac{1}{2\pi}\!\!\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\theta\,e^{2i\theta(h-\bar{h}-(k-\bar{k}))}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dxe^{x(a^{\prime}-a+i(\beta^{\prime}-\beta))}\,|\lam,\lamt;h,\bar{h}\rangle\\
=&\sum_{h-\bar{h}\in\frac{\mathbb{Z}}{2}}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{d\beta}{2\pi}\delta_{h-\bar{h},k-\bar{k}}2\pi\delta(\beta^{\prime}-\beta)\,|\lam,\lamt;h,\bar{h}\rangle=|\lam,\lamt;k,\bar{k}\rangle,\end{split}

(G.8)

where we have used a change of variable
u=e^{\frac{x}{2}}e^{i\theta}
. For the case in hand we have that both
h,\bar{h}
and
k,\bar{k}
are on the principal continuous series:
a^{\prime}=a(=1)
, which guarantees that
\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{C}^{-1}
is the identity. This condition can be relaxed if we admit generalised delta functions
[
67
]
.

Appendix H
Spinor Conventions

Here we state our conventions for spinor helicity, largely drawn from
[
78
]
.

The Pauli matrices are given by

\sigma^{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad\sigma^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-i\\
i&0\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad\sigma^{3}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad\underline{\sigma}=(\sigma^{1},\sigma^{2},\sigma^{3})\,,

(H.1)

and we then define

\begin{split}&(\sigma^{\mu})_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=(\mathbbm{1},\underline{\sigma}),\quad(\bar{\sigma}^{\mu})^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}=\ep^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\ep^{\alpha\beta}(\sigma^{\mu})_{\beta\dot{\beta}}=(\mathbbm{1},-\underline{\sigma}),\\
&(\sigma_{\mu})_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=(\mathbbm{1},-\underline{\sigma}),\quad(\bar{\sigma}_{\mu})^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}=\ep^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\ep^{\alpha\beta}(\sigma_{\mu})_{\beta\dot{\beta}}=(\mathbbm{1},\underline{\sigma})\,.\end{split}

(H.2)

For any given 4-vector
p^{\mu}
we define

p_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=(p_{\mu}\sigma^{\mu})_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\,

(H.3)

If
p^{2}=0
we can decompose this matrix into spinors

p_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=\lam_{\alpha}\lamt_{\dot{\alpha}}.

(H.4)

The two-dimensional Levi-Civita symbol, used to raise and lower two-spinor indices, is defined by

\ep^{\alpha\beta}=\ep^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\ep_{\alpha\beta}=\ep_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}.

(H.5)

We raise and lower indices as follows

\begin{split}\lam^{\alpha}=\ep^{\alpha\beta}\lam_{\beta}\quad\lamt^{\dot{\alpha}}=\ep^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\lamt_{\dot{\beta}}\,.\end{split}

(H.6)

Products of two different spinors
i
and
j
are given by

\langle\lam_{i}\lam_{j}\rangle=\lam_{i}^{\alpha}\lam_{j\alpha},\quad[\lamt_{i}\lamt_{j}]=\lamt_{i\dot{\alpha}}\lamt_{j}^{\dot{\alpha}}\,,

(H.7)

and additionally if
p^{\mu},q^{\mu}
are massless we may write

(p+q)^{2}=2p\cdot q=\langle pq\rangle[qp]\,.

(H.8)

We also define the spinor derivatives

\partial_{\alpha}\coloneqq\frac{\partial}{\partial\lam^{\alpha}}\,,\quad\tilde{\partial}_{\dot{\alpha}}\coloneqq\frac{\partial}{\partial\lamt^{\dot{\alpha}}}\,,

(H.9)

whose indices must be raised and lowered in the opposite way

\begin{split}&\partial^{\alpha}\coloneqq\frac{\partial}{\partial\lam^{\alpha}}=\ep^{\beta\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\\
&\tilde{\partial}^{\alpha}\coloneqq\frac{\partial}{\partial\lamt^{\dot{\alpha}}}=\ep^{\dot{\beta}\dot{\alpha}}\tilde{\partial}_{\dot{\beta}}\,.\end{split}

(H.10)
